# As Through a Veil — takeaways

*Source: Annemarie Schimmel, *As Through a Veil: Mystical Poetry in Islam* (New York: Columbia
University Press, 1982; Lectures on the History of Religions, New Series 12). 382 pages, transcribed
`text_layer` on 2026-09-14; chunked 2026-09-22; provenance in `out/ocr/schimmel_as_through_a_veil/`.
Page references are PDF page indices; folio = printed page number where known. Reviewed: no.*

## What this book is

Five public lectures on mystical poetry in Islam, ranging from the Arabic beginnings through Persian
and Turkish to the vernaculars of the Subcontinent, and closing on poetry in praise of the Prophet.
Schimmel writes as an interpreter rather than an editor: the book argues for how this poetry should
be read — ẓāhir and bāṭin, intended double meaning, form as the carrier of religious content — and it
is the South Asian chapters, on Sindhi, Panjabi, Siraiki and Urdu material, that make it useful to
this archive. Her evidential base for those chapters is substantially her own prior work.

**Reliability.** The `text_layer` is clean for ordinary English prose but has **destroyed the entire
transliteration system**: no macrons, no dots-below and no ʿayn or hamza survive anywhere in 764,603
characters, and the substitutions are not reversible token by token (ʿayn prints as `c`, `e` or `<`;
ī as `f` or `r`; final ā as `ii`; ḥ as `IJ`). Every affected word carries a `[text-layer?]` flag
below — 1,010 of them. Digits in the **body text survive**, so dates from the body are quotable;
digits in the **index and endnote locators do not**, and no locator from either may ever be used as a
page reference. The printed folio is recoverable throughout: it sits inside the running header, and
is PDF − 10 up to PDF 128 and PDF − 18 from PDF 137, the step being an eight-page unpaginated plate
insert at PDF 129–136 that the transcription wrongly records as blank pages.

## Shrines in the archive this book informs

*Twenty-one archive rows carry site- or person-specific material, ordered below by volume. A further
seventy-two rows are attached only at place level under ruling (a) and are listed in the next
section. Read the `Evidence base` line before using any bullet: much of what follows is
author-only or literary reference, which the 18 September ruling admits but which must never be read
as the book describing a shrine.*

### bhit-bhit-shah — Bhit (Bhit Shah)

The book's most heavily cited Pakistani figure, appearing in ten of the sixteen chunks. He is named
throughout with a destroyed ʿayn and destroyed diacritics: `Shah c Abdul Latif`, `Shah ' Abdul Latif`,
`Shah < Abdul Latif` [all text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf], as well as "Shah Latif", "Shah ʿAbdul Latif
of Bhit", "Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit", "Abdul Latif", "the Sindhi mystic Shah ʿAbdul Latif", and — inside
Ernest Trumpp's title — "Sayyid Abdul-Latif" (pp. 11, 157–158, 166–187, 223–225, 241, 276–292, 307–311,
327–340, 349–363).

- **Saint**
  - Placed only by century and region: "Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Latīf] in
    eighteenth-century 5ind [text-layer?: Sind] teaches his disciples" (p. 158, folio 140); the same
    eighteenth-century placing is repeated at p. 171, folio 153 and p. 187, folio 169. Birth year,
    village and shrine are **(not stated)** in the body text.
  - Called "the Sindhi mystic Shah c Abdul Latif" [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf]; "the only thing
    attached to him geographically is the adjective 'Sindhi'" (p. 241, folio 223).
  - Kinship: he is the great-grandson of Shah ʿAbdul Karim of Bulrri [text-layer?: Bulri] — the mystical
    use of Sindhi folk tales "reach full bloom in the Risiilo [text-layer?: Risālō] of his
    great-grandson, Shah c Abdul Latif of Bhit in the eighteenth century" (p. 171, folio 153).
  - **Death year — the book's two registers disagree in kind, keep both.** The Index of Proper Names
    prints the headword `c Abdul Latif, Shah (d . 1752)` [text-layer?: `ʿAbdul Laṭīf, Shāh`], with the
    work `Risal6` [text-layer?: `Risālo`] as a sub-entry under the name (p. 349, folio not stated).
    The body text gives **no death year anywhere**: Pass-1 workers record "no death year is stated"
    (p. 171, folio 153) and "no shrine, tomb, ʿurs, custodian or death date for him appears anywhere
    in this chunk: **(not stated)**" (pp. 223–225, 241, folios 205–207, 223). The chunk-001 material
    likewise gives "no dates for the poet, and no silsila" (p. 11, folio not stated).
  - No silsila is given anywhere (p. 11, folio not stated; p. 241, folio 223).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Not stated.** The book never describes a tomb, shrine, ʿurs, custodian or endowment for him
    (explicitly noted at pp. 223–225, 241, folios 205–207, 223; also pp. 11, 276–280, folios not
    stated / 258–262).
  - "Bhit" — this row's own toponym — occurs in the book only inside his epithet, "Shah c Abdul Latif
    of Bhit" (p. 171, folio 153), and inside the titles of two studies: "Henry T. Sorley, Shah Abdul
    Latif of Bhit, His Poetry, Life and Times" (p. 283, folio 265) and "Sorley, Herbert T. Shah Abdul
    Latif of Bhit. London: Oxford University Press, t940 [text-layer?: 1940]; repr. 1966." (p. 340,
    folio 322). A Pass-1 worker marks the second: **"'Bhit' is the toponym of this row"**.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - He stands in the book's list of saints attached to the alif legend: "The story that this or that
    saint was acquainted only with the letter alif, the first letter of the Arabic alphabet and cipher
    for Allah, is commonplace in the legends of Muslim saints from Turkey to India (Yunus Emre, Shah c
    Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Latīf], Bullhe Shah, Bedil Rohriwaro, etc.)" (p. 157,
    folio 139). The teaching quoted from him is the alif verse itself: "Those who remember the line
    which begins with alif: / 'There is no goal but Him in both worlds,' [that is what] they think"
    (p. 158, folio 140).
- **Significance**
  - *European reception.* "And some forty years later (1867) Ernest Trumpp made some poisonous remarks
    about one of the masterpieces of classical Sindhi poetry, Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shah
    ʿAbdul Laṭīf] 's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], which he edited with great pains although he
    thoroughly disliked anything that smacked of mysticism." (p. 11, folio not stated; the date `1867`
    is quoted as printed — four-digit years survive intact in this book). "Yet even he could not help
    admitting that some of Shah c Abdul Latif's verses were worth reading." (p. 11, folio not stated),
    which is Schimmel's judgement on Trumpp, not on the poet. She returns to Trumpp at p. 283,
    folio 265: "The overcritical approach of the German missionary-philologist to Sindhi mystical
    poetry is due partly to his 'protestant,' violently antimystical attitude, partly also to the fact
    that the texts which he collected were hopelessly confused."
  - *Folk-tale method.* He is the source of Sindhi folk-poetry motifs "beginning at least from Shah c
    Abdul Latif's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō]" (p. 166, folio 148). "Since the original tales were
    known to everyone, he usually begins his poetical renderings with the most dramatic moment in order
    to describe, with remarkable psychological insight, the moods of the soul on its way to the
    beloved." (p. 171, folio 153)
  - *The woman-souls.* **Sohni**, who "swims every night through the Indus to meet her beloved on an
    island, until the jar which she uses as a kind of life-vest is shattered and she is drowned"
    (p. 171, folio 153); **Sassui**, who follows "her beloved Punhun through burning deserts and over
    terrifying hills", loses him during the "sleep of heedlessness" and "is completely transformed into
    Love" (pp. 171–172, folios 153–154); **Marui**, "the ideal soul", "Captured from her native village
    by the powerful c omar [text-layer?: ʿUmar] of Omar-kot" (p. 172, folio 154); and **Nuri**, "a
    historical figure, the consort of the Sindhi ruler Jam Tamachi in the fifteenth century", "though a
    simple fishermaid" (p. 172, folio 154).
  - *Gendered soul.* "Shah c Abdul Latif applies to one of his heroines the famous saying of the
    thirteenth century Chishti saint, Jamaluddin Hanswi: talib al-maula mudhakkar . . . , 'He who seeks
    the Lord is male . . . .'" (p. 173, folio 155). His heroines "who write with tears of blood (Ma-rui
    II, v. 4) would like to decorate with golden threads the wings of the messenger crow that would
    bring news from the beloved (Sur Purab I, v. 8)" (p. 284, folio 266). He "expresses Ruzbihan-i
    Baqli's ideal: the lover becomes completely transformed into Love" (p. 283, folio 265), with the
    verses "0 voice in the desert, as though it were a wild goose" and "It is the song of Love-only
    common people thought it / To be a woman's voice" (p. 284, folio 266).
  - *Music.* "The musical imagery in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō] culminates in
    King Dhyaj's [text-layer?: name uncertain] offering his head to the minstrel whose ravishing
    melodies had kindled his love and made him forsake honor and life." (p. 169, folio 151). "The
    admittedly very cruel Hindu story of Sorathi and King Dhyaj, located originally in Svar-ashtra
    [text-layer?: Saurashtra — not certain, see Doubt], becomes for Shah Latif an allegory of the
    absolute power of the mystical guide for whose sweet songs the initiate gladly sacrifices
    everything, including his life." (p. 283, folio 265). "Shah c Abdul Latif praises also the
    instruments of the Yogis for their bewitching power (Sur Ramakali)." (p. 283, folio 265). `Sorathi`
    stands as its own headword in the Index of Proper Names (p. 362, folio 344).
  - *Tree image.* Named among "popular Sufi poets-Shah Latif in Sind as much as the Anatolian minstrel
    Yunus Emre-[who] see the unbeliever or the ungrateful person as a dried-up tree which cannot be
    moved by the breeze of Divine kindness and should be cut off and burned (a subtle allusion to Sura
    111)" (p. 160, folio 142).
  - *Unity of being.* Set beside Yunus Emre at a chapter's close: "Shah c Abdul Latif in
    eighteenth-century Sind knows that: hika qa:iaru dara lak'ha [text-layer?: transliteration damaged]
    / One castle, a hundred thousand doors, / Windows without number- / Wherever I look, there the
    Beloved confronts me." (p. 187, folio 169)
  - *Prophetic devotion.* "The great mystical poets of Sind have devoted wonderful songs to the Prophet,
    who appears as the dawn between the night of created human life and the Divine day" — the named
    example that follows is Shah ʿAbdul Latif (p. 223, folio 205). "The lyrical parts at the end of many
    chapters in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālō] speak of the poet's hope for
    Muhammad's intercession" (p. 223, folio 205). "The most beautiful example among them is Sur Siirang
    [text-layer?: Sur Sārang], in which the poet dramatically describes the longing of all creatures for
    rain" (p. 223, folio 205), the sentence completing at p. 224, folio 206: "rain brings fertility to
    the parched soil, milk to the cows, bread to the poor". "Out of this description Shah Latif turns to
    a short, touching song in which the Prophet, once more the cloud of mercy, is praised because he
    will protect his community at Doomsday" (p. 224, folio 206). "And poets who followed Shah c Abdul
    Latif's example saw this blissful rain cloud extending not only from Istanbul to Jaisalmer, from
    Delhi to Omarkot but from Kairouan to Rohri and from England to China" (p. 224, folio 206).
  - *Reception by Hindu commentators.* Schimmel states that "the Hindu commentators on Shah c Abdul
    Latif's poetry failed to understand the importance of the Prophet (the same i s true for Panjabi
    poetry)" (p. 308, folio 290). She names "Lilaram Watanmal, the author o f the first major biography
    of Shah ' Abdul Latif (Hyderabad/Sind, 1889)", quotes him at length, and closes: "These patronizing
    remarks (pp. 37-38) are certainly out of place." (p. 308, folio 290). Watanmal is quoted as writing
    that "some of the verses in praise of the prophet, somewhat vulgar in language, are not our poet's .
    . . . But it cannot be doubted that there are several genuine verses in which our poet has expressed
    his full belief in the prophet." (p. 308, folio 290), and further: "It may be that Shah Latif wanted
    to lead the minds of his followers slowly and gradually into higher Sufism by allowing them to
    believe first in their prophet, and then by degrees to ascend higher and higher." (p. 308,
    folio 290).
  - *Formal firsts.* "the first known example in Sin-dhi occurs in Shah ' Abdul Latif, Risalo, Sur
    Ramakali" — said of the golden-alphabet form, the `Tih ak'haryun` edited by "Dr. N. A. Baloch"
    (p. 281, folio 263). "the first known example is again in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risalo, Sur Ramakali"
    — said of poems enumerating the days of a week or of a month (p. 282, folio 264).
  - *Marthiya.* "Shah c Abdul Latif, in Sur Kedar6 [text-layer?: Sur Kedāro] VI, vv. 10 and 11, takes up
    the motif of nuptials for the death of the martyrs", with the quoted line "The houris wind rose
    chains; flower decorations for the martyrs!" (p. 285, folio 267).
  - *Symbolic geography.* "With Shah c Abdul Latif, pfirab [text-layer?: pūrab] always denotes the
    'mystical' East, the home of the soul" (p. 288, folio 270).
  - *Transmission from Rumi.* Note 418 to chapter 3: "M I 1704; the verse was translated verbatim into
    Sindhi poetry by Shah ' Abdul Latif, Risa/6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Sassui Abri I, 8. See also
    D 445/4691" (p. 276, folio 258).
  - *Satan as true monotheist.* "the Sindhi mystic Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf]
    calls Satan 'Lover': cashiq c Azazil [text-layer?: ʿāshiq ʿAzāzīl], Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālo],
    Sur Yaman Kalyan V 24" (p. 241, folio 223). He is named third in a series of three holders of that
    view, after "Ahmad Ghaz­zali's remark, 'Who does not learn tauhid from Satan, is an infidel'" and
    "similar remarks … made by the Persian poet Sarmad at the Moghul Court"; the note frames all three
    against "Ritter, Meer der Seele" for the claim that in "Hallaj's and his followers' view Satan
    'becomes more monotheist than God Himself.'" (p. 241, folio 223).
  - *Other citations of the Risalo by sur and verse* (the numerals are internal locators of the cited
    work, not pages of this book): `Ristil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Yaman Kalyan V, verse 2i
    [text-layer?: verse 21]` (p. 279, folio 261); `Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Dahar` and
    `Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Ripa II, v. 8.9` (p. 280, folio 262); "Shah ' Abdul Latif,
    Risalo, Sur Barvo Sindhi II, v. 1 i . [text-layer?: v. 11]" and "Shah c Abdul Latif, Risdl6
    [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Kapa'iti I, v . 4 ." (p. 281, folio 263); "Risa/a [text-layer?: Risālo],
    Sur Ripa II, vv . 13-15" and "Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Sohni I, vv 17, 18" (p. 284,
    folio 266); "Ristil6 [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Kalyan, I v. 20" (p. 292, folio 274); "Thus `S/zii/z
    j6 Risiil6` [text-layer?: Shāh jo Risālo], Sur Dahar II, 1-3", cited in note 169 for three
    invocations of the Prophet as lord of Medina (p. 307, folio 289). Note 20 to chapter 1 cites "a
    Sindhi poet [who] admonishes his listeners to tum [text-layer?: turn] 'to the end of The Afternoon,
    al-'aiir' [text-layer?: al-ʿaṣr]", glossed as the last word of "the Surat al-'a$r (Sura 103), which
    is $abr, 'patience.'", the reference printed "(Shah c Abdul Latif, Shiih jo Risiilo [text-layer?:
    Shāh jo Risālō], Sur Kalyan III 8)" (p. 237, folio 219).
  - *Cross-reference in the book's own apparatus.* Note 169 continues "For details see Schimmel, 'The
    Veneration of the Prophet Muhammad, in Sin­dhi,' p. 134 ff." (p. 308, folio 290). The title also
    stands as its own index headword with a cross-reference: `Risalo, see ' Abdul Latif` (p. 363,
    folio 345).
  - *Editions and studies the book prints.* Bibliography, as printed: "c Abdul Latif Bhita'i, Shah. Shah
    jo risiilo, ed. Kalyan B . Adwani. Bombay: Hindustan Kitabghar, 1958." (p. 311, folio not stated) —
    the editor is "Kalyan B . Adwani", the publisher "Hindustan Kitabghar" of Bombay, 1958 (p. 311,
    folio not stated). "Jotwani, Motilal. … Shah Abdul Latif, His Life and Work. University of Delhi,
    1975." (p. 327, folio 309). "Kazi, Elsa. Risalo of Abdul Latif. Selections. Hyderabad/Sind: Sindhi
    Adabi Board, 1965 ." (p. 327, folio 309). "Rashdi, Sayyid Hussamuddin." … "'Shah kafia jo miijid na
    ahe.' Mehran jufi maujufi [text-layer?: Mehrān jā maujā]. Karachi: Sindhi Adabi Board, 1956. ('Shah
    c Abdul Latif did not invent the kaff [text-layer?: kāfī],' see pp. 215-24 .)" — Schimmel supplies
    the English gloss of the Sindhi title herself (p. 334, folio 316). "Watanmal, Lilaram. The Life of
    Shah Abdullatif. Hyderabad/Sind, 1889." (p. 343, folio 325), that author himself indexed as
    "Watanmal, Lilaram (d. after 1900)" (p. 364, folio 346). Three studies are listed together at
    p. 283, folio 265: "Henry T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit, His Poetry, Life and Times; Motilal
    Jotwani, Shah Abdul Latif, His Life and Work; Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace, Part II."
- **Doubt:** the chunk-001 material is an **author-only mention** — he is "named twice on one page, only
  as the author of a Sindhi classic, in the course of Schimmel's account of European scholarship on
  mystical poetry", with no epithet (`Bhittai` does not appear), no shrine, no tomb, no site, no dates
  and no silsila; the `Risalo` is called "one of the masterpieces of classical Sindhi poetry" and nothing
  more (p. 11, folio not stated). The Pass-1 worker raised the identification question explicitly. The
  mapping rests on the 18 September 2026 person rule.
- **Doubt:** the title of his work prints **four different ways in four citations in chunk 011 alone**
  (`Risa/6`, `Ristil6`, `Risii/6` at pp. 276–280, folios 258–262) and several more ways elsewhere
  (`Risalo`, `Risdl6`, `Risa/a`, `Shah jo risiilo`, `S/zii/z j6 Risiil6`, `Shiih jo Risiilo`,
  pp. 281–311, folios 263–289). Pass-1 workers flagged this and did **not** unify it (BRIEF §7).
- **Doubt:** "Svar-ashtra [text-layer?: Saurashtra]" is explicitly **not certain** (p. 283, folio 265).
- **Doubt:** the chunk-002 Pass-1 bullet heads him as "the book's 'Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shah
  ʿAbdul Latif] in Sind'" but carries **no facts and no page reference** — the bullet is empty as
  transmitted. A human should check whether chunk 002 in fact held material that was lost.
### mazar-e-iqbal — Tomb of Allama Iqbal (Mazar-e-Iqbal)

- **Kind of evidence — read this first.** The book nowhere describes a tomb, a shrine or a visit. Iqbal
  enters the archive row under the 18 September ruling only as (i) a modern poet and thinker whose
  doctrine and verse Schimmel discusses, (ii) an author cited in notes, index and bibliography, and
  (iii) a run of bibliography entries whose sole Lahore content is a **publisher's address**. The three
  kinds are kept apart below.

**Saint (the person, as the book presents him)**
- Named in full as `Muhammad Iqbal (d. 1938)` and introduced as "a modern Muslim poet who has most
  eloquently praised Muhammad although he has never written a formal nac t" [text-layer?: naʿt] "in
  traditional style" (p. 226, folio 208). The Index of Proper Names repeats the date in its headword
  `Iqbal, Mul;lammad (d . 1938)` [text-layer?: `Iqbāl, Muḥammad`] (p. 356, folio 338). The bibliography
  heads him `Iqbal, Sir Muhammad` (p. 325, folio 307).
- Repeatedly typed as a modern: "a modernist thinker like Muhammad Iqbal" (p. 20, folio 10); "a modem
  [text-layer?: modern] thinker like Iqbal" (p. 192, folio 174); "a modern critic like Iqbal"
  (p. 178, folio 160).
- One biographical incident is given: "'The Arabian friend,' as he calls Muhammad (like many Indian
  Muslim poets before him), is praised in a poem which Iqbal wrote in 1936 in the hope of being cured
  from an ailment, as he tells in a letter. He had been advised to write the poem by a dream apparition
  of Sir Sayyid Ahmad Khan: the healing quality of the Burda is remembered" (p. 226, folio 208).
- The Hijaz is "the country which he never visited" (p. 227, folio 209).

**Shrine / Darbar**
- **(not stated) throughout.** No tomb, burial place, city, death place or silsila is given anywhere the
  chunk notes reach: p. 20, folio 10; p. 77, folio 67; p. 84, folio 74; pp. 192–216 (p. 192, folio 174);
  pp. 247–254 ("named four times, always for his poetry, never for his tomb"); p. 274, folio 256. The
  only Lahore tie in the running text is the imprint of a cited edition — "his epic ]avidniima (Lahore,
  1932)" (note printed on p. 233, folio not stated).

**Significance (what Schimmel uses him for)**
- The mystical/prophetical distinction: against `c Abdul Quddus Gangohi` [text-layer?: ʿAbdul Quddūs
  Gangohī]'s saying that the true mystic "would never like to return", "In this remark about Muhammad's
  ascension and his return in order to work in the world, a modernist thinker like Muhammad Iqbal found
  the difference between 'mystical' and 'prophetical' religion expressed in a nutshell" (p. 20, folio 10);
  Schimmel adds that "the Muslim mystics have been long aware of this dichotomy which was defined, in
  Western scholarship, by Nathan Soderblom" [text-layer?: Söderblom] "and Friedrich Heiler" (p. 20,
  folio 10).
- Avicenna, condemned by Sana'i as "the paragon of love-less intellectualism", "is regarded this way in
  Persian verse to our day (e.g., in Iqbal's work)" (p. 77, folio 67).
- Sultan Mahmud transformed into "the ideal lover", the king who "finally became 'the slave of his
  slave'", is "a topic used still in Iqbal' s poetry" (p. 84, folio 74).
- Hallaj's "name looms large in high Persian and Urdu poetry from c Attar to Ghalib and Iqbal" (p. 176,
  folio 158).
- Against Sufi poetry: "the sharp reaction of a modern critic like Iqbal to this kind of Sufi poetry, with
  its sweeping generalizations which seemed to leave no room for the truly Islamic values, can be
  understood and appreciated" — "this kind" being the wahdat al-wujud imagery of indifference toward
  ritual (p. 178, folio 160).
- Devotion to the Prophet's name, within the argument that "the very name of the Prophet was often praised
  for its baraka": he "claims in his Asriir-i khiidi [text-layer?: Asrār-i khudī] (written in 1915): / All
  our glory is from the name of Muhammad," and "he had sung a few years earlier in Jawiib-i shikwii
  [text-layer?: Jawāb-i shikwā]: / Light the world, too long in darkness, / With Muhammad's radiant name."
  Iqbal stands there beside "a medieval Turkish folk poet" as a modern instance of the same devotion
  (all p. 192, folio 174). The parenthetical year is the book's own.
- Prophetology, the fullest treatment: he "describes his own role as that of the caravan bell that helps
  guide the travelers to the central sanctuary in Mecca (as the title of his Urdu Diwan, Biing-i darii"
  [text-layer?: Bāng-i darā] ", indicates)"; "even his early Urdu poems, such as /awiib-i shikwiih"
  [text-layer?: Jawāb-i shikwa], "contain verses in praise of the Prophet" (both p. 226, folio 208); "in
  his first revolutionary Persian mathnawi Asriir-i khildi (1915)" [text-layer?: Asrār-i khudī] "he has
  devoted some of his finest lines to Muhammad"; "the Rumilz-i bekhildi" [text-layer?: Rumūz-i bekhudī],
  "the poetical collection which translates Iqbal's sociopolitical ideals into Persian verse", where the
  Prophet appears "as the lamp in the darkness of existence"; "his last verses, which are devoted to the
  Hijaz … For us Mustafa is enough"; and "he had gone even further in the /iividniima" [text-layer?:
  Jāvīdnāma], "where the daring line appears: You can deny God, but you cannot deny the Prophet!" (all
  p. 227, folio 209).
- "Iqbal sees Muhammad as the political leader who is able to guide people toward their destiny even in
  this world", reflected "in the mirror of the complaint of Abu Jahl, one of his grimmest enemies, who
  defends earthrootedness, narrow patriotism and distinction of races and castes"; and "Iq.bal defines
  also his more-than-human state with an important passage in the ]avidniima" [text-layer?: Jāvīdnāma],
  "in a poem which he puts in the mouth of Hallaj, whose hymn in honor of the Prophet forms the actual
  beginning of mystical prophetology" — on the epithet "cabduhu" [text-layer?: ʿabduhu], "'His slave'"
  (all p. 228, folio 210).
- Summing up: "Iqbal's prophetology combines elements of both a modern and a traditional world view:
  Muhammad is both the political leader of his community and the scent of the rosa mystica" (p. 229,
  folio 211).
- On mystical poetry: "Hall's" [text-layer?: Hali's] "verdict was taken over and emphasized by Iqbal, who
  claimed that mystical poetry after Hafiz might prove utterly dangerous for the morals of a people;
  however, he admitted in his epic ]avidniima (Lahore, 1932), lines 727-728: If the purpose of poetry is
  to form men, / Poetry is the heir of prophecy" (note printed on p. 233, folio not stated).
- Shabistari's *Gulshan-i rāz* "evoked an interesting modem" [text-layer?: modern] "response in the 1920s:
  Muhammad Iqbal, Gulshan-i riiz-i jadid" [text-layer?: Gulshan-i rāz-i jadīd], "which he appended to his
  Persian diwan Zabar-i <ajam" [text-layer?: Zabūr-i ʿajam]; the note refers the reader to "Alessandro
  Bausani, 'II gul§an-i raz-i gadid.'" (p. 247, folio 229). The decade "the 1920s" is quoted as printed.
- Philosophy against mystical experience: "Muhammad Iqbal, particularly in his Payam-i Mashriq", with "the
  poem which contrasts Avicenna and Rumi" and "the bookworm that dwells in Ibn Sina's pages and therefore
  lacks real experience of life and love" (p. 250, folio 232).
- Farhad, the luckless lover, "has become in modem" [text-layer?: modern] "progressive writings from Iqbal
  to Nazim Hikmet the representative of the oppressed working class" (p. 253, folio 235).
- The tulip: "In Iqbal's work, Iii/a" [text-layer?: lāla] "represents the independent, vigorous plant that
  appears in the middle of the desert, resembling in its shape and color the flames which Moses saw on
  Mt. Sinai; it is seen as a positive contrast to the traditional rose which lives in well-trimmed
  gardens, and thus becomes a symbol of man's striving against heavy odds to realize himself"
  (pp. 253–254, folios 235–236).
- Receiver of a Rumi motif: Schimmel quotes `D 18212030-32` on Intellect and Love ("When Intellect looks
  still for a camel to perform the pilgrimage, / Love has already reached the hill Safa [= Purity}",
  p. 273, folio 255, running onto p. 274) and calls these "ideas which Iqbal has taken up in his lyrical
  poetiy [text-layer?: poetry], particularly in the Payam-i Mashriq" (p. 274, folio 256).

**Cited as author only — notes, index and non-Lahore bibliography (the book is citing his work, not

### data-darbar — Data Darbar

- **Kind of evidence — read this first.** The book never describes Data Darbar, an urs, a custodianship or
  any practice at the site. Hujwiri appears almost entirely as **the author of the *Kashf al-maḥjūb***,
  quoted or cited (usually in the compound form `Hujwiri/Nicholson`), plus one biographical half-sentence
  in the running text and one index headword. The id is taken under the 18 September ruling.

**Saint**
- He is the author of the `Kash/ al-mafJjub` [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb], which the book calls "the
  first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- The one thing the running text says of the man: he is the one "who spent his last years in Lahore"
  (p. 61, folio 51).
- Name forms as printed: "c Ali ibn c Othman al-Hujwiri" [text-layer?: ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān al-Hujwīrī] in the
  notes (p. 236, folio 218); "Hujwiri, c Ali ibn c Uthman al-Jullabi al-" [text-layer?: ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān
  al-Jullābī al-Hujwīrī] in the bibliography (p. 324, folio 306).
- He is placed in the book's chronology of Persian mystical literature, which "developed first in the
  Eastern Lands of the Caliphate, namely in Herat and northwest India, in the second half of the eleventh
  century" (p. 61, folio 51), and paired with a contemporary: "at approximately the same time another
  mystic, `c Abdullah-i Ansari` [text-layer?: ʿAbdullāh-i Anṣārī]" translated "Sulami's `Tabaqat a�-` /
  `�afiyya`" [text-layer?: Ṭabaqāt aṣ-Ṣūfiyya] and wrote "the first devotional booklet in Persian, the
  `Munii-` / `jiit`" [text-layer?: Munājāt] (p. 61, folio 51).
- **Epithet and death date — the two places differ, kept side by side.** In the running text at p. 61
  (folio 51) the book gives no death date, no order and no epithet: Pass 1 records explicitly that "Data
  Ganj Bakhsh" does not appear there (p. 61, folio 51). The Index of Proper Names, by contrast, heads him
  `Hujwiri, c Ali ibn <Othman Data Ganj Bakhsh (d. c. 1071) (Kash[ al-mabjiib)` [text-layer?: `Hujwīrī,
  ʿAlī ibn ʿOthmān Data Ganj Bakhsh`, work `Kashf al-maḥjūb`] (p. 355, folio 337) — so the epithet that
  gives the shrine its name, and a death date, occur once each, in the index only. The date is printed
  with the book's own hedge, `(d. c. 1071)` (p. 355, folio 337). The index attaches exactly one work to
  him, in the parenthesis after the name, and gives him no sub-entries (p. 355, folio 337).

**Shrine / Darbar**
- **(not stated) throughout.** No tomb, no shrine, no order and no silsila are given at p. 61 (folio 51);
  the bibliography entry gives "no place, tomb, date or silsila for him — only the authorship" (p. 324,
  folio 306; p. 325, folio 307); and Lahore is not named at all in the chunk containing the note on union
  (p. 276, folio 258).

**Significance — cited as author of the *Kashf al-maḥjūb***
- Transmitter of a saying of Sumnun al-Muhibb: "Sumnun al-Muhibb, quoted by c Ali ibn c Othman al-Hujwiri,
  Kashf al-mahjub, p. 137" (note 25 to chapter 1, printed on p. 236, folio 218).
- Cited for the doctrine of *tauhid*: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjub, p. 2cn" [text-layer?: page
  number not recoverable]; "see also Gramlich, 'Mystical Dimensions of tauhid'" (note 81 to chapter 1,
  printed on p. 240, folio 222).
- The reference for early Persian Sufi prose in the opening run of the Persian chapter's notes:
  "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjub" (p. 245, folio 227).
- For two contrasted contemplative positions: the *Kashf* "discusses the two approaches to the world: some
  mystics state, 'I never saw anything without seeing God therein,' which points to an advanced stage of
  contemplation because he sees the agent only, while the statement of others, 'I never see anything
  except God,' shows that he, fully enraptured, sees only the agent" (p. 248, folio 230).
- For a prohibition: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-ma'1jiib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] "says: 'Looking
  at youth and associating with them are forbidden practices and anyone who declares this to be allowable
  is an unbeliever!'" (p. 250, folio 232).
- For the law of love: "As Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjlib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] "says: 'It
  is a law that when the lover advances, the beloved retires. If the lover is satisfied with love alone,
  then the beloved draws near. In truth, the lover has honor only while he has no desire of union.'" —
  which "forms the topic of most Persian works on love" (p. 251, folio 233).
- Cited bare, without comment, for the imagery of wine or the tavern: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf
  al-ma}/jiib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] (p. 254, folio 236).
- Cited once in the note on union: `See Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf a/-mal1jiib [text-layer?: Kashf
  al-maḥjūb], p. 398` (p. 276, folio 258).

**Bibliography entry (no Lahore imprint — the edition cited is European)**
- One entry, its head on the last line of p. 324 (folio 306) and the rest on p. 325 (folio 307): "Kashf al
  ma/Jjub [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb], trans. by / Reynold Alleyne Nicholson. GMS XVII . London:
  Luzac-Leiden: Brill, 1911; 3d ed . 1959, and often." (p. 324, folio 306; p. 325, folio 307)
- **Doubt:** in the notes the evidence is a bare bibliographic citation, with no saint, shrine, city or
  date attached (p. 236, folio 218; p. 240, folio 222) — flagged by Pass 1 as thin. The only Lahore
  sentence in the whole book's Hujwiri material is "who spent his last years in Lahore" (p. 61, folio 51),
  and the only appearance of "Data Ganj Bakhsh", the name the archive row carries, is the index headword
  (p. 355, folio 337). A human should decide whether an entry may rest on those two lines.
### shrine-of-sachal-sarmast — Shrine of Sachal Sarmast

Named "Sachal Sarmast", and printed damaged as "Sacha! Sarmast" and "Sacha! Sannast" [both text-layer?:
Sachal Sarmast], also simply "Sachal" (pp. 158, 169–177, 279–285, 335, 361).

- **Saint**
  - **Dating — two registers, keep both side by side.** In the body he is dated only *relatively*, by
    the word "compatriot", the antecedent being "Qadi Qadan (d. 1551) in Sind": "And 250 years later his
    compatriot Sachal Sarmast writes that:" (p. 158, folio 140); a Pass-1 worker records that "his own
    dates and shrine are **(not stated)**" there. The Index of Proper Names prints the headword `Sacha!
    Sarmast (d. 1826)` [text-layer?: `Sachal Sarmast`] (p. 361, folio 343).
  - Called "the Sindhi Sachal Sarmast" (pp. 176–177, folios 158–159). No place, no shrine, no tomb, no
    ʿurs, no custodian and no silsila are given anywhere (explicitly noted at p. 158, folio 140 and
    p. 279, folio 261).
- **Significance**
  - *Ecstatic tendency.* "the Panjabi Bullhe Shah and the Sindhi Sachal Sarmast, with their highly
    ecstatic songs, are the most typical exponents of this tendency" — the tendency of the poets to
    "identify themselves with everything created" (pp. 176–177, folios 158–159).
  - *Against the madrasa handbooks.* "Those who have tasted a sip from the syrup of the ocean of love /
    Cast Kanz Quduri Kiifiya [text-layer?: Kāfiya] in the waves, / They say goodbye to syntax, and don't
    look any more at grammar!" (p. 158, folio 140) — the three named works being the madrasa handbooks
    the book has just listed.
  - *Comparison with Yunus Emre.* "one can almost translate a Sindhi poem by Sachal Sarmast into Turkish
    and take it for an original verse by Yunus Ernre [text-layer?: Emre]" (p. 169, folio 151).
  - *Martyrdom poem.* "A telling example is Sacha! [text-layer?: Sachal] Sarmast's Sindhi poem with the
    beginning:" followed by the quoted poem — "Welcome, welcome You are-- / to which place will You
    bring me? / You will again cut off a head! / Giving a kick to Sarmad You have killed him, / You have
    brought Mansur on the gallows, cut off Shaikh c Attar's head, / Now You are asking the way here!"
    (p. 174, folio 156, running into p. 175, folio 157).
  - *Indian martyrs.* "Sachal adds to these generally known Sufis the names of martyred Indian religious
    heroes, such as Sarmad, Dara Shikoh's friend" (p. 175, folio 157), and "Sachal also speaks of Shah c
    1nayat [text-layer?: ʿInayat] of Jhok" (p. 175, folio 157).
  - *Spiritual correspondence.* "One of the loveliest expressions of such a 'spiritual correspondence'
    is found in Sachal's 'Sur Malkos,' IV, vv. 1-10, in Sacha! Sarmast, Risa/6 Sindhi; see also his
    'Marui,' III, vv. 2-4" (p. 284, folio 266).
  - *Cited works* (internal locators of the cited work, not pages of this book): `Sacha! Sarmast,
    Wa/jdatntima [text-layer?: Waḥdatnāma], in Ristilo Sindhi [text-layer?: Risālo Sindhi], p . 36`
    (p. 279, folio 261), supporting the chapter-4 discussion of vernacular mystical poetry; "Sacha!
    Sannast [text-layer?: Sachal Sarmast], 'WalJdat' [text-layer?: Waḥdat], Risa/a Sindhi [text-layer?:
    Risālo Sindhī]" (p. 284, folio 266); and "Sachal, Risdlo Sindhr [text-layer?: Risālo Sindhī]",
    alongside "Christopher Shackle, Styles and Themes in the Siraiki Mystical Poetry of Sind" (p. 285,
    folio 267).
  - *Editions the book prints,* both Karachi: "Risiil6 Sindhi [text-layer?: Risālō Sindhī], ed. c Othman
    c Ali Arn;;arL [text-layer?: ʿUthmān ʿAlī Anṣārī]. Karachi: Sindhi Adabi Board, 1958." (p. 335,
    folio 317); "Siraiki kaliim [text-layer?: Sirāikī kalām], ed . Maulwi I:Iakim Mul:tammad Sadiq
    RanipiirL [text-layer?: Maulwī Ḥakīm Muḥammad Sādiq Ranīpūrī]. Karachi: Sindhi Adabi Board, 1959."
    (p. 335, folio 317).
- **Doubt:** the relative dating "250 years later" than "Qadi Qadan (d. 1551)" (p. 158, folio 140) sits
  awkwardly beside the index's "(d. 1826)" (p. 361, folio 343); the two are the book's own, are not
  reconciled here, and a human should decide which (if either) may be cited.
### mazar-of-bulleh-shah — Mazar of Bulleh Shah

- **Evidence base:** six chunks (006, 007, 011, 012, 014, 015), all literary — body text naming him as a
  poet, three endnote citations, one bibliography entry and one index headword. No chunk describes a
  tomb, shrine, ʿurs or custodianship for him.
- Saint:
  - Named in the book's alif-legend list of poets (p. 157, folio 139), and placed in time in body text
    only by relation to Shah ʿAbdul Latif: "And his Panjabi contemporary Bullhe Shah repeats in ecstatic
    rhythms" (p. 158, folio 140).
  - The lines quoted in body text: "The mollas have made me read lessons, / They have not gone beyond the
    alif!" (p. 158, folio 140).
  - Paired with Sachal Sarmast: "the Panjabi Bullhe Shah and the Sindhi Sachal Sarmast, with their highly
    ecstatic songs, are the most typical exponents of this tendency" (pp. 176–177, folios 158–159).
  - Named as a poet to whom other verse was ascribed: "transplant some Bektashi verses into Panjabi and
    declare them to be compositions of Bullhe Shah" (p. 169, folio 151).
  - Index headword printed `Bullhe Shah (d. 1754)` (p. 351, folio 333) — the book's only death year for
    him, and index-level evidence only; see Doubt.
  - Cited through a commentary rather than directly, in an endnote: `Qanun-i cishq, ya<nr
    [text-layer?: yaʿnī] Halwa-e Panjtib [text-layer?: Halwa-e Panjāb], commentary on the kiifis
    [text-layer?: kāfīs] of Hz. Bullhe Shah, Kafi no. 75, p. 182; cf. also no. 77, p. 184, and no. 78,
    p. 186` (p. 279, folio 261); the same note adds `See also Lajwanti Ramakrishna, Panjabi Sufi Poets,
    p. xxx` (p. 279, folio 261).
  - A second endnote to the same work: "Thus Bullhe Shah in Qtiniin-i <ishq [text-layer?: Qānūn-i ʿishq],
    Kafi no. 109, p. 240." (p. 283, folio 265). "109" and "240" are the note's own internal locators and
    are not page references.
  - The same work appears in the bibliography as a Lahore chapbook: "Qiinun-e cishq
    [text-layer?: Qānūn-e ʿishq], i.e., l;lalwii-ye Panjiib [text-layer?: Ḥalwā-ye Panjāb], commentary on
    Bullhe Shah's kdfis [text-layer?: kāfīs]. Lahore: Allahwale ki qaumi dukiin [text-layer?: dukān],
    n . d . , no. 372." (p. 333, folio 315)
  - Quoted at second hand in a further endnote: "Bullhe Shah, quoted in L . Ramakrishna, Panjabi mystical
    poetry" (p. 286, folio 268), the note continuing that "Turk is the general designation of the Muslim
    in Indian popular tradition" (p. 286, folio 268).
  - Name form: "Bullhe Shah" throughout, "Hz. Bullhe Shah" in the endnote at p. 279 (folio 261); the
    Pass-1 note for chunk 006 records that "Kasur", "Abdullah Shah" and "Qadri" do not appear
    (pp. 157–158, folios 139–140).
- Shrine / Darbar: **(not stated)** in any of the six chunks — no site, burial place, date or order is
  given for him (p. 158, folio 140; p. 169, folio 151; p. 279, folio 261; p. 333, folio 315).
- Significance: he is the book's representative Panjabi ecstatic voice — the alif-legend (p. 158,
  folio 140) and the "most typical exponents" pairing with Sachal Sarmast (pp. 176–177, folios 158–159);
  the one trace of circulation the notes record is an undated Lahore chapbook commentary on his kāfīs
  (p. 333, folio 315).
- **Doubt:** the index headword `Bullhe Shah (d. 1754)` (p. 351, folio 333) carries a death year the
  Pass-1 worker recorded as differing from the archive row's — a human must reconcile them before either
  enters an entry. The book's spelling is "Bullhe Shah", not the archive row's "Bulleh Shah" (p. 279,
  folio 261). Chunk 012 flagged a third, unattributed Ramakrishna citation (p. 292, folio 274) not
  resolved to a named work. The mapping is author-only throughout (18 September ruling): no chunk ties
  the name to a mazar.
### garh-maharaja-shorkot — Garh Maharaja (Shorkot)

- **Evidence base:** five chunks (006, 011, 012, 014, 015) under the book's "Sultan Bahu" / "Sultan
  Bahoo" / `Sultan Bahii` — one substantial body-text discussion, two endnote citations, one bibliography
  entry and one index headword. No site, tomb or ʿurs anywhere.
- Saint:
  - Placed in time only relatively, in body text: "the Panjabi poet Sultan Bahu some 300 years later
    begins his Golden Alphabet with the lines" — "300 years later" than "the North Indian Chishti mystic
    Mascud Bakk [text-layer?: Masʿūd Bakk] in the late fourteenth century" (p. 160, folio 142).
  - His order, in the book's own words: "the dhikr of the profession of faith (la ilaha ilia Allah
    [text-layer?: lā ilāha illā Allāh], the negative and the positive assertion as used in the Qadiriyya
    order, to which Sultan Bahu belonged) is the water that makes the 'plant' God grow in man's heart"
    (p. 161, folio 143).
  - The Alif stanza quoted in full, four lines with the refrain "0 Hu! [text-layer?: O Hu!]", opening
    "Alif: Allah is a jasmine bud which the preceptor has planted in my heart" and closing "May the
    efficient preceptor live long, says Bahu, who planted this plant"; it cites "[cf. Sura 50116)
    [text-layer?: Sura 50/16]" for the jugular vein (p. 160, folio 142).
  - Cited as an author in an endnote: `Sultan Bahu, Abyiit [text-layer?: Abyāt], verse 1` (p. 280,
    folio 262), set beside a couplet of Angelus Silesius, `Cherubinisd1er Wandersmann
    [text-layer?: Cherubinischer Wandersmann], I, 81` — i.e. used in a comparison with German Baroque
    mysticism (p. 280, folio 262). Nothing else is said of him there.

### shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori — Shrine of Makhdoom Abdul Rahim Girhori

Named "c Abdur Rahim Girhori" [text-layer?: ʿAbdur Raḥīm Girhōrī], "Girhori", "the Sindhi mystic
Girhori", and indexed `Girhon, c Abdur Ral;lim` [text-layer?: `Girhorī, ʿAbdur Raḥīm`] (pp. 224–225, 294,
318, 353).

- **Saint**
  - "The Naqshbandi mystic c Abdur Rahim Girhori, who was killed when destroying a Shiva temple (1778),
    offers his splendid version of a Divine address to the Prophet in his poetical commentary on Surat
    al-kauthar" (p. 224, folio 206). The date is printed "(1778)" and is quoted as printed (body-text
    digits survive, BRIEF §3). The temple is named only as "a Shiva temple" — its place is **(not
    stated)**, and the book names no shrine, tomb or ʿurs for him.
  - Index headword printed `Girhon, c Abdur Ral;lim (d . 1778)` [text-layer?: `Girhorī, ʿAbdur Raḥīm`]
    (p. 353, folio 335). A Pass-1 worker records that **the book's death year matches this row's
    exactly**; the body date (1778) and the index date (1778) also agree with each other.
  - Order: Naqshbandi (p. 224, folio 206) — the only silsila the book attaches to any of these five
    figures.
- **Significance**
  - His commentary is introduced as an instance of "some highly complicated nac tiyya" [text-layer?:
    naʿtiyya] "poetry in the Sindhi language" (p. 224, folio 206). Surat al-kauthar "was, besides A4-4uhii
    [text-layer?: Aḍ-Ḍuḥā] and An-Najm, always a favorite of those poets who eulogized the Prophet"
    (p. 224, folio 206).
  - "Girhori's commentary takes up many earlier elements; thus he has God say, among other things: So
    much gift did I not give to anyone but you" (p. 224, folio 206); the quoted Divine address runs on
    through Jesus, the Torah and Joseph (p. 224, folio 206) to "A small quantity only of your love, 0
    Ahmad, has reached Zulaikha" and "Hell is at your door supplicating like a beggar" (p. 225,
    folio 207).
  - Cited again in the notes: "Cf. a similar statement by the Sindhi mystic Girhori in his 'Commentary
    Surat al-kauthar,' see Annemarie Schimmel, 'Commentaries and Translations of the Qur"an in the
    Sindhi lan-guage'" (p. 294, folio 276).
  - Bibliography: "Daudpota, c umar Mu):i.ammad [text-layer?: ʿUmar Muḥammad]. Kalam-i Girhoi;i
    [text-layer?: Kalām-i Girhōṛī]. Karachi: Matbac al-c arab [text-layer?: Maṭbaʿ al-ʿArab], 1956."
    (p. 318, folio 300).
- **Doubt:** at p. 294, folio 276 the book gives only the surname plus "the Sindhi mystic" — the Pass-1
  worker flagged the thinness of that identification.
- **Doubt:** at p. 318, folio 300 **the evidence is a damaged word inside a title, not a named person**
  (`Kalam-i Girhoi;i` [text-layer?: `Kalām-i Girhōṛī`]) — the Pass-1 worker asked that it be weighed
  accordingly.
- **Doubt:** the index headword itself is damaged — `Girhon` for `Girhorī` (p. 353, folio 335).
### dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed — Dargah / Roza Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed

The book's "Shah ʿInayat of Jhok", printed variously "Shah c 1nayat of Jhok", "Shah < Inayat of Jhok",

### shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya — Shrine of Bahauddin Zakariya

- **Evidence base:** three chunks (004, 012, 015) — one body-text mention, two endnote statements and one
  index headword. He is named always in relation to the Persian poet Fakhruddin ʿIraqi, except in the one
  note that states his resting place.
- Saint:
  - Titled in an endnote: "Multan is the resting place of the great Suhrawardi saint Baha'uddin Zakariya
    (d. 1267) ." (p. 286, folio 268), printed in a note otherwise citing "Qa4i Qddan j6 kalam"
    [text-layer?: Qāḍī Qādan jo kalām].
  - Death year 1267 given three times and consistently: body text (p. 104, folio 94), endnote (p. 286,
    folio 268) and index headword `Ba ha' uddin Zakariya M ultani (d . 1267)`
    [text-layer?: `Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā Multānī`] (p. 351, folio 333); the intrusive spaces in
    `Ba ha' uddin` and `M ultani` are as printed. Pass-1 records that body-text digits survive in this
    book (BRIEF §3), so "1267" is quotable as printed (p. 104, folio 94).
  - As a master: "Fakhruddin c Iraqi"[text-layer?: Fakhruddīn ʿIrāqī] "lived for some years in Konya
    after his master, Baha'uddin Zakariya of Multan, had died in 1267" (p. 104, folio 94).
  - The surrounding context is a remark on Rumi's circle — it is "remarkable that practically nothing is
    known about his relations with Sadruddin' s friend, the gifted Persian poet Fakhruddin c
    Iraqi"[text-layer?: ʿIrāqī] (p. 103, folio 93, continuing to p. 104, folio 94).
- Shrine / Darbar: the only statement is "Multan is the resting place" (p. 286, folio 268). Chunk 004
  records that at his body-text appearance the book gives him no other title, no silsila, no shrine, no
  tomb, no ʿurs and no custodianship (p. 104, folio 94); the "Suhrawardi" attribution comes only from the
  later note (p. 286, folio 268), and the nisba "of Multan" from the body text (p. 104, folio 94).
- Practices & Events: "Fakhruddin 'Iraqi's poems for his master Baha'uddin Zakariya Multani" are given as
  the classical parallel to popular poems composed in honour of saints (p. 287, folio 269).
- Significance: he anchors ʿIraqi's biography in India for the book (p. 104, folio 94) and supplies its
  classical case of poetry written in a saint's honour, against which the Sindhi, Panjabi and Balochi
  popular genres are set (p. 287, folio 269).

### darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz — Darbar Malik Ahmad Ayaz

- **Evidence base:** one chunk (004), body text only, four sentences all on p. 84 (folio 74). Purely a
  literary reference — Ayaz as a figure in the Persian lover/beloved topos — mapped under the
  18 September person ruling.
- Saint / figure:
  - "More surprising is the transformation of the great warrior king of Ghazna, Sultan Mahmud, into the
    model of the ideal lover" (p. 84, folio 74).
  - "This conqueror of northwest India is praised for his close relationship with his Turkish officer
    Ayaz, who appears as the exemplar of the 'servant.'" (p. 84, folio 74)
  - "But the king finally became 'the slave of his slave'" (p. 84, folio 74).
- Shrine / Darbar: **(not stated)** — the book gives Ayaz no date, no place, no burial and no title
  beyond "his Turkish officer", and does not name Lahore (p. 84, folio 74).
- Significance: he functions in the book as the exemplary "servant" of the Mahmud–Ayaz topos and as the
  hinge of its inversion, the king becoming his slave's slave (p. 84, folio 74).
- **Doubt (unresolved — for human adjudication):** this row is deliberately **not** on this book's Lahore
  place-level list. Its `location_short` in the archive is truncated and never says Lahore, although its
  `principal_figure` field reads "governor of Lahore". Pass-1 chunk 009 included it under a Lahore head;
  the corrected map excludes it, and that exclusion is what this file follows — the row carries only its
  own p. 84 (folio 74) material. The question stays open pending a regeneration of the `location_short`
  column, after which a human must decide whether the row rejoins this book's Lahore place-level list.
  Independently of that, the book itself never names Lahore in connection with Ayaz (p. 84, folio 74),
  so nothing in this text supports the Lahore attachment on its own.

### madho-lal-hussain — Shrine of Hazrat Madho Lal Hussain (Shah Hussain Darbar)

- **Kind of evidence — read this first.** Alone of these three ids, this one rests on the book **describing
  the figure and his burial place**: p. 170–171 is, per Pass 1, "the chunk's only explicit tomb description
  inside Pakistan". The remaining mentions are literary.

**Saint**
- "the ecstatic mystic of the Qadiri order", writing in Panjabi verse "as early as the sixteenth century"
  (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153); elsewhere "the Panjabi mystic Madho Lal Husain" (p. 185, folio 167).
- Index headword printed `Madho Lal };Iusain (d. 1593)` [text-layer?: `Madho Lāl Ḥusain`] (p. 357,
  folio 339).

**Shrine / Darbar**
- He "is buried together with his Hindu beloved in a modest tomb near the Shalimar gardens in Lahore"
  (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). No urs, custodianship, endowment, visitor practice or building date is
  stated.

**Significance**
- His Panjabi verse carries the spiritualization of Hir and Ranjha: "Allusions to this theme are found as
  early as the sixteenth century in the Panjabi verse of Madho Lal Husain" (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153).
- Named for a poetic topic he shares with the Turkish Bektashi Kaygusuz Abdal: "he sings of his insatiable
  appetite (a topic also used by the Panjabi mystic Madho Lal Husain), ridiculing or reversing the
  classical topic of the mystic's insatiable thirst for more and more love" (p. 185, folio 167).

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

- **Evidence base:** two chunks (012, 015) — two statements inside a single endnote and one index

- Saint: index headword printed `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (13th cent.)` (p. 357, folio 339) — a century, not
  a year.
- Shrine / Darbar: named with his site as "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan" (p. 287, folio 269); no
  building, ʿurs, custodianship or date is described.
- Practices & Events:
  - "in Sind, where the women often use endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal) in addressing the saints,
    we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan, who is often implored when a woman wants a
    son (Annemarie Schimmel, "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal")" (p. 287, folio 269) — women's
    petitionary devotion for a son, the genre being women's wedding songs.
  - The devotion crosses languages: "examples for Lal Shahbaz and Pir Murad of Thatta are also found in
    Balochi because Baloch tribes migrate in winter into the Indus Valley; see Longworth Dames, Popular
    Poetry." (p. 287, folio 269)
- Significance: he is the book's instance of a saint addressed in vernacular women's poetry and carried
  by seasonal migration into a second language community (p. 287, folio 269).
### shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir — Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir

- **Evidence base:** two chunks (012, 015) — one endnote sentence and a bare index headword. Place-level
  under ruling (a): the book names a mountain, not a temple, and gives no deity, date or custodian.
- Shrine / Darbar: index headword printed `Hingliij, Mount`[text-layer?: `Hinglāj, Mount`] (p. 354,
  folio 336), with no description in the index beyond the word `Mount`; located "west of Sind" (p. 288,
  folio 270).
- Practices & Events: pilgrimage to the mountain, with a mystical reading of its direction — "even when
  the pilgrims travel to Mt. Hinglaj, which is situated west of Sind, they are said to "go East," i.e.,
  to find illumination." (p. 288, folio 270). That is the whole of the practice material: the direction
  of travel and its symbolic gloss, with no account of who goes, when, or to what.
- **Doubt:** the attachment is place-level under ruling (a) and is to the mountain; the book names no
  temple, deity, date or custodian (p. 288, folio 270; p. 354, folio 336).
### shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi — Shri Panchmukhi Hanuman Mandir (Karachi)

- **Evidence base:** among the thinnest of the thirteen — a bare name inside a literary formula
  (chunk 007) and a bare index headword with no sub-entries (chunk 015). Nothing about Karachi, the
  temple, or any practice.
- Figure: named once inside the book's paraphrase of a folk-poetic collapse of categories — "finally
  Hallaj and his judge, Shams-i Tabriz and the molla, and Abu Hanifa and Hanuman are one and the same,
  and there is no longer Turk, Hindu, or Peshawari." (p. 176, folio 158). Index headword printed
  `Hanuman`, with no sub-entries (p. 354, folio 336).
- Shrine / Darbar: **(not stated)**.
- **Doubt:** Pass-1 flagged both occurrences as figure-level and weak (p. 176, folio 158; p. 354,
  folio 336). The attachment is to the deity's name as one term in a Muslim poet's list of paired
  opposites, not to this temple; a human should decide whether it is worth carrying into the entry at all.
### umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir — Umarkot (Amarkot) Shiv Mandir

- **Evidence base:** one chunk (009), one occurrence — a place name inside a merism in body text, and
  nothing else. No temple, deity, practice, date or custodian.
- Shrine / Darbar: **(not stated)**.
- Significance (such as it is): "Omarkot" is named in a list of the reach of the Prophet's "blissful rain
  cloud" as imagined by poets following Shah ʿAbdul Latif — "extending not only from Istanbul to
  Jaisalmer, from Delhi to Omarkot but from Kairouan to Rohri and from England to China" (p. 224,
  folio 206). The Pass-1 worker records that this is the whole of the material: a place name inside a
  merism, i.e. the town figures only as a far edge of an imagined horizon.
- **Doubt:** the identification with the archive's "Umarkot (Amarkot)" rests on spelling alone — the book
  prints "Omarkot" (p. 224, folio 206) — and was flagged as such in chunk 009.

## Place-level material (ruling (a))

*One subsection per place. Every archive row in that place takes this material, because under ruling
(a) (Rauf, 21 September 2026, confirmed heavy 22 September 2026) a bare toponym takes the id of every
archive row in that place: place-level material is part of the tradition of every site there. These
attachments are preserved in full, not pruned. Places are ordered by volume of material, most first.
Every fact carries the page reference its Pass-1 chunk gave it, and every `[text-layer?]` flag is kept.*

> **READ THIS BEFORE USING ANY OF IT.** In *As Through a Veil* almost every Lahore, Karachi and
> Islamabad attachment — and a substantial part of the Multan one — is a **place of publication in a
> bibliography entry or a note**, i.e. where a book was printed, not the book discussing that place.
> Each subsection carries a bold **Kind of evidence:** line saying which kind it is, so that whoever
> writes a shrine entry can weigh or drop the imprint-only material wholesale. The genuinely topical
> items are few and are named explicitly where they occur.

> **RECONCILIATION MADE HERE (Lahore row count only).** The chunk files themselves still disagree,
> because the toponym map was corrected mid-run, and they have **not** been edited:
> chunk 003 and chunk 007 head their Lahore bullets with **31** ids (the old, undercounting map);
> chunks 010, 013, 014 and 015 use **35** (correct); chunk 009 uses **36** (the 35 plus
> `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz`). **The correct set is 35** and every Lahore bullet below has been
> normalised to it. `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` is excluded: its archive `location_short` is truncated
> and never says Lahore, so it is handled as its own row elsewhere. The four ids missing from the
> 31-id bullets are `darbar-abul-muali-qadri`, `ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed`, `tahir-bandagi-qadri` and
> `wasif-ali-wasif`. **This is the only reconciliation made in this section**; every other
> disagreement is kept side by side.

### Lahore — 35 rows

**Rows:** `abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi`, `bibi-pak-daman`, `darbar-abul-muali-qadri`, `data-darbar`, `ganj-e-inayat-sarkar`, `ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed`, `gurdwara-babay-nanki`, `gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore`, `gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan`, `gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang`, `gurdwara-chowmala-sahib`, `gurdwara-dera-sahib`, `gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji`, `gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore`, `gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh`, `gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian`, `jain-mandir-lahore`, `khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz`, `krishna-mandir-ravi-road`, `loh-temple-lava-temple`, `madho-lal-hussain`, `mazar-e-iqbal`, `peer-makki`, `samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh`, `shah-jamal`, `shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh`, `shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal`, `shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari`, `shrine-of-mian-mir`, `shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib`, `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri`, `tahir-bandagi-qadri`, `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak`, `valmik-mandir-naqi-road`, `wasif-ali-wasif`

**Kind of evidence: OVERWHELMINGLY IMPRINT-ONLY — a place of publication or a publisher's address in
the bibliography and the notes, not the book discussing Lahore.** Chunk 014 states it flatly: "in this
chunk every single 'Lahore' is a place of publication or a publisher's address — the book nowhere
discusses Lahore itself here." Chunk 010 says the toponym "occurs twice, both times in one note, and
both times only as a place of publication," and calls the attachment "place-level and weaker than
place-level." **Chunk 009's entire Lahore attachment — all 35 rows — rests on the single imprint
"(Lahore, 1932)".** **Exactly two facts below are genuinely topical** and are the only Lahore material
about the city itself: Hujwiri's last years in Lahore (p. 61, folio 51) and Madho Lal Husain's tomb
near the Shalimar gardens (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). A shrine-entry writer can drop everything
else in this subsection as bibliographic apparatus.

**Topical (the book on Lahore itself) — two facts only**
- Lahore is named once in twenty-five pages of chunk 003, and only as the place where a book was
  finished: "The `Kash/ al-mafJjub`[text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] of Hujwiri, who spent his last years
  in Lahore, is the first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- Madho Lal Husain "is buried together with his Hindu beloved in a modest tomb near the Shalimar
  gardens in Lahore" (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). This is chunk 007's only Lahore occurrence. The
  Shalimar gardens are named only as the landmark for that tomb; nothing else is said about them.
- Negative finding (chunk 003): no shrine, tomb, mosque, ʿurs, custodian, endowment or building in
  Lahore is mentioned, and the city is not otherwise described (not stated) (p. 61, folio 51 context).

**Index**
- `Lahore` stands as its own headword in the Index of Proper Names (p. 357, folio 339).

**Imprint-only — notes**
- Iqbal's "epic ]avidniima (Lahore, 1932), lines 727-728", note 2 to chapter 1, printed on p. 233
  (folio not stated). This single imprint is the whole of chunk 009's Lahore evidence; the chunk says
  nothing else about the city, and nothing at all about any Lahore site, saint, shrine or practice.
- "Part translation in verse by Pir Imam Shah, Lahore 191 1"[text-layer?: 1911], "fly leaf. Another
  Panjabi verse translation by Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin, Lahore, 1939 (see L. D. Barnett, Panjabi,
  p. 39)." (p. 254, folio 236). The note is note 3 of the Rumi chapter and stands two notes after "The
  literature about Jalaluddin is almost inexhaustible"; **the work being translated is not named in the
  note itself** (not stated). So chunk 010's whole Lahore holding is: two Panjabi verse translations
  were published at Lahore, one in "191 1"[text-layer?: 1911] and one in "1939", by "Pir Imam Shah" and
  "Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin" respectively (p. 254, folio 236).
- Note 77: "A fine Panjabi version with a commentary by Haji Muhammad ad­din Naqshbandi Mujaddidi from
  Bombay was published in the `Alliihwiile ki qaumi dukkiin` [text-layer?: Allāhwāle kī qaumī dukkān],
  Lahore; it preserves the rhyme letter m, followed by the genitive particle di." (p. 301, folio 283).

**Imprint-only — bibliography**

  [text-layer?: Ḥāfiẓ] Jhanda. Guldasta-i `l;:lafiz;` [text-layer?: Ḥāfiẓ] Jhanda. Lahore: Allah-wale

**Kind of evidence: IMPRINT-ONLY, plus one conference venue. The book nowhere discusses Karachi
itself.** Chunk 013: "Karachi appears only as a place of publication." Chunk 014: "again **imprint
only — the book does not discuss Karachi in this chunk**", and notes that the imprints "together make
Karachi the single densest Pakistan-side publishing place in the bibliography." Chunk 012's Karachi is
a **conference venue only** (the 1975 Seerat Conference), which is the nearest thing to a topical
mention and is still not about the city. Nothing here concerns a Karachi shrine, saint or practice.
The one qualification chunk 013 makes in the material's favour: the imprints are "for five
source-bodies the archive's own bibliographies would use," i.e. they are a genuine finding aid.

### Multan — 8 rows

**Rows:** `prahladpuri-temple`, `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya`, `shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani`, `shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi`, `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam`, `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`, `shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez`, `shrine-of-syed-musa-pak`

**Kind of evidence: MIXED, and the only place in this book with a substantial GENUINELY TOPICAL
holding.** Multan is named as the site of a living shrine cult (Shams-i Tabriz's shrine, p. 175, folio
157), as a third holy city for the vernacular poets of Pakistan (p. 178, folio 160), and as
Baha'uddin Zakariya's resting place (p. 286, folio 268); two bibliography items are genuinely *about*
Multan (Shackle, "The Multani marsiya"; Ivanow, "Shums Tabrez of Multan"). **One item is imprint-only**
and is marked as such below. This is the subsection a shrine-entry writer should keep.

**Topical — the book on Multan**
- Multan is named as the site of a living shrine cult: Sachal's poem speaks "of Shams-i Tabriz,
  Maulana Rumi's spiritual beloved who, according to the Indian tradition, was flayed alive by the
  mollas in Multan where his shrine is still venerated" (p. 175, folio 157). The book attributes this
  to "the Indian tradition", not to itself.
- Multan is named as a third holy city for the vernacular poets of Pakistan: "the Great Feast (cid
  [text-layer?: ʿīd]) is to meet the Beloved; Mecca and Medina (and for the Sindhi and Panjabi, also
  Multan) are inside the heart" (p. 178, folio 160).
- "Multan is the resting place of the great Suhrawardi saint Baha'uddin Zakariya (d. 1267) ."
  (p. 286, folio 268).
- The place name occurs exactly once in the whole of chunk 004, inside the phrase "Baha'uddin Zakariya
  of Multan" (p. 104, folio 94). That chunk says nothing else about Multan — no shrine, no building,
  no practice, no date attached to the place itself; the one datum the place carries there is the
  death year "1267" given for Baha'uddin Zakariya "of Multan" (p. 104, folio 94).
- "the tales of Tamim ad-Dari in Dakhni and Tamil as well as in Multani verse" are named as an epical
  elaboration of early Islamic topics (p. 284, folio 266).
- "Fakhruddin 'Iraqi's poems for his master Baha'uddin Zakariya Multani" (p. 287, folio 269).

**Topical — bibliography items genuinely about Multan**
- "Christopher Shackle, 'The Multani marsiya,' offers an excellent analysis of popular religious
  poetry." (p. 285, folio 267); the full entry reads "Shackle, Christopher. 'The Multani mar§iya
  [text-layer?: marsiya].' Der Islam 55 (1978), pp. 281-3 1 1 ." (p. 339, folio 321).
- "About the mysterious figure of Shams see Vladimir Ivanow, 'Shams Tabriz of Multan.'" (p. 285, folio
  267) — the book's own words; it does not identify this Shams further. The full entry reads "lvanow
  [text-layer?: Ivanow], Vladimir. 'Shums Tabrez of Multan.' Muhammad Shafic Pre-/sentation Volume, ed.
  Dr. Syed Abdullah . Lahore: University of the Punjab, 1955; pp . lO<j-18." (p. 326, folio 308). The
  book names **no shrine, tomb or date** for "Shums Tabrez of Multan".

**Imprint-only**
- "Multan: Bazme §aqafat [text-layer?: Bazm-e s̱aqāfat], c. 1976" — "Styles and Themes in the Siraiki
  Mystical Poetry of Sind" (p. 340, folio 322). The only "Multan" in that entry is where it was
  published.

**Index**
- `Multan/i` stands as its own headword, the slash marking place and adjective together (p. 358, folio
  340). The only Multani figure the index names is `Ba ha' uddin Zakariya M ultani` (p. 351, folio 333),
  mapped in his own (site-specific) bullet elsewhere.

**Provenance note kept as the chunks left it**
- The 8-id set is the union of the toponym map's "Multan" and "Multan City" lines per BRIEF §7 (chunks
  007, 012, 015). Chunk 004 additionally records a row-count discrepancy between BRIEF §4 ("Multan 8")
  and `toponym_map.txt` ("Multan (7)"), unresolved in the chunk files. The authoritative count is 8.

### Peshawar — 10 rows

**Rows:** `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee`, `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple`, `gurdwara-bhai-beba-singh`, `gurdwara-bhai-joga-singh`, `kali-bari-mandir`, `panj-tirath`, `rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine`, `shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba`, `shrine-of-mian-umar-baba-chamkani`, `wadpagga-sharif`

**Kind of evidence: NOT imprint — but vanishingly thin. One demonym inside a line of verse-paraphrase,
plus an index headword.** The book never discusses Peshawar, names no Peshawar site, and the index
names **no** Peshawar figure at all. Nothing here can support a factual claim in a shrine entry; it is
recorded because ruling (a) is unconditional.

- The place is named only through its demonym, in the book's paraphrase of folk-poetic monism: "there
  is no longer Turk, Hindu, or Peshawari." (p. 176, folio 158). That is the whole of chunk 007's
  Peshawar material; the chunk's Doubts records the thinness of this evidence.

  at all (chunk 015, recorded under that chunk's Doubts).
