## Province-level material

*Swept from the raw chunks (`chunk_001`–`chunk_016`) under ruling (c). Only statements about a
province or region in general are kept. Statements really about one named town or site stay with that
toponym in `## Place-level material (ruling (a))` and are listed in the sweep notes; so are statements
that only place one named poet or ruler in a province. In this book "Panjabi" and "Sindhi" almost always
name a language or a body of poetry, and those language remarks are not province statements. Each
bullet is marked with its register: **book's historical prose**, **author's argument** (including her
argument in the endnotes), or **author's anecdote** (her own experience, in the present tense of the
book, 1982). Page references are PDF pages. The folio follows the book's rule (PDF − 10 up to PDF 128,
PDF − 18 from PDF 137), and the running header was checked at pp. 155, 172, 179, 222, 223 and 281.
Every bullet is province-level: weigh accordingly.*

### province: punjab

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after the historian Bada'uni, as the book states, with no dates given; book's historical prose): "only in the centuries after his death did mystical poets in Sind and the Punjab develop the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the poets being "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): "While the urban poets in India expressed their veneration of the Prophet in grandiose hymns to display their erudition, their contemporaries in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, endnote 80 to "Mystical Poetry in the Vernaculars"): the story of Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya, "a son of 'Ali by another wife than Fatima", "is found in the Panjab, in Bengal, South India, and Malaysia" (p. 284, folio 266).
- (book's word: "Panjabis"; refers to: present tense, no date; the version is dated "the late eighteenth century"; author's argument): the romance of Hir Ranjha (printed "Hfr Riinjhii" [text-layer?] [Hīr Rānjhā]), "elaborated in more than a hundred versions in Panjabi, Urdu, Sindhi, and Persian, was given its final form in the late eighteenth century by Warith Shah. It is his verse which is sung, time and again, wherever Panjabis meet" (p. 171, folio 153). This is about a people, not a territory, and the book does not say where they meet.

### province: sindh

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after Bada'uni, no dates given; book's historical prose): mystical poets "in Sind and the Punjab" developed "the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the first half of the sixteenth century" (Qadi Qadan) to "the eighteenth century" (Shah 'Abdul Latif), as printed; book's historical prose): "In Sind, the range of folk tales utilized for mystical purposes is even wider." Early allusions appear "however veiled, in Qadi Qadan's verse"; they "become more obvious in Shah 'Abdul Karim of Bulrri's [text-layer?] short verses, and reach full bloom in the Risiilo [text-layer?] [Risālo] of his great-grandson, Shah 'Abdul Latif of Bhit"; "Since the original tales were known to everyone, he usually begins his poetical renderings with the most dramatic moment" (p. 171, folio 153).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "shortly after 900", as printed; book's historical prose): Sind is "the country which he [Hallaj] traversed shortly after 900 to call people to God" (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote): "It is moving to listen to illiterate folk musicians in remote corners of Sind ... when they repeat his name [Hallaj's] in ecstatic songs, and claim that he was the true lover who paid with his life for his love" (the same sentence names "qawwals in the dargah of Gulbarga"; that part is not Sind) (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated, "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): poets "in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him [the Prophet] into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "The great mystical poets of Sind have devoted wonderful songs to the Prophet, who appears as the dawn between the night of created human life and the Divine day" (p. 223, folio 205).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote in endnote 48): "Incidentally, I have learned more about Islamic piety from women in Anatolia and Sind than from most of my learned colleagues or from sophisticated books—they still cling to 'the faith of the old women' as it was praised in early Islam, and represent the values of a moderately mystical Islam" (p. 281, folio 263).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: present tense, and "in more recent times" for the Pir Pagaro verses; author's argument in endnote 109, citing Annemarie Schimmel, "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal"): "in Sind, where the women often use endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal [text-layer?]) in addressing the saints, we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan ... and, in more recent times, verses devoted to the Pir Pagaro" (p. 287, folio 269). Only the region-wide part (the women's diminutives and the existence of saint-poems in Sind) belongs here. The Lal Shahbaz/Sehwan detail ("often implored when a woman wants a son") is a site statement under ruling (a).

### not attached

- "Baloch tribes" / "Indus Valley": endnote 109 says "examples for Lal Shahbaz and Pir Murad of Thatta are also found in Balochi because Baloch tribes migrate in winter into the Indus Valley; see Longworth Dames, Popular Poetry" (p. 287, folio 269). The book names a people and a valley but no province, so it is given neither to `balochistan` nor to `sindh`.
- "a Balochi folk tradition": the tradition that 'Abdul Qadir Gilani offered the Prophet his neck to step on during the heavenly journey (p. 179, folio 161). This is the lore of a people, and the book gives it no region. Not given to `balochistan`.
- "Sindhi mystics" / "Sindhi musicians": "The Sindhi mystics who migrated to Burhanpur at the beginning of the sixteenth century used their mother tongue for the little songs which they recited during the sama'", and "the Sindhi musicians who, around 1580, tore the historian Bada'uni's heart 'with grating tunes' may well have belonged to this group" (p. 168, folio 150). These are migrants from Sind, and the scene is set outside Pakistan (Burhanpur), so the statement is not about the province.
- "Kashmir" / "Kashmiri": the only occurrences are bibliography titles (Tikku, *Persian Poets in Kashmir 1339-1846*, in a note at p. 247, folio 229, and in the bibliography at p. 342, folio 324; Aslah, *Tadhkirat-i shu'ara-i Kashmir*, at p. 313, folio 295), the remark that the Shaikh San'an story "is known in Kashmiri and Malay versions" (p. 285, folio 267, note 88, a language remark), and the index headword "Kashmiri" (p. 357, folio 339). Nothing about the region, and an unspecified Kashmir would not attach in any case.

*Sweep notes:* Hit counts in the 16 raw `.txt` chunks (case-insensitive except where noted): "Punjab" 7 (body 2: pp. 154, 222; the rest are "University of the Punjab" imprints and one index headword); "Panjab" not followed by -i 3 (p. 284 note 80; the title *Qanun-i 'ishq ya'ni Jalwa-i Panjab* in note 159; and the subject index); "Panjabi" 50; "Sind" as a whole word 39 (many are "Hyderabad/Sind" imprints); "Sindhi" 90; "Kashmir"/"Kashmiri" 5; "Balochi"/"Baloch/i" 3 in the body plus the index; "Sindh", "Baluchistan", "Balochistan", "Baluchi", "Frontier", "Pakhtunkhwa", "NWFP", "Multan province" and "suba" 0; "Pathan" 0. Excluded because they place one named figure or work in Sind, not because they describe the province: Shah 'Abdul Latif "in Sind" (p. 43, folio 33); Qadi Qadan "(d. 1551) in Sind" (p. 158, folio 140); "Shah Latif in Sind" (p. 160, folio 142); "the Sindhi ruler Jam Tamachi in the fifteenth century" (p. 172, folio 154); Shah 'Inayat of Jhok, "called the first social reformer in Sindhi Islam", executed "early 1718" (p. 175, folio 157); "Shah 'Abdul Latif in eighteenth-century Sind" (p. 187, folio 169); Mir 'Ali Shir Qani', "An eighteenth-century Persian poet of Sind" (p. 207, folio 189); "an eighteenth-century poet in Sind" (p. 218, folio 200); a "blackish slave girl from Sind" in the tradition about Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya's mother (p. 284, folio 266); Jahangir Hashimi's mathnawi "from sixteenth century Sind" (p. 304, folio 286, note 129). Excluded as toponym or site statements: Multan as a holy city "for the Sindhi and Panjabi" (p. 178, folio 160), which is already in the ruling (a) Multan subsection; Mt. Hinglaj, "situated west of Sind" (p. 288, folio 270, note 113); and Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan (p. 287, folio 269). Excluded as language or literature remarks with no regional claim: the "Sindhi folk poetry" genres madih, munaqibo and mu'jizo, with the bards who call themselves "bhan" (p. 222, folio 204); the kang crow as messenger "In Sindhi and Panjabi" (p. 162, folio 144); the remark that Pashto poetry is "like Balochi, more ascetic in outlook" (p. 155, folio 137); and all the Panjabi and Sindhi verse citations. Index and bibliography: the headwords "Punjab: 136, 204, 266n80" (p. 360, folio 342) and "Sind: 33, 136, 140, ..." (p. 362, folio 344) were used only to check that nothing was missed, and their locators agree with the body pages above. The subject index entry "Kang, the crow, messenger bird in Sind and the Panjab: 144" (p. 369, folio 351) turns a body language remark ("In Sindhi and Panjabi", p. 162) into a regional one. The body wording is followed and the entry is not used. Flags: "Bulrri" and "Piral, Ghauthal" carry text-layer damage that may hide lost diacritics, and they are kept as printed. "Sind" and "the Punjab" are never bounded or qualified historically by the book (no "undivided", no suba), so no boundary is supplied. "Hallaj ... shortly after 900" and "around 1580" are body-text digits, which survive in this text layer.

## Cross-cutting material

*Practices, institutions and economy first, then the author's own arguments.*

### Practices, institutions, economy

#### Samaʿ and the lawfulness of music

- Samaʿ is given a date and a place as an institution: `as early as in the mid-ninth century, the institution of samac` [text-layer?: samāʿ]`, mystical concert, was known at least in Baghdad, and verses were recited or sung in connection with music and often whirling dance. This was a starting point for one kind of Sufi poetry.` (p. 27, folio 17).
- Before the orders existed: `Even during its formative period, during the ninth and early tenth centuries, when only a small number of worthy disciples gathered at the masters' houses and regular fraternities had not yet developed, it was common to discuss mystical problems among initiated friends` (p. 27, folio 17); novices `would be warned not to care too much for the turuhat afi-tiiiffya` [text-layer?: reading uncertain]`, the half-technical, verbose talk of the Sufis` (p. 27, folio 17).
- Form follows performance: "popular style" verses "immediately sound singable and must have been used in samac [text-layer?: samāʿ] meetings; they usually consist of rather short lines in light meter" (p. 33, folio 23). Rumi's rhythmical forms "are thus much closer to popular poetry as it was used in the dervish tekkes during the samac [text-layer?: samāʿ] than to the ideals of classical, courtly poetry" (p. 17, folio 7).
- In the Subcontinent, samaʿ is the original setting of vernacular mystical verse: "That mystical poetry in folk idioms was first composed for samac [text-layer?: samāʿ] assemblies is known also from the Subcontinent." (p. 168, folio 150) The Burhanpur migrants sang in Sindhi during the samac, and "the Sindhi musicians who, around 1580, tore the historian Bada'uni's heart 'with grating tunes' may well have belonged to this group." (p. 168, folio 150) The formal consequence is "throbbing, repetitive sounds with the swaying motion of the body and the strong rhythmical element, which can be underscored by the use of drums" (p. 168, folio 150).
- Bada'uni "mentions at various instances Sufis who 'used to sing ecstatic songs in Hindi'" (p. 278, folio 260).
- In Rumi's imagery "in an unexpected turn the `samac` [text-layer?: samāʿ] is once called his army", i.e. Solomon-Love's army (p. 139, folio 121); and a ghazal is quoted on "the garden of Love, longing for the mystical dance / Those with cypress-statures come hand-clapping like plane-trees", called by the author "an early, playful ghazal" (p. 121, folio 111).
- Note 49 records the use "of the (Persian) rubifi" [text-layer?: rubāʿī] "in samii'" [text-layer?: samāʿ] (note printed on p. 237, folio 219).
- **The orthodox reaction is treated as constitutive, not incidental.** Love poems read mystically "inspired them to ecstasy and more than once caused the death of a lover, be he Sufi or layman"; orthodoxy recognised the danger of "breaking out of the well-ordered world of the nomos, the Divinely inspired law, into the vast fields of eros in all its shades; hence the Muslim orthodoxy's fierce reactions to the introduction of music in Sufi circles" (p. 34, folio 24). Note 53 states: "The question to what extent, and if at all, music is permitted in Islam, continues up to this day, and has resulted in an enormous amount of treatises pro and con", and records "Abu'l-Hasan an-Nuri, who died in ecstasy after listening to a little song", and "Bakhtiar Kaki, the Chishti leader of Delhi, who died in 1235 after listening for days to a verse by Ahmad-i Jam Zandapil" (note printed on p. 238, folio 220). The index carries the sub-entry `orthodoxy against` under the headword `Music` (p. 372, folio 354), and the bibliography lists `Vajda, George. "Un libelle contre la danse des soufis ." Studia Islamica 51 (1980), pp . 163-77.` (p. 342, folio 324).
- **Two glosses of the term stand side by side and are not reconciled.** The text calls samaʿ a "mystical concert" (p. 27, folio 17); the index glosses `Sama ', mystical dance` [text-layer?: samāʿ] (p. 374, folio 356).

#### Dhikr, the name, blessing, and initiation

- Dhikr defined as a whole-body discipline: in *dhikr*, "the recollection of God", "not only the tongue and then the heart are filled with the name of God, but the whole body of the meditating Sufi is permeated so that his blood and each of his limbs is replete with the name of God and practices, as it were, its own dhikr" (p. 38, folio 28). Against this, Hallaj: "Dhikr is a means [or 'a central pearl'] which hides You from my view" (p. 44, folio 34).
- The formula: the "bipartite profession of faith, `lti iltiha illti Allah`[text-layer?: lā ilāha illā Allāh]" — "This formula is used by many Sufi orders as dhikr" (p. 69, folio 59). No order is named there (not stated).
- Graded dhikr taught through women's work: "As you take the cotton, you should do dhikr-i jali, / As you separate the cotton, you should do dhikr-i qalbi, / And as you spool the thread you should do dhikr-i 0aini [text-layer?: dhikr-i ʿainī]: / The threads of breath should be counted one by one, 0 sister" (p. 163, folio 145).
- Dhikr becomes a genre: "In Gujarat, a special form of poetry called jikrr [text-layer?: jikrī], from dhikr, developed around 1500 ... and "the qawwtils [text-layer?: qawwāls] of Gujarat sang these jikri which bore traces of rapture and love"" (p. 283, folio 265); the index registers the variant with its own cross-reference, `Jikri, see Dhikr` (p. 369, folio 351).
- The blessing on the Prophet as a devotional office: "the !ja/awat-i shanJ [text-layer?: ṣalawāt-i sharīf] or durud came to constitute an important element of the dhikr in some mystical groups, and is repeated hundreds of times as a protective prayer by innumerable faithful" (p. 195, folio 177), on the strength of a tradition that "God would bless ten times a person who blessed the Prophet once" (p. 195, folio 177). "Some reciters would repeat after each of its lines a blessing over the Prophet" when reciting the Burda (p. 204, folio 186).
- Handbooks of blessings named as in current use: "c Ali al-Muttaqi's Kanz al-'ummtil [text-layer?: Kanz al-ʿummāl], c Ali al-Qari's Al-Jiizb al-a'4Qm [text-layer?: al-Ḥizb al-aʿẓam], Giimiishkhanawi's [text-layer?: Gümüşkhanawī's] Majmu<at al-aurtid wa'el-ahzab, and Jazuli's Dalti'il al-khairtit" (p. 295, folio 277), together with the modern anthology "Mohammad Elias Burney, Mishkaa t us-salawaat. A Bouquet of Blessings of Muhammad the Prophet (Peace be upon Him)" (p. 295, folio 277).
- The power of the name as the rationale for controlled access: "to know the name of something means to be able to control it: how then could anyone be allowed to use the sacred name without proper initiation?"; and "the Sufis assume unusual or 'heavenly' names known only to the innermost circles of the initiated" (p. 38, folio 28). The notes speak of "this 'magic of the name,' of which the Sufis in their dhikr were very well aware" (p. 256, folio 238).
- Naming as an initiatic act: "One has to remember that a new name is usually given at an initia­tion, be it into an order, or a conversion or baptism; Maulana, by assuming Shamsuddin's name for his poetical activity, has as it were died to himself and found a new life in the mystical beloved" (p. 255, folio 237).
- Initiation without a living master: the *uwaisī* mystic "enters the Path without formal initiation through a living master" (p. 250, folio 232); the index glosses `Uwaisi, mystic who has entered the mystical path without fo rmal initia­tion by a living master` (p. 377, folio 359).
- Dream-vision as initiation: "among Sufis a dream of the Prophet was sometimes regarded as a kind of initiatory experience. It was commonly accepted that Satan can never assume the form of the Prophet" (p. 296, folio 278).
- The forty days' seclusion is glossed by the book itself: "steamed in the oven for forty days (i .e ., in the forty days' seclusion)" (p. 185, folio 167).
- Naming practice around the Prophet's name: it "or one of its equivalents (Ahmad, Mustafa, Taha, etc.) was commonly given to boys, but in order to pre­serve both its sanctity and its power, the word Muhammad is vocalized, in some languages, differently when applied to nor­mal human beings (thus Mehmet in Turkey, Mihammad or Mahammad in Morocco), while the full and correct vocaliza­tion is preserved for the Prophet" (p. 192, folio 174); in later times "he, like God, had 99 names", and "Sometimes, even more honorific names-up to 200!­were invented for him" (p. 192, folio 174). Saʿdi's eulogy "has also contributed to Islamic nomenclature, par­ticularly in India, for all four qualities-qasfm, jasfm, nasfm and wasfm [text-layer?: qasīm, jasīm, nasīm, wasīm]-are found as proper names, sometimes in compounds, such as Jasimuddin" (p. 214, folio 196); "yiisfn (Sura 36) or tiihii (Sura 20) ... became proper names in the Muslim world" (p. 214, folio 196).

#### Qawwali, the shrine, and the singers

- **The single clearest statement of shrine practice in the book:** "There are many songs in honor of other saints too, because each community has hymns in praise of the mystical guide at whose shrine the qawwali gather. Here was and still is the center of popular religious life." (p. 179, folio 161)
- Qawwali described as a performance: "ONE OF THE most moving experiences in the Subcontinent is to listen to a good qawwali. The musicians recite Persian or Urdu ghazals, beginning with a solo voice to which the group responds" (p. 189, folio not stated). The index gloss makes the word name both the songs and the performers: `Qawwiili, religious folk songs, group of singers, mainly in lndo-Pakistan` [text-layer?: qawwālī] (p. 373, folio 355).
- The form is traced to a ninth-century poem: one short poem of Dhu'n-Nun "has a form which is still used today by the qawwiili [text-layer?: qawwālī]. This form includes a first line which can be repeated by the chorus and different second hemistichs" (p. 35, folio 25). Qudsi Mashhadi's short naʿt "must be mentioned because it is still often sung in qawwalf" [text-layer?: qawwālī] "sessions" (p. 217, folio 199).
- Chorus-and-soloist forms named: "Soon the kaff [text-layer?: kāfī] developed, in which the first one or two lines are repeated over and over again by a chorus while the main lines of the poem are recited or sung by a single person." (p. 166, folio 148) Of the `tarjfband`[text-layer?: tarjīʿband]: "This form is also very fitting for musical performance, where the repeated verse can be taken up by the chorus to form a kind of continua" (p. 67, folio 57).
- Sung recitation at a dargah, in two languages and in sequence: at Maneri's dargah "after the noonday prayer, and the minstrels began to sing, first in Persian, the language of higher education, and then in Hindwi" (p. 153, folio not stated).
- "It should always be kept in mind that this poetry is mostly sung by members of Sufi orders and fraternities" (p. 18, folio 8).
- Living folk performance: "It is moving to listen to illiterate folk musicians in remote corners of Sind" repeat Hallaj's name (p. 175, folio 157).
- Instruments and players. "For the use of the flute see Qadi Abdul Mannan, Literary Heritage of Bangladesh, Medieval Period" (p. 283, folio 265); Shah Abdul Latif "praises also the instruments of the Yogis for their bewitching power" (p. 283, folio 265); Rumi "turns to the rebeck player who accompanied his song, admonishing him not to be so sleepy but rather to listen to his words" (p. 105, folio 95). The index treats instruments as apparatus: `Barba{, great lute` (p. 366, folio 348); `Surntl, a kind of oboe` (p. 375, folio 357); `Harp` (p. 368, folio 350); `Rebeck … player` (p. 374, folio 356); `Bhan (Sindhi), singer of praise poems for the Prophet` (p. 366, folio 348); `ghazal singer` (p. 368, folio 350).

#### The Mevlevi ritual and the whirling dance

- Dress symbolism in the ceremony: "The symbolism of the white robes-garments of resurrection-which appear after the Mevlevi dervishes have cast off their black coats at the beginning of the whirling dance, points to this deep secret of transforming Love" (p. 151, folio 133).
- The ritual's own exegesis, still current: `Abdulbaki GO!pinar!I [text-layer?: Gölpınarlı], Mevldna'dan sonra Mevlevilik [text-layer?: Mevlânâ'dan sonra Mevlevilik], pp. 473-75, quotes Divane Mehmed Celebi's Risale [text-layer?: Dīvāne Mehmed Çelebi's Risāle], in which the dervish explains: "The khirqa is my tomb, / The high hat my tombstone," and interprets the mystical dance in terms of fanii' and baqii [text-layer?: fanāʾ and baqāʾ], an interpretation which is still current among the Mevlevi dervishes. GolpinarlI also offers texts and melodies used in the Mevlevi ceremonies` (p. 277, folio 259). Further references in the same note: `Fritz Meier, "Der Derwischtanz"` (p. 276, folio 258); `Marijan Mole [text-layer?: Molé], "La Danse exstatique en Islam"` and `Hellmut Ritter, "Die Mevlanafeiern in Konya"` (p. 277, folio 259); `Heiler, Erscheinungsformen, pp. 239-43` and `Gerardus van der Leeuw, In den hemel is enen dans, RGG 3rd. ed. , VI, pp. 61.i--1 4, "Tanz ."` (p. 276, folio 258).
- The opening music of the ritual is a named poem in a named tune: Rumi's most famous eulogy "is sung in c ltri's beautiful tune, at the beginning of the ritual dance of the Mevlevis" (p. 212, folio 194).
- Whirling in public space, before the order existed: "the rhythmical, melodious sound of the goldsmiths' hammers in the bazaar of Konya so enraptured Maulana that he took Salahuddin with him to whirl through the streets" (p. 100, folio 90); "In whirling dance he poured out his longing and love" (p. 95, folio 85).
- The order is founded a generation later: Sultan Walad "took over to organize his disciples into a proper order, known in the West as the Whirling Dervishes"; "With this organization and with his poetry about his father's life, Sultan Walad secured immortality for himself" (p. 104, folio 94). Schimmel notes the possibility of tracking "his influence on the poetry written in Persian or Turkish among the members of his order" (p. 17, folio 7).
- The index carries the order under both names — `Mevlevi (order)` with `see also Whirling Dervishes` (p. 358, folio 340) and `Whirling Dervishes (Mevlevis)` (p. 364, folio 346) — and glosses `Khirqa, the dervish frock` (p. 370, folio 352) and `Tekke, dervish lodge` (p. 376, folio 358). **The lodge term the index gives is Turkish, and no Indo-Pakistani equivalent (khanqah, astana) is a headword anywhere in the index.**

#### The tomb, the ʿurs, and the cult of the saint

- The book's own definition of the ʿurs: `curs, "wedding," celebrations during a saint's death anniversary` [text-layer?: ʿurs] (p. 377, folio 359).
- The one ʿurs observed in the book, with a named musical genre: "the Hindi verse sung in the `band samif` [text-layer?: band samāʿ] during the `<urs` [text-layer?: ʿurs] in Gulbarga", cited to "S . S . Khusro Hussaini, 'Bund sama<,' p. 183" (p. 307, folio 289).
- The book's definition of a dargah is institutional, not architectural: `Dargah, seat of the head of an order or its branches` (p. 366, folio 348).
- Tomb visitation: Burhanuddin Muhaqqiq's "modest tomb beneath the majestic Mt. Erjiyas is still visited by the pious" (p. 94, folio 84).
- Music at a tomb, requested by its occupant: Rumi "asked his friends not to visit his tomb without drums and instruments, for his intoxication would last beyond the grave" (p. 104, folio 94); and "all inhabitants of Konya participated in his funeral, which ended in an ecstatic dance" (p. 104, folio 94).
- Tomb epigraphy, two instances: Gesudaraz's poem "is inscribed around the interior of the grand dome over his tomb in Gulbarga" (p. 254, folio 236); and of a poem with the *takhalluṣ* Shamsuddin, "these verses are inscribed on his sarcophagus" (p. 257, folio 239) — whose sarcophagus is left ambiguous in the source.
- The only mention in the book of a death anniversary or a vow as an occasion for recitation: Chelebi's poem "is still sung on the Prophet's birth­day, but also at the anniversary of a bereavement or in fulfill­ment of a vow" (p. 197, folio 179).
- Petition to a saint, and the gendered form it takes: Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan "is often implored when a woman wants a son", in women's poems collected as "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal" (p. 287, folio 269). Address to saints in Sind uses "endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal)" (p. 287, folio 269).
- Seasonal migration carries the devotional verse outward: "examples for Lal Shahbaz and Pir Murad of Thatta are also found in Balochi because Baloch tribes migrate in winter into the Indus Valley" (p. 287, folio 269).
- Cemeteries as a scholarly subject: "Louis Massignon, La Cite des Morts au Caire" is cited bare (p. 243, folio 225).

#### The maulid and the ritual calendar

- **Two origin dates for the maulid stand in the book and are not reconciled.** (i) "Celebrations of his maulid, birthday, on 12 Rabic al-awwal [text-layer?: Rabīʿ al-awwal], the third lunar month, are mentioned first about 1200 and soon spread into the various Islamic lands" (p. 197, folio 179). (ii) "The first recorded maulid celebrations took place in Arbela (northern Iraq) in `604'1207` [text-layer?: 604/1207]; see `Handworterbuch des Islam` [text-layer?: Handwörterbuch des Islam], s.v. maulid." (p. 297, folio 279).
- **12 Rabiʿ al-awwal is given as the Prophet's birthday and as the day of his death, both as statements of what is observed.** Birthday: p. 197, folio 179, and the index gloss `Rabi" al-awwal, third lunar month, on the 12th Muhammad's birthday` [text-layer?: Rabīʿ al-awwal] (p. 373, folio 355). Death-day: "In Indo-Pakistan maulid literature proper is rather recent, for the 12 Rabic al-awwal was formerly mainly remembered as the day of Mu­hammad's death" (p. 199, folio 181).
- Spread, with rulers and dates: "In Morocco the Merinid ruler Abu Yac qub Yusuf introduced maulid cele­brations in 1291, while the Mamluks in Egypt celebrated the Prophet's birthday with great splendor during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries" (p. 197, folio 179); "In Turkey, such celebrations must have been known in the early four­teenth century" (p. 197, folio 179).
- How a maulid is kept: "One lights candles, puts on good clothes; sometimes incense is burned and from the Maghreb to Turkey and East Africa it is customary to distribute sweets" (p. 199, folio 181). A source "mentions the `ctliiida` [text-layer?: ʿaṣīda], a sweet which is usually distributed at childbirth" (p. 297, folio 279). "In Turkey, candy and lemon sherbet is offered; sometimes the guests receive small amounts of sweets to take home tabarrukan, 'for the sake of blessing.'" (p. 298, folio 280) During the Turkish recitation, "when this line is recited in the Turkish mevlad [text-layer?: mevlud] all those present gently touch their neighbor' s back" (p. 198, folio 180).
- Recorded and commercial forms: "In Egypt, the maulid of the Shadhili Sufi al-Munawi is particularly well loved, and is often played on records" (p. 199, folio 181).
- Regional maulid literatures: "Major maulid in the Subcontinent were gen­erally composed in Persian; the genre was introduced into Bengali not long ago... In Panjabi and Sindhi, the miracles of the Prophet's birth are told in balladesk or lyrical forms" (p. 199, folio 181). "As for the maulid in Sindhi, it deals not only with the Prophet's birth but is a lyrical encomium with a refrain which, in singing, is repeated before every new line. It is used on festive occasions such as the shab-i bariit" [text-layer?: shab-i barāt] "or after the tariiwilJ" [text-layer?: tarāwīḥ] "prayers in Ramadan" (p. 222, folio 204). "Barzanji was Shafiite `qti4i` [text-layer?: qāḍī] of Medina, h e died in 1765; his `mauliid` [text-layer?: maulūd] is also used in North Africa" (p. 298, folio 280).
- Reform and reaction: "some modernists have at­tacked the custom of reciting this kind of poetry" (p. 199, folio 181); "Lately however maulid celebrations everywhere are put in the service of reformist movements and serve to remind the Muslims not so much of the traditional miracles but of the wonderful, heroic and nation-building acts of their beloved Prophet" (p. 200, folio 182).
- Genealogy inside the maulid, joined to Sufi succession: "Some maulid con­tain the genealogy of the Prophet, for the nobility of his de­scent is important; this feature is also found in the Sufi tradi­tion where the silsila of the Pir is led back to Muhammad and from him to Adam" (p. 200, folio 182).
- Feast-day naming: "In India and Pakistan, I found Mi'raj, or Miraj Din even as proper names, most likely for children born on the feast of `mi<raj` [text-layer?: miʿrāj], as among Turkish and North African Muslims boys may be called maulud and girls mauludiyya, when they are born on the Prophet's birthday." (p. 299, folio 281)
- The index treats maulid as a genre as well as a feast: `Maulid, "birth," poems and celebra­tions in honor of Muhammad's birthday`, with the sub-entries `in Sindhi`, `maulidiyyat`, `maulad, Tur­kish mevlad` and `maulad as proper name` (p. 371, folio 353); the genre is marked in parentheses after two authors, `lbn al-Jauzi,C Abdur Ral;iman (d. 1200) (maulid)`[text-layer?: `Ibn al-Jauzī, ʿAbdur Raḥmān`] and `lbn Kathir, Isma'il (d. 1372) (maulid)` (both p. 355, folio 337); and `Maulid` is a `see also` target under the `MuJ:iammad, the Prophet` headword (p. 358, folio 340).
- The rest of the ritual year, as the index defines it: `Mul;tarram, first lunar month, remembrance of the tragedy of Kerbela` [text-layer?: Muḥarram] (p. 371, folio 353); `Ramaqan, the ninth lunar moon, the month of fasting` [text-layer?: Ramaḍān] (p. 374, folio 356); `Shab-i bartU, full moon in the month of Shacban, in which the destinies for the coming year are fixed, celebrated with fireworks` [text-layer?: Shab-i barāt] [text-layer?: Shaʿbān] (p. 375, folio 357); `Lai/at al-qadr, the Night of Might, in which the Koran was first revealed` (p. 370, folio 352); `cfd: the Feast of Fastbreaking at the end of Ramadan, c[d 11/-fifr` [text-layer?: ʿīd al-fiṭr] `… the Feast of Offerings, cfd al-al/bii` [text-layer?: ʿīd al-aḍḥā] (p. 368, folio 350); `Tart'iwib prayer, performed after the evening meal during the fasting month` [text-layer?: tarāwīḥ] (p. 376, folio 358).
- The text adds detail the index omits: "The 'white nights' are the nights be­tween the thirteenth and fifteenth of each month, which were sometimes used for additional fasting or other supererogatory works; the `shab-i bariit` [text-layer?: shab-i barāt] was cel­ebrated, from the early Middle Ages, on the fourteenth `shacban` [text-layer?: shaʿbān]; one believes that in that night the destinies for the next year are fixed ." (p. 303, folio 285)
- The two ʿĪds in Rumi's usage: "love of Shams is the great Feast of Offerings, `c rd al-a4hii` [text-layer?: ʿīd al-aḍḥā]", with the author's comment that Rumi's idea "that growth is possible only through constant self-sacrifice, is conveniently expressed in such comparisons to Islamic ritual", and that Love "not only slaughters the lover at `c Id` [text-layer?: ʿĪd] day" (p. 123, folio 113); "`c1d`[text-layer?: ʿĪd], the great Feast, is meeting the beloved", "We celebrate the ' Id but once a year" (p. 83, folio 73).
- Muharram as an observance with its own genres: "For a Bengali jarf [text-layer?: jārī] celebration, which is accompanied in the Mymensingh area by dancing" (p. 285, folio 267); "The genre of maqtal l;iusain [text-layer?: maqtal Ḥusain] was popular in India from at least the thirteenth century, as can be concluded from Amir Khusrau's remarks, and even in a predominantly Sunni country like Egypt the feast of the Hasanain is celebrated with great enthusiasm." (p. 285, folio 267) The Bengali "jiiriniima (from Persian ziir, 'lamentation')" [text-layer?: jārīnāma / zār] are the Kerbela form (p. 174, folio 156); `Maqtal l;iusain`[text-layer?: `Maqtal Ḥusain`] is an index headword (p. 358, folio 340), so the book registers the martyrdom-narrative genre as such.
- The ritual year set inside a poetic genre: "Starting with the tragic events of Muharram, the soul-represented as a young woman-lives through the festive days of the year until it finally finds, in Dhu'l-hijja, the way to Mecca, the center of its longing, where the Divine Friend dwells, or to Medina, the beloved Prophet's resting place" (p. 165, folio 147); "The Muslim poets sometimes replaced the Indian months by the Muslim lunar months (in recent times even Christian months are sometimes found in Sindhi biirahmiisa [text-layer?: bārahmāsa])" (p. 165, folio 147).
- A weekly observance carrying a promised reward: "the author of a Sindhi mucjizo" [text-layer?: muʿjiza] "about Muhammad's miracles promised innumerable future rewards to those who read or recited it on a Thursday night. A similar promise is connected with na' tiyya poetry in South India, and probably in other areas as well" (p. 218, folio 200).

#### Poetic form as a religious institution: vernacular genres and performance forms

- The strategy stated: "Everywhere outside the center of the Islamic world it was the goal of mystical preachers and leaders of fraternities to reach the masses and infuse into them the love of God and His Prophet in words which they could easily comprehend, using images from their daily life" (p. 155, folio 137).
- The medium defined against the book: folk poetry "was meant to be recited or sung and not to be read or studied by scholars ... it was intended for the illiterate masses and was written down, if at all, only at a very late stage." (p. 166, folio 148)
- Didactic and calendrical genres: "both sifJarJf [text-layer?: sīharfī] and biirahmiisa [text-layer?: bārahmāsa], as well as imitations of the Arabo-Persian qa�fda [text-layer?: qaṣīda] with a very simple form of monorhyme, were used not so much for enthusiastic love songs but rather for didactic purposes (n�ihatniima [text-layer?: naṣīhatnāma]): descriptions of Muhammad's life and miracles, especially his ascension, the suffering of the martyrs of Kerbela, and the teaching of Islamic values, customs, and rituals." (p. 166, folio 148)
- Genres as pedagogical instruments, each tied to an occupation or a letter-scheme: "charkhf niima [text-layer?: charkhī nāma], spinning songs", "some of the earliest Urdu poems for mystical instruction" (p. 163, folio 145); "chakkf niima [text-layer?: chakkī nāma]", taking "inspiration from the most important occupation of the Indian housewife, the grinding of grain" (p. 164, folio 146); "The Golden Alphabet, known from the Ancient Near East and the Old Testament", "used among the Muslims in Pashto, Panjabi, and Sindhi poetry as well as in Turkish dervish circles and Swahili religious verse" (p. 165, folio 147); "many Panjabi si1]arfr [text-layer?: siharfī] ('thirty letter poems')" which "use indigenous words for each letter" (p. 165, folio 147); the "Biirahmiisa [text-layer?: Bārahmāsa] ('Twelve-Months-poem')", "restricted to the Indian tradition" (p. 165, folio 147). Teaching texts for villagers: "their contemporaries in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- Indigenous metre: "the poets used indigenous forms such as the two-lined d6ha [text-layer?: dohā] with its 24 (11 plus 13, or 13 plus 1 1 [text-layer?: 11]) miitras [text-layer?: mātrās]" (p. 166, folio 148).
- Sindhi devotional genres: "Sindhi folk poetry developed the genres of madiih, muniiqib6, and mucjiz6" [text-layer?: madāḥ, manāqiba, muʿjiza], "all of which deal with the Prophet, the last one now and then also with saints" (p. 222, folio 204); the *manāqiba* is spelled a second way on the same page — "The miraculous birth stories are part of the muniiqibii" [text-layer?: manāqiba] (p. 222, folio 204). Professional bards: "The madiih (from Arabic madl1" [text-layer?: madḥ], "praise) is usually recited by professional bards who call themselves bhiin" [text-layer?: bhān — vowel not recoverable], "a word which they derive with a fanciful etymology from Biinat Suciid" [text-layer?: Bānat Suʿād], "Kacb ibn Zuhair's" [text-layer?: Kaʿb] "famous qa$fda" [text-layer?: qaṣīda] (p. 222, folio 204).
- Ismaili ginans: "It is also generally used in the ginans of the Ismaili community where the beloved for whom the bridal soul is waiting is the Imam." (p. 173, folio 155)
- Arabic-side popular forms taken into Sufi use: "didactic verse in the simple meter Rajaz", "the strophic muwashsha]J [text-layer?: muwashshaḥ] and zajal, and 4-ot [text-layer?: 4- or] 5-lined forms such as billfq [text-layer?: reading uncertain], dabait, and mawiiliyii [text-layer?: mawāliyā]" (p. 55, folio 45); and the arts of "tash tir and takhmis", "adding two verses of one's own to a hemistich of a well-known poem, or filling up each of its distichs with three new lines" (p. 56, folio 46).
- Convention of the opening: "even the worldly poets usually headed their collections of verse with the epigraph "In the name of God," thus later writers, especially in the Persianate tradition, began every work with hymns to praise the Creator and the beloved Prophet." (p. 22, folio 12)
- The index glosses this whole apparatus as a working vocabulary: `Kafi, singable verses in Sindhi and Panjabi` (p. 369, folio 351); `Madab (Sindhi), "praise poem"` [text-layer?: madaḥ] (p. 370, folio 352); `Mu<jizo, "miracle," ballad, in Sindhi, about the miracles of the Prophet` [text-layer?: muʿjizo] (p. 371, folio 353); `Muniiqib6, Sindhi poems about the` / `Munaqib6 (Continued ) miracles of the Prophet and the saints` (pp. 371–372, folios 353–354); `Chakkinama, popular mystical poems which use the imagery of grinding grain` and `Charkhiniima, popular mystical poems which use the imagery of spinning` (both p. 366, folio 348); `Si{tarfi, Golden Alphabet` (p. 375, folio 357), also entered as `Golden Alphabet, siQm:fi` (p. 368, folio 350); `Doha, two-lined verses in Indian me­ters` (p. 367, folio 349); `Biirahmiisa, Indian poetical genre in which a loving woman expresses her feelings during the twelve months of the year` (p. 366, folio 348); `Ginan, religious poetry of the Ismaili community in Indo-Pakistan` (p. 368, folio 350); `Kang, the crow, messenger bird in Sind and the Panjab` (p. 369, folio 351).
- The language of the urban orders: "The `literahtre`[text-layer?: literature] of the Sufi urban orders was generally composed in Persian, the standard language of `literahtre`[text-layer?: literature] and, from the late fifteenth century, of administration" (p. 65, folio 55).
- Hagiography as a working resource for poets: ʿAttar's prose `Tadhkirat al-auliyti`[text-layer?: Tadhkirat al-auliyā] "furnished innumerable poets in Iran, Turkey, and India with stories about the lives of earlier Sufis, particularly Hallaj" (p. 63, folio 53); Jami "reworked Ansari's hagiographical work, `reti-` / `tled` `Nafa]Jat al-uns`[text-layer?: Nafaḥāt al-uns]" (p. 62, folio 52); and the `Muniijiit`[text-layer?: Munājāt] "are used, though in different redactions, to our day, and its form became a model for many later writings" (p. 61, folio 51).
- A datum on how such poetry comes into being, from the author's own fieldwork: "I myself witnessed the 'birth' (doghush) of such folk poetry in Anatolia in the 195o's [text-layer?: 1950's]" (p. 157, folio 139).
- Even the secular stage carries it: "Verses in his honor are found even in popular, certainly not predominantly religious, Egyptian shadow plays" (p. 225, folio 207).

#### Recitation, baraka, and the prescribed use of poems

- The doctrine: "This firm belief in the baraka, the spiritual power of a poem praising the Prophet, was widely spread" (p. 218, folio 200). Of an eighteenth-century Sindhi poet's "tarji'band" [text-layer?: tarjīʿband] the book says it "was immensely well received by the tongues of old and young, and was a means of help for those who wished to obtain their wants" (p. 218, folio 200).
- Recitation as mass capture, in Bada'uni's words: "by its public recitation human hearts are taken captive" (p. 154, folio 136).
- Poems prescribed for a condition: the `Qaniin-i 'ishq ya'ni }Jalwii-i Panjab` [text-layer?: Qānūn-i ʿishq yaʿnī ḥalwā-i Panjāb] "devotes a long chapter in mixed Persian, Urdu, and Panjabi to `fana' fi 'r-rasiil` [text-layer?: fanāʾ fī ʾr-rasūl], closing with a simple little Persian poem which one should recite 'when one feels restless and har­rassed'" (p. 306, folio 288).
- Recitation in named ritual settings: "Recitations of the Burda in a festive set­ting are still practised in the Deccan" (p. 205, folio 187); Sana'i "improvised" his finest eulogy "in a religious meeting" (p. 208, folio 190); Shibli's verse "is still sung today in religious meetings to praise the Prophet" (p. 195, folio 177).
- A privately convened session, dated: "S. S. Khusro Hussaini, Gulbarga, who arranged a special session of the 'Burda' for me in his house in Hyderabad in October 1979" (p. 301, folio 283).
- Multiplication of a single poem as a devotional act: "in Egypt alone not less than eighty quintuplet versions (takhmfs [text-layer?: takhmīs]) were made out of it, not to men­tion the variations in the non-Arabic countries like India" (p. 205, folio 187); the splitting-and-filling technique is named "(tashfir [text-layer?: tashṭīr])" (p. 205, folio 187).
- Performance continuing into old age: Gesudaraz "even at the age of `9<J`[text-layer?: probably 90] still enjoyed singing of his love" (p. 77, folio 67).

#### The Pir, the khalifa, and succession

- The book states the political function of the poetry outright: "The role of at least one branch of mystical folk poetry in cementing the tremendous influence of the Pir over his followers should never be forgotten" (p. 159, folio 141).
- And its missionary function: "mystical verse in the popular languages was largely composed by members of the fraternities for missionary purposes, and thus a special genre developed which centers around the veneration of the founder of the order or the patron saint." (pp. 178–179, folios 160–161)
- Lineage put into verse and into acclamation: "Sometimes such poems are merely rhymed accounts of the spiritual or family lineage (silsila, shajara) of the Pir, or they contain blessings over the saint and his family, repeating dozens of times in sweet tunes and enrapturing rhythms a formula like as-saltimu calaika ya Bandanawaz [text-layer?: as-salāmu ʿalaika yā Bandanawāz]" (p. 179, folio 161).
- **Spiritual territory — the book's closest approach to a shrine's jurisdiction:** "the Indian Sufi practice of assigning to each saint, and he in turn to his khalzya [text-layer?: khalīfa], a spiritual territory, wiltiyat [text-layer?: wilāyat]" (p. 179, folio 161); the index glosses `Wilayat, territory fo r a spiritual leader` [text-layer?: for] (p. 377, folio 359), a definition only. A Sindhi poem maps such a domain: "The first geographical poem in Sindhi, which enumerates dozens of cities and countries as c Abdul Qadir's domain, was written in the early eighteenth century in his praise ." (p. 179, folio 161) The cities are not listed.
- Formal installation of a successor: "When the dictation was taken up again in 1262, Husamuddin had fully matured and was now officially installed as Rumi's `khalifa`" (p. 103, folio 93); earlier, `ifa`[text-layer?: reading uncertain], "a ray of light from the sun", "was, however, officially given to Husamuddin Chelebi a few years later" (p. 101, folio 91); "According to Aflaki, Sultan Walad mentioned that this poem was recited when Maulana chose Husamuddin as his khalifa" (p. 257, folio 239).
- Succession in a teaching chair: Baha'uddin Walad "was called to teach in one of the numerous madrasas of Konya" in 1228; on his death "his son succeeded him in the chair" (p. 94, folio 84).
- Marriage used to bind discipleship: Shams "was married to a girl from the household" (p. 96, folio 86); "To cement the relationship with the friend, he married his eldest son Sultan Walad to Salahuddin's daughter, and his letters to his beloved daughter-in-law are touching proofs of his humanity" (p. 102, folio 92); a later poem ending in `khiimush`[text-layer?: khāmūsh] is "the wedding song for Salahuddin's second daughter" (p. 107, folio 97).
- Pastoral apparatus: Rumi "wrote a great number of letters which show him as a man deeply concerned with the welfare of those who were close to him" (p. 103, folio 93). "The training of the falcon at the hand of the falconer is a fine image of the education of the novice by the Pir" (p. 86, folio 76).
- A founder's written legacy as a genre: "Sometimes the founder of an order would leave a rhymed waliiya [text-layer?: waṣāya — reading uncertain], an exhortation for his followers" (p. 287, folio 269); the index glosses `Waijiya, testament, last will, exhorta­tion` (p. 377, folio 359).
- **Rank claimed at the top, with the index unable to settle it.** "the founder of a fraternity … claims to be the qutb or ghauth, the highest member of the mystical hierarchy" (p. 47, folio 37); the index gives the superlative to both without reconciling them — `Ghauth, "Help," title of the highest member of the mystical hierarchy` (p. 368, folio 350) and `Qufb, "Pole, axis," the highest member of the mystical hierarchy` [text-layer?: quṭb] (p. 373, folio 355).
- A ṭarīqa organised around Prophet-veneration: "c Abdallah al-Mirghani, in whose fariqa [text-layer?: ṭarīqa] the veneration of the Prophet found its outlet in a large literature of devotion" (p. 294, folio 276).
- Index glosses for the offices: `Khalifa, successor of a mystical leader` (p. 369, folio 351); `Murshid, spiritual guide` (p. 372, folio 354); `Pir` (p. 373, folio 355); `Makhdam, "he who is served," Master` [text-layer?: makhdūm] (p. 371, folio 353); `Silsila, spiritual pedigree` and `Shajara, spiritual lineage` (both p. 375, folio 357).

#### The orders as institutions

- The thirteenth-century build-out, stated as the framework for everything later: "the Sufi orders, which were just beginning to crystallize, carried the message of Divine love into the various lands of Islam so that from Morocco to Transoxania, from Central Anatolia to Bengal an almost uninterrupted chain of mystical centers was established, on the framework of which all later mystical movements as well as literary activities developed" (pp. 45–46, folios 35–36).
- The Shadhiliyya: identified with "the sober trend grounded in the teachings of the school of Baghdad, particularly those of Junaid", and remembered for prose rather than poetry (p. 46, folio 36); Busiri "was an early member of the Shadhiliyya in Egypt" (p. 47, folio 37); Suyuti is named as "a member of the Shadhiliyya order" (p. 243, folio 225).
- The qalandar and the futuwwa: the epithet `cayyiir` [text-layer?: ʿayyār] ("wild," "roguish") suggests "the vocabulary of the `fu tuwwa` [text-layer?: futuwwa] circles with whom Rumi may have had some contact", the related terms being "rind and qalandar" (p. 125, folio 115). "The qalandar, usually a dervish of the be-shar type, i.e., not following all injunctions of the religious law, becomes for Rumi often the symbol for the highest mystical stage. The term is therefore applied at times to Shamsuddin (who, in fact, may have been a member of a qalandar-group)", with `See Franz Taeschner, Ziinfte und Brudersdiaften im Islam [text-layer?: Zünfte und Bruderschaften im Islam], pp. 43-52 for <ayyiir [text-layer?: ʿayyār]; p . 28o for Rumi's relationship with the futuwwa`, and `J. Christoph Burge! [text-layer?: Burgel]` on "the 'cosmic dimensions' of expressions like rind, qalandar, and <ayyiir" in "The Pious Rogue." (p. 268, folio 250)
- **Which orders the book indexes** (relevant to the archive's `silsila` column, though the index attaches none of them to a Pakistani site): `Bektashi (order)` (p. 351, folio 333); `Chishtuyya`[text-layer?: `Chishtiyya`] (p. 352, folio 334); `Firdausiyya order` (p. 353, folio 335); `Heddawa order` (p. 354, folio 336); `Isawiyya order` (p. 356, folio 338); `Kaisaniyya` (p. 357, folio 339); `Mevlevi (order)` (p. 358, folio 340); `Naqshbanduyya`[text-layer?: `Naqshbandiyya`] (p. 359, folio 341); `Rifa' i/yya` [text-layer?: `Rifāʿī/yya`] (p. 360, folio 342); `Shadhililyya`[text-layer?: `Shādhilīyya`] (p. 362, folio 344); `Suhrawardi/yya` (p. 362, folio 344); `Tijaniyya` (p. 363, folio 345).
- **There is no `Qadiriyya` headword anywhere in the index (pp. 349–364)**; the order's eponym is indexed as a person only, `c Abdul Qadir al-Gilani (d. 1 166)`[text-layer?: `ʿAbdul Qādir al-Gīlānī`] (p. 349, folio not stated).
- Index glosses for the institution itself: `Orders, fraternities (tariqa)`, with the sub-entry `urban` (p. 373, folio 355); `Tariqa, the mystical path, order` and `compared to grapes` (p. 376, folio 358); `Qalandar, member of a wandering dervish order` (p. 373, folio 355); `Be-shar<, "without the Law," dervishes who do not conform to the outward forms of Islamic law` [text-layer?: be-sharʿ] (p. 366, folio 348); `Satpanth, the "true Path," branch of the Ismailis` (p. 374, folio 356).
- Bibliography, on the orders as an institution: `Trimingham, J. Spencer . The Sufi Orders in Islam . Oxford: Oxford University Press, 197i .`[text-layer?: year `1971`] (p. 342, folio 324).

#### Pilgrimage

- Hajj read as both act and metaphor: the caravan bell "guides them to the sanctuary, Mecca, where the Beloved dwells", and the pilgrimage "is understood, as it is by many, as a metaphor for the journey into one's own soul" (p. 75, folio 65). "to kiss the black stone during the pilgrimage would remind the lover of kissing the mole on his beloved's cheek" (p. 83, folio 73).
- Iḥrām and circumambulation: a poem "about the sky which turns, like Jalaluddin himself, out of love around the sun, or may be a pilgrim who, in his dark blue il1ram [text-layer?: iḥrām], circumambulates the Kacba [text-layer?: Kaʿba], the House of God" (p. 266, folio 248).
- **A state-funded annual offering to a sanctuary — the one such instance in the book:** "just as the Egyptian government used to send every year the black, gold-embroidered veil for the Kacba [text-layer?: Kaʿba] to Mecca, thus lbn al-Farid has woven his poetical kiswa for the pilgrimage of the soul" (p. 52, folio 42). The index records both the cloth and its transport: `Kiswa, the cover of the Ka< ba` (p. 370, folio 352); `Mabmal, camel litter, particularly the decorated mabmal sent from Egypt to Mecca` (p. 371, folio 353).
- A non-Muslim pilgrimage taken up in Sindhi idiom: "the pilgrims travel to Mt. Hinglaj, which is situated west of Sind, they are said to "go East," i.e., to find illumination." (p. 288, folio 270)
- Interior pilgrimage: "the combination of exercise and interior pilgrimage in the work of Khwaja Farid" (p. 286, folio 268).
- The holy cities inside Indo-Pakistani devotional verse: the "holy geography" of Mecca and Medina "is also found in poetry composed in honor of the Prophet in Indo-Pakistan, in sf]Jarjfs [text-layer?: sīharfīs] and biirahmasa [text-layer?: bārahmāsa]" (p. 205, folio 187).
- Pilgrimage generating a printed devotional commodity: "Booklets like one Mirza Shakur Beg's `Madina kii !?Qdaqa` [text-layer?: Madīna kā ṣadaqa], which contains na'ts written by the author during his pilgrimage and visit to the Prophet's tomb, are frequently found ." (p. 301, folio 283)
- Index headwords for pilgrimage places and apparatus: `Ka< ba`[text-layer?: `Kaʿba`] (p. 356, folio 338); `Mecca` and `Medina` (pp. 358 and 358, folio 340); `Kerbela` (p. 357, folio 339); `l:firii`[text-layer?: `Ḥirā`] (p. 354, folio 336); `$afa, hill`[text-layer?: `Ṣafā, hill`] (p. 361, folio 343); `Pilgrimage … pilgrim` (p. 373, folio 355); `lbriim, garment for the pilgrimage` (p. 368, folio 350); `ljaram in Mecca … ljaramain, the two holy cities Mecca and Medina` [text-layer?: ḥaram] [text-layer?: ḥaramain] (p. 368, folio 350).

#### Devotional objects, amulets, and bodily practice

- The *hilya* as a household object: the descriptions "were later written in fine calligraphy to adorn the houses of the pious, and were in turn again elaborated in poetical form in Arabic, Turkish, and Urdu" (p. 194, folio 176), on the promise "Whosoever sees my l1ilya [text-layer?: ḥilya] after my death will be as if he had seen me, and whosoever sees it, longing for me, God will make Hellfire forbidden for him" (p. 295, folio 277).
- Verses as amulets and as prophylaxis: Burda verses "were believed to cure certain ailments or to protect from plague or poverty, and were therefore used in amulets or written on walls" (p. 204, folio 186).
- Devotional image in modern public space: "Pictures of Buraq are found today on trucks and tank waggons in Pakistan" (p. 201, folio 183); the ascension "inspired marvelous miniatures by the artists of Iran and Central Asia" (p. 201, folio 183).
- Relics as poetic subject: poets "sometimes specialized in praising certain items connected with the Prophet, such as his sandal or his footprint" (p. 206, folio 188).
- Insignia the index treats as markers of community: `Rosary, tasbi/1, sign of the Muslim, hence contrasted with the infidels' girdle` and `Infidels' girdle, zunnilr, badge of the non-Muslim` (pp. 374 and 369, folios 356 and 351); `Millil, Hindu prayer beads` [text-layer?: mālā] (p. 371, folio 353); `c A$ida, sweets distributed at childbirth` [text-layer?: ʿaṣīda] (p. 365, folio not stated).
- Marking the body: "The image of the mark is still very real with Rumi; one should also remember that some Sufis, particularly in India, used to inflict upon themselves branding marks as sign of complete surrender" (p. 269, folio 251).
- Sacred calligraphy as a document practice turned devotional: "The tughrii [text-layer?: tughrā] is the artistically elaborated hand sign of the ruler at the beginning of a document; it found its finest expression in Ottoman Turkey, see Ernst Kuhnel [text-layer?: Kühnel], "Die osmanische Tughra." Nowadays, tughrii is the general term for interlaced patterns made of proper names or pious formulas" (p. 261, folio 243).
- A living informant's devotional cipher, reported first-hand: "In Turkey, the fondness of poets and painters for the tulip was explained to me by mystically minded friends with the re­mark that Iii/a"[text-layer?: lāla], "hi/iii"[text-layer?: hilāl] "('crescent'), and Al/ii/I"[text-layer?: Allāh] "all consisted of the same four letters and had the same numerical value, namely 66, hence the tulip became a kind of religious cipher" (p. 254, folio 236). Relatedly, "The green color of the parrot reminded the Sufi poets of the green robes in Paradise, and his gift for languages earned him the constant epithet 'sugar chewing'" (p. 253, folio 235).
- A proverb attached to a saint's death: "still today the Turkish proverb says doshm attfgf gal onar"[text-layer?: dost attığı gül] "'The rose cast by a friend, hurts.'" (p. 241, folio 223)

#### The publishing trade as devotional infrastructure

- **The Burda as a printed and translated commodity across five centuries.** It "was soon translated into almost every Islamic language... The poem is available in Turkish, Urdu, Pashto, and Panjabi. In the sixteenth century a Malay trans­lation was produced, while several Swahili recensions of more recent date are in use in East Africa. The Burda has often been printed, and fine illuminated manuscripts exist in Cairo and elsewhere" (pp. 204–205, folios 186–187). Its printing history as given: "The `editio princeps` by J. Uri (Leiden 1761) , with Latin translation"; a German translation of 1824; "C. A . Ralfs, Die Burda … (Vienna, 186o)"; "Printed with an English version by Shaikh Faizullah­bhai, A Moslem Present; Part I: `171e` [text-layer?: The] Poem of the Scarf (Bombay, 1893)"; "The most widely used commentary is that by Bajuri, finished in 1814, printed in Cairo `1297'1880` [text-layer?: 1297/1880], with the commentary by Khalid al-Azhari (d. 1500) on the margin." (p. 300, folio 282) An Urdu verse Burda by a named professional: "the Urdu verse translation by the noted `modem` [text-layer?: modern] architect Fayyazaddin Nizami, `Naf/1 al-warda fi sharli al-burda` [text-layer?: Nafḥ al-warda fī sharḥ al-burda], was issued in `HyderabadJAP` [text-layer?: Hyderabad/AP] 1969" (p. 300, folio 282).
- Other transmission histories: the *Fuṣūṣ* was "commented upon and translated until our day (the Fu�ii� [text-layer?: Fuṣūṣ] even into Urdu verse during the eighteenth century !)" (p. 49, folio 39); the first German rhymed *Ta'iyya* was Joseph von Hammer-Purgstall's, "despite the beautiful print in oriental style (Vienna, 1854)" (p. 54, folio 44).
- **The South Asian devotional imprints the bibliography runs on.** "Sindhi Adabi Board" is the single most frequent South Asian imprint, at Karachi (1955, 1956, 1957, 1958, 1959, 1956) and at Hyderabad/Sind (1965, 1967) — pp. 323, 324, 327, 333, 334, 335, 336 (folios 305, 306, 309, 315, 316, 317, 318); it also appears as Hyderabad/Sind 1960–1962 and Karachi 1954, 1957, 1959 (pp. 311–317, folios not stated to 299). "Shah Waliullah Academy", Hyderabad/Sind, 1961 (p. 326, folio 308).
- **A numbered devotional chapbook series, undated by design:** "Allah wale ki qaumi dukan" / "Allahwale ki qaumi dukan", Lahore, its numbers printed as "No. 200", "No. 195", "Nr. 87" and "no. 372", all four undated ("s . d .", "n.d.") (pp. 321, 329, 331, 333; folios 303, 311, 313, 315); also listed as "Allah-wale kr qaumi dukan" (Lahore, n.d.) among the imprints at pp. 311–317.
- Further imprints as printed: "Ashraf", Lahore (1930 "and often", 1952, 1956, 1967) — pp. 321, 325, 326, 341, and (Lahore, n.d. and 1968) at pp. 311–317; "University of the Punjab", Lahore (1955, 1969 ×2) — pp. 321, 326; "Idara-i rnatbii'at-i Pakistan" [text-layer?: Idāra-i maṭbūʿāt-i Pākistān], Karachi, 1953 (p. 325, folio 307); "Mal;ifil-i dhauqiyya" [text-layer?: Maḥfil-i dhauqiyya], Karachi, 1388h/1968 (p. 319, folio 301); "Matbac al-c arab" [text-layer?: Maṭbaʿ al-ʿArab], Karachi, 1956 (p. 318, folio 300); "Educational Publishing Company", Karachi, 1934 (p. 322, folio 304); "Majlis-i ta-raqqi-e Urdu", "Urdu Dunya" and "Minerva Bookshop", Lahore (pp. 318, 331, 340); "Bazme §aqafat" [text-layer?: Bazm-e s̱aqāfat], Multan, c. 1976 (p. 340, folio 322); "Anjuman-i taraqqi-yi Urdu" (Karachi 1953; `A':?amgarh` [text-layer?: Aʿẓamgarh] `1364h'1945` [text-layer?: 1364h/1945]), "Iqbal Academy" (Karachi 1967-1968), "Ayina-i adab" (Lahore 1958), "Hindustan Kitabghar" (Bombay 1958) (pp. 311–317, folios not stated to 299).
- University presses and academies in South Asia: "University of Delhi, 1975" (p. 327), "Aligarh University Press, 1955" (p. 332), "University of the Panjab" at Chandigarh (p. 339), "Dept. of Bengali, University of Dacca, 1 963" (p. 340), "Ghalib Academy" New Delhi (p. 337), "Bharatiya Kala Kandra" New Delhi (p. 331), "Munshiram Manoharlal Publishers" New Delhi (p. 335).
- Manuscript holdings named, useful for provenance: "Berlin, Staatsbibliothek Preussischer Kulturbesitz" and "a manuscript in the Pierpont Morgan Library, New York, M 476, fol. 219 b" (p. 256, folio 238).

#### State patronage, rulers, and official religion

- Patronage converted into relief: Rumi's "good relations with the ministers, who for all practical purposes ruled the remains of the Seljuk empire after several Mongol attacks on Anatolia, enabled him to extend help to many poor and needy" (p. 103, folio 93).
- Vernacular religious verse commissioned and weaponised: Lor Chanda was "written for Feroz Shah Tughluq's vizier around 1370" (p. 154, folio 136); "That religious poetry in the folk idiom could also serve political purposes is evident from the verse of the Safawid ruler Shah Ismacil Khata'i [text-layer?: Shāh Ismāʿīl Khaṭāʾī]", whose poems "aimed at winning over the Turkish tribes in Western Iran and Eastern Anatolia" (p. 156, folio 138).
- Ceremonial musicians as an institution of patronage: "Nauba is a group of musicians who play with fifes and drums during fixed hours at the gates of kings or grandees." (p. 305, folio 287) The index gloss is blunter: `Nauba, military music at the gates of grandees` (p. 372, folio 354).
- **State-sponsored conference and festival publishing, the book's clearest modern institutional material.** "the First Inter-/national Congress on Seerat 1976 . Ministry of Religiou s Affairs . Islamabad: Government of Pakistan, 1976" (p. 330, folio 312); "Proceedings All Pakistan Historical Conference 1 (1951)" and "2 (1952)" (p. 329, folio 311); "the proceedings of the Seerat (sfrat) Conference in Karachi 1975" (p. 292, folio 274); "Government of Pakistan" (Islamabad 1976) (pp. 311–317). Commemorative festivals as publishing occasions: "one of the numerous publications released during the Sana'i-festival in 1977", the volume issued by "Kabul: Ministry of Cul-/ture" (p. 336, folio 318); and "'Die Mevlanafeier in Konya vom 1 1-17. Dezember 196o [text-layer?: 1960].' Oriens 1 5 (1962)" (p. 335, folio 317).
- Political geography behind a devotional revival: "Egypt being the only major country that was spared a Mongol invasion and remained therefore the leading cultural center of the Arab world" (p. 46, folio 36).
- A dated public emergency attached to a poem: a poem naming Husamuddin "was written at the time that the Mongols intended a new attack on Konya; it even bears the date of this event, 5 `Dhu'l-qac da`[text-layer?: Dhū'l-qaʿda] 654 (25 November 1256)" (p. 102, folio 92).

#### Law, the madrasa, and contested practice

- The teaching institution and its syllabus: Baha'uddin Walad taught "in one of the numerous madrasas of Konya" from 1228 (p. 94, folio 84); in India, "Kanz Qu duri Kafiya [text-layer?: Kanz, Qudūrī, Kāfiya], the handbooks of hadith, jurispruqence [text-layer?: jurisprudence], and Arabic grammar, which were taught in Indian madrasas for centuries" (p. 158, folio 140), and of the same three works — the Kanz al-ʿummal, Quduri's Hanafi survey and Ibn Malik's Kafiya — "All these books were taught in Indian madrasas" (p. 279, folio 261).
- Index glosses for the legal-institutional vocabulary: `Madrasa, theological college` (p. 370, folio 352); `Fatwil, legal counsel` (p. 367, folio 349); `Qadi … qiI4i al-qu<jiit, highest judge` (p. 373, folio 355); `Molla, the ritualistic representative of the outward law` (p. 371, folio 353); `Sharf"a, the divine law` (p. 375, folio 357); `Ulema` as a headword, "printed without a diacritic and without the article" (p. 363, folio 345).
- The doctrinal flashpoint over ritual itself: "isqat al-wasa'""it"[text-layer?: isqāṭ al-wasāʾiṭ], "the abrogation of intermediaries: ritual practices are optional for those who have reached union with the Di­vine-a theory which could lead to dangerous consequences, particularly in social ethics, and was therefore objected to by the orthodox" (p. 241, folio 223).
- **A dated juridical trial of a mystical poet:** "That happened in Muharram 875'July 1470" [text-layer?: Muharram 875/July 1470]: "the Hanafite qiicli al-qucliit"[text-layer?: qāḍī al-quḍāt], "Muhibbuddin ibn ash-Shihna, was the leader of the faction which claimed that lbn al-Farid was a heretic or infidel, while the strongest defense of the mystical poet came from Jalaluddin as-Suyuti, the noted traditionist and po­lyhistor, who was a member of the Shadhiliyya order. The ruler, Sultan Qait­bai, who himself had some religious inclinations, sided with the latter group" (p. 243, folio 225), the source given being "Ibn lyiis"[text-layer?: Ibn Iyās], "Bada'i' az-zuhar fi waqa'i' ad-duhar". Earlier, "in the late fifteenth century a fierce fight raged in Cairo between those religious scholars who supported his views and those who regarded him as a heretic" (p. 52, folio 42).
- Censorship by a religious institution, with a date: Ibn ʿArabi's work was "often regarded … as dangerous, if not outright heretic, and its perusal was prohibited once more in February 1979 in Egypt by the authorities of al-Azhar" (p. 49, folio 39). Execution for religious claims in verse: Nesimi's "religious claims, expressed in daring verse in classical style, led to his execution in Aleppo in 1417" (p. 247, folio 229).
- **The contemplation of a human witness, and the discipline around it.** The book describes "an aspect of Sufism that had become more and more popular: to see the Divine beauty reflected in or manifested through a human being, ideally a boy of fourteen", regarded as "the true witness, `shiihid`[text-layer?: shāhid], of Divine beauty" (p. 77, folio 67). Against it: "The veneration of young boys-branded by the orthodox as a kind of manicheism-developed into a highly refined spiritual art, but could also degenerate into a more or less crude homosexuality"; "The sober Sufis were always aware of the danger of 'looking at the unbearded'"; Sana'i "accused the Sufis of his time of having no other qibla but shahid, shame, and shikam (a handsome boy, candles at parties, and their stomachs)"; "The lovers, however, maintained that such a love was licit, provided that the chastity of the glance was preserved" (p. 78, folio 68). And, beside the *Kashf al-maḥjūb* prohibition, "The Hanbalite Ibn Qayyim al-Jauziyya goes even farther. He writes: 'The immoral among mystic lovers commit a more serious sin than either idolators or sodomites . They combine the transgression of the two groups-worshipping their minions and copulating with their idols'" (pp. 250–251, folios 232–233).
- Martyrdom redefined away from the state's holy war: the Sufis "too claimed to die ff sabfl Allah [text-layer?: fī sabīl Allāh], on the path of G od, as martyrs; but that meant not, as in orthodox Islam, during the Holy War against the infidels, but rather in the 'major holy war,' the struggle against their lower selves, in mortifications and by willingly taking upon themselves all afflictions" (p. 40, folio 30).
- Unlicensed devotion at village level: "in the private prayers of contemporary village 'saints' one can still hear extremely daring, if not coarse addresses to God or the Prophet" (p. 182, folio 164).

#### Architecture, ritual space, and building dates

- A mosque with a building date, still in use: "the mosque which he erected in 1220 close to the fortress is still in use" — Sultan `c Ala'uddin`[text-layer?: ʿAlāʾuddīn] Kaikobad's mosque in Konya (p. 94, folio 84).
- A dated hospital-and-mosque complex used as a visual analogy: "Rumi's most rhapsodic poetry sometimes sounds like a verbal replica of the incredibly rich foliage-like ornaments which decorate the hospital and the Great Mosque in Divrigi [text-layer?: Divriği], Anatolia, built in 1228" (p. 267, folio 249). The same note names the "stone lions which serve as fountains" alluded to in `Fi/ii mii fi11i [text-layer?: Fīhi mā fīhi]` (`see Arberry, Discourses, p. 52`) and reliefs of the Tree-of-Life and dragons on "Seljukid architecture", citing `Goniil Oney [text-layer?: Gönül Öney], "Das Lebensbaum-Motiv in der seldschukischen Kunst in Anatolien" and "Dragon Figures in Anatolian Seljuk Art."` (p. 267, folio 249).
- A palace carving: the unicorn-and-elephant legend "is represented in a stone carving on the walls of the palace in Konya" (p. 124, folio 114).
- Secular decorated space as a topos: "Bath houses were often decorated with wall paintings; hence the 'picture in the bath house' became a topos to express something immobile and soulless", citing `Heinz Grotzfeld, Das Bad im arabisch-islamischen Mittela/ter [text-layer?: Mittelalter], pp. 44-5t [text-layer?: pp. 44-51]` (p. 264, folio 246).
- The index's ritual-space vocabulary: `cJdgiih, place where the community gathers for the cfd prayers` [text-layer?: ʿīdgāh] (p. 368, folio 350); `Qibla, prayer direction` and `prayer niche` (p. 373, folio 355); `Tekke, dervish lodge` (p. 376, folio 358); `Madrasa, theological college` (p. 370, folio 352).
- No mosque in the poetry is named or located: "Love is also the Imam, and when this Imam appears, a thousand mosques are filled" (p. 137, folio 119).

#### Women, the household, and illiterate transmission

- The book's strongest institutional claim about women: images are "taken from the world of women, who were even less educated than men but were, and still are in rural areas, the best transmitters of religious poetry, songs, and proverbs, and the depositaries of mystical lore" (p. 163, folio 145).
- The author's own claim of method: "I have learned more about Islamic piety from women in Anatolia and Sind than from most of my learned colleagues or from sophisticated books-they still cling to "the faith of the old women" as it was praised in early Islam" (p. 281, folio 263).
- Transmission by illiterate performers: "illiterate bards are also found, who took up the popular tradition founded by the masters, and helped the ideas of mystical poetry reach into popular literature, to lullabies and wedding songs, riddles and even jokes. But the songs of illiterate dervishes and their styles of recitation have only rarely been studied in detail." (p. 18, folio 8)
- Domestic transmission of Prophet-devotion: "allusions to the Prophet's life and miracles percolated into all levels of society and are found in lullabies and bridal songs, so that love for him is infused into a child's soul and grows as it breathes" (p. 223, folio 205).
- The spinning and grinding genres (charkhī nāma, chakkī nāma) and the graded dhikr taught inside them are the mechanism by which this works — see the genre and dhikr groups above (pp. 163–164, folios 145–146).

#### The shrine economy: an explicit negative

- **The archive's own shrine-economy categories are, with the narrow exceptions listed below, absent from this book.** No ʿurs revenue, no mela, no langar, no offerings-as-income, no endowment, no land, no revenue figure, no visitor number, no property dispute, no Auqaf, no sajjada nashin, and no custodianship-by-family appears anywhere in the sixteen chunks. Two chunks state the negative in their own right: the endnote-and-abbreviations chunk records "**no** material on ʿurs or mela, langar, offerings, custodianship (sajjada nashin, Auqaf, family), endowments, land, revenue, visitor numbers, disputes, state involvement, architecture or building dates" (pp. 217–238, folios 199–220), and the bibliography chunk records that a bibliography "records no urs, mela, langar, offering, endowment, custodianship or building date, and **none appears anywhere in these 24 pages**" (pp. 318–341, folios 300–323). The opening chunk likewise returns `(not stated)` throughout for every one of these categories (pp. 17–33, folios 7–23). This is a book about mystical poetry and its performance; the material economy of the shrine is not one of its subjects, and an entry should not expect to find it here.
- The three things that come nearest, and what each actually is:
  - **Territory, defined but never quantified.** "the Indian Sufi practice of assigning to each saint, and he in turn to his khalzya [text-layer?: khalīfa], a spiritual territory, wiltiyat [text-layer?: wilāyat]" (p. 179, folio 161), and the index gloss `Wilayat, territory fo r a spiritual leader` [text-layer?: for] (p. 377, folio 359) — "the nearest thing in the chunk to a statement about a shrine's material domain, and it is a definition only".
  - **Fiscal and coercive vocabulary that is Rumi's imagery, glossed by the author as period practice, not shrine finance.** "confiscation in the Middle Ages, as is well known, was connected with all kinds of humiliation if not torture" (p. 126, folio 116); Love brands man "like an animal is branded by its owner", and "those who have received such a brand of Love will be exempt of all taxes" (p. 126, folio 116); Love appears as "the judge who extorts taxes", against which "the village of the heart is completely in ruins, how could the poor lover pay taxes?" (pp. 126–127, folios 116–117); "as a true Turkish hero Love is used to `yaghmii` [text-layer?: yaghmā], plundering" (p. 126, folio 116).
  - **Trades and commodities, likewise occupational vocabulary rather than economy.** Love's professions: cupper and physician who bleeds with needles (p. 123, folio 113), shield-maker (p. 125, folio 115), bleacher/washerman who "hits the laundry repeatedly on stones", bottle-maker, carpenter (p. 127, folio 117), ragman who "calls in vain (and Maulana has him speak Turkish): 'Who has got old shoes?'" (p. 127, folio 117), baker and cook (p. 128, folio 118), weaver and "tailor of the lovers" (p. 137, folio 119), sorcerer (p. 137, folio 119); "The Sufis' use of the term `dhauq`, 'tasting,' for the immediate mystical experience has possibly contributed to this imagery" of kitchen and food (p. 128, folio 118). Two material details: "tutmiij [text-layer?: tutmāj] is a thick broth with meat, vegetables, and a kind of noodles" (p. 270, folio 252); "The cheetah, in Persian poetical tradition at least since Sana'i, is supposed to live on cheese, i.e., he is tame and meek and is kept on a meatless diet days before he is used for the hunt" (p. 259, folio 241).
  - **The only economic sub-entries in the index are national, not devotional:** under `Egypt`, `sugar export` and `bread` are printed as sub-entries alongside `ancient`, `Egyptian` and `women in` (p. 352, folio 334).
- What the book *does* supply in place of a shrine economy is a **publishing and conference infrastructure** (the imprints, chapbook series, government congresses and commemorative festivals catalogued above, pp. 292, 300–301, 311–341) and a **performance economy** (professional bards, qawwali groups, nauba musicians, recorded maulids — pp. 179, 189, 199, 222, 305). Those are the economic facts an entry can actually cite from this book.

Transcribed exactly as printed, one line per entry; damaged sigla and words flagged. Nothing here is supplied from general knowledge — each expansion is the expansion the page itself prints.

**Mis-keyable sigla — flagged, and one of them dangerous.**

Nothing on either page is wholly illegible. The Pass-1 note reads: "the five sigla flagged above (`AR W`, `El`, `El2`, `]AOS`, `]RAS`, `5`) are the entries a reader could mis-key, and `5` for `S` is the one that would silently corrupt a citation, since `S` is used throughout the notes for Brockelmann's supplements (e.g. `GAL I 76, S I 1 1w`, p. 235, folio 217)."

- **`5` printed for `S` is the dangerous one.** `S` is this book's siglum for Brockelmann's supplements throughout the notes — the `GAL` entry itself defines it as "plus S, three supplements to the first edition" (p. 231) — so mis-keying `S` as `5` produces a citation that looks plausible and is wrong. Check every supplement reference against p. 231 and the sample at p. 235 (folio 217) before it enters an entry.
- The remaining flagged sigla (`AR W` for `ARW`, `El` for `EI`, `El2` for `EI2`, `]AOS` for `JAOS`, `]RAS` for `JRAS`) are mis-keyable but self-evidently so: the wrong reading is not a valid siglum in this book.
- **Discrepancy in the Pass-1 note, reproduced rather than corrected:** the note says "**five** sigla" and then lists **six** (`AR W`, `El`, `El2`, `]AOS`, `]RAS`, `5`). Both the count and the list are given here as written; a human should decide which is intended. The likely reading is that the count is wrong and the list right, but that is not settled here.

### Arguments and interpretations

#### The book's governing theses and its declared exclusions (Introduction)

- **The vernacular thesis — the book's most archive-relevant claim.** "While Western scholars devoted
  much time to the study of mystical poetry written in Arabic and even more to that in Persian, and to
  a lesser extent that in Turkish, the mystical poetry in the regional languages has largely been
  neglected." (p. 18, folio 8). Schimmel refuses the term "folk poetry" "because as much as this
  literature is meant for the masses, many of its authors were literate people, who were proud of
  their acquaintance with Islamic learning and sometimes liked to show off" (p. 18, folio 8). The
  mechanism she proposes is pastoral: each such mystic "knew that the nonurban Muslims needed
  religious instruction in an idiom which they could easily understand, and in images with which they
  could identify. It is largely thanks to them that languages like Panjabi, Sindhi, Pashto, etc. grew
  into vehicles for poetry and literature." (p. 18, folio 8). The result is uniform but not
  placeless: "popular poetry all over Islamdom is very similar in its character, but it still
  preserves the local peculiarities which reflect the landscape out of which it grew" (pp. 18–19,
  folios 8 and 9).
- **Naʿt poetry is the key to Muslim piety, and the reason the book ends where it does.** "To
  understand Muslim piety in full, one has to take into account the poetry written in honor of the
  Prophet, for much of the 'mystical' poetry composed in all Islamic languages bears strong
  similarities to mystical poetry in other religious traditions; but in the trusting and dedicated
  songs and hymns written in honor of the final Prophet … Islamic literature has gained a very
  distinctive flavor." (p. 20, folio 10).
