- **Eros- versus nomos-oriented religion, following Joseph N. Bell.** The love-feeling of mystical
  poetry is "typical of the eros-oriented type of religion, which basically contrasts with the
  nomos-oriented core of Islam" (p. 19, folio 9); "Logically, the nomos-oriented attitude becomes most
  conspicuous in poetry addressed to the Prophet" (p. 19, folio 9). She grounds the distinction in the
  shahada: it is "the second half of the profession of faith, i.e., 'Muhammad is the messenger of
  God,' which makes Islam a distinct religion" (p. 19, folio 9), restated later as the half "which
  determines Islam as a distinct religion and was understood to point to the legal aspect of Islam,
  while the first half, 'There is no deity save God,' could be accepted by most believers in the
  various religious traditions." (p. 303, folio 285).
- **Declared exclusions** (what the book cannot be cited for): Nasir-i Khusrau's philosophical poetry,
  the `marthiya` complex, and Hurufi poetry are all named and set aside (p. 15, folio 5).
- **The historiographical opening thesis.** "THE MYSTICAL POETRY of the Islamic countries,
  particularly Iran, has attracted European scholars for some 200 years; it was, in fact, the first
  source for their acquaintance with the spiritual aspects of Islam." (p. 11, folio not stated), and
  "The mystical aspects of Islam understandably appealed more to non-Muslim readers than did the legal
  and dogmatic aspects of Islam" (pp. 11–12, folio not stated at p. 11; folio 2 at p. 12).
- **Three named hostile precursors.** Friedrich A. D. Tholuck's thesis of 1821, *Ssufismus sive
  theosophia persarum pantheistica*, "paved the way for the constantly repeated misunderstanding that
  Sufism was nothing but pantheism" (p. 11, folio not stated); Trumpp in "1867" on the `Risiil6`
  [text-layer?: Risālō] (p. 11, folio not stated); and René Basset on Būṣīrī's *Burda*, of a
  "Soufisme qui commen�ait" [text-layer?: commençait] "des lors a exercer son influence si nefaste"
  [text-layer?: néfaste] "sur la poesie" [text-layer?: poésie] "orientale" (p. 11, folio not stated).
  Her diagnosis of the cause recurs in the notes: "It is due to this kind of poetry, which became
  known in the West prior to other Islamic works, that most Western scholars regarded Sufism as sheer
  pantheism, as is evident from the very title of Tholuck's pi­oneering thesis of 1821" (p. 249,
  folio 231).
- **A short canon of the scholarship she works from.** Reynold Alleyne Nicholson for his "excellent
  editions of classical Sufi works in both Arabic and Persian"; Louis Massignon, "though often
  difficult to read"; and "Among the German-speaking pioneers in the field of Islamic mystical poetry
  the name of Hellrnut Ritter" [text-layer?: Hellmut Ritter] "outshines all others" (p. 12, folio 2);
  also the Scottish clergyman William Hastie, who took Rumi as "an antidote to Omar Khayyam's
  frivolous verses", and "Riickert's" [text-layer?: Rückert's] "free but congenial translations"
  (p. 12, folio 2).
- **A theory of mystical language, built from outside Islamic studies.** She endorses "Evelyn
  Underhill's Mystidsm" [text-layer?: Mysticism] as "still outstanding", for whom "mysticism is a
  practical life process, in which the One is the object of love" (pp. 12–13, folios 2 and 3); she
  holds that Karsten Harries's claim — "all metaphor that is more than an abbreviation for more proper
  speech gestures toward what transcends languages; thus metaphor implies lack" — "says in scientific
  terms what Rumi meant" (p. 13, folio 3); she cites Hermann Pongs on "metaphor" versus "symbol" and
  "Bild" (p. 13, folio 3), and glosses "Einbildung" as "coined by the medieval German mystics to point
  to the act of forming images of (imagining) the Divine in the mystic's mind" (p. 14, folio 4). The
  Christian comparison is deliberate: the medieval Christian mystic found his images "in 'God's two
  books, in Nature and Scripture,'" and Alan de Lille's verse "can be applied to a large part of
  medieval Islamic mystical poetry as well" (p. 14, folio 4); Hans Heinrich Schaeder wrote "the best
  description of this lyrical poetry" (p. 14, folio 4).
- **Periodisation and geography stated at the outset.** "Islamic mystical poetry is, at least during
  the first seven centuries, many-faceted. It begins with the simple ascetic songs of the early
  mystics and soon develops into chaste and lucid verses" (p. 14, folio 4), "finally reaching full
  bloom in the grand odes of Ibn al-Farid" (p. 15, folio 5); in Iran the forms "grew first in the
  eastern fringe of the country, present-day Afghanistan, and later in the central south, in Shiraz,
  with its influence soon extending to India and later to Turkey" (p. 15, folio 5).
- **Why Rumi is singled out, and why he is not quite "classical."** He is treated at length "because
  of his tremendous influence on most writers in subsequent centuries" (p. 15, folio 5). Then her own
  judgement: "I feel that he does not belong completely to the 'classical' Persian tradition" (p. 16,
  folio 6), because "Rumi's ghazals usually constitute a unity" where "the ideal ghazal … consists of
  verses which follow each other like pearls on a string without needing a 'logical' sequence"
  (pp. 16–17, folios 6 and 7), and because "Rumi's vocabulary is taken from all walks of life and he
  does not shun crude or obscene words" (p. 17, folio 7). Conclusion: "Thus, Rumi … belongs, in a
  certain way, more to the popular than to the 'high' tradition." (p. 17, folio 7) — a reading she
  attributes in part to "The noted Turkish scholar Abdulbaki Golpi'.narli" [text-layer?: Abdülbaki
  Gölpınarlı], "who is certainly one of the greatest experts in the Mevlevi tradition" (p. 17,
  folio 7). She also compares Rumi to "Beethoven's music" and cites "Dr. Samuel Johnson's statement
  comparing Shakespeare to other, truly 'classical' poets" (p. 16, folio 6). Her caution on dating:
  "Although nobody has ever attempted to establish a chronology of his more than 40,000 lyrical
  verses, one can perhaps draw a few guidelines" (p. 15, folio 5).

#### Poetry under suspicion: the Koranic verdict, secrecy, and the limits of language

- **Chapter One's thesis: the Koran condemns poets, and the Sufis had to answer for it.** "THE LAST
  VERSES of Sura 26 (v. 224 ff.) in the Koran contain a sharp criticism of the poets 'who wander
  distracted in every valley and say what they do not do.'" (p. 21, folio not stated), supported by
  "alleged traditions" condemning poetry as "nafth ash-shaitan, 'what Satan has spit out'" (p. 21,
  folio not stated). Schimmel reads the prohibition as aimed at "poetry in praise of sensual love" and
  of wine, and carries the argument straight into South Asia: "The utterances of Indo-Muslim reformers
  of the late nineteenth century against flirtatious or dreamy Urdu poetry echo the Koranic and
  Prophetic aversion to 'worldly' poetry" (p. 21, folio not stated). The counter-moves she catalogues:
  Rumi's own disavowal, the inspiration defence, the "IJadfth" [text-layer?: ḥadīth] "inna min
  ash-shier '1ikma" [text-layer?: inna min ash-shiʿr ḥikma] "('There is wisdom in poetry')", and the
  maxim "the best poetry is that which is most untrue" (p. 22, folio 12). Unease persisted:
  biographers wrote "the learned scholar condescended to committing to paper some verse, which is
  beneath his dignity." (p. 23, folio 13). Goethe's *Noten und Abhandlungen* is her image for the same
  tension: poet and prophet are "both obsessed by the same spirit, but while the poet squanders his
  talents in multifaceted, colorful verse, the prophet concentrates on the one truth" (p. 23,
  folio 13).
- **The constitutive problem: the mystic is solitary, the poet is sociable.** "The mystic is basically
  a solitary wayfarer" who calls not "O my Lord!" but "O my Beloved!" (p. 26, folio 16); "The poet,
  however, is a sociable person, 'wandering in every valley'; he is the mouthpiece of the society"
  (p. 27, folio 17). She poses it as "How then could a Sufi be a poet?" (p. 27, folio 17) and resolves
  it through the Sufis' wish "to attract people" and through samāʿ.
- **Concealment as a historical cause of style, and then as doctrine.** Junaid's cryptic manner is
  explained by government suspicion of the Sufis "around 900" (p. 32, folio 22); "The Sufi poets knew
  that they had to hide their feelings, but that is impossible" (p. 37, folio 27), and the unnamed
  beloved becomes "the model for later love lyrics in which the name of the beloved is never revealed"
  (p. 37, folio 27; cf. "It is an old Sufi rule not to reveal the name of the Beloved", p. 96,
  folio 86). By the Persian chapter it is doctrine: "the mystical poets knew that it is both
  impossible and illicit to express in plain words their experience" (p. 82, folio 72), and "the
  greatest sin of the lover is `ifshii' as-sirr`[text-layer?: ifshāʾ as-sirr],
  `d.ivulgence`[text-layer?: divulgence] of the secret" (p. 83, folio 73).
- **Contradiction kept: why Hallaj was executed.** Schimmel reports, and marks as the poetic
  tradition's view rather than her own, that divulging the secret "was at least according to the
  poetical tradition, the reason for Hallaj's execution. Persian poets have therefore woven a veil of
  symbols in order to point to and at the same time hide the secret" (p. 83, folio 73). Against this
  she states as fact: "Massignon has proved that it was not so much his famous sentence
  anii'l-l]aqq [text-layer?: anā'l-ḥaqq], 'I am the Absolute Truth,' which brought about his
  execution, but rather some of his religious theories" (p. 40, folio 30).
- **Secrecy as a structural cause of obscurity.** "Secrecy is an integral part of mysticism, and as the
  number of followers of a Sufi order grew greater, the greater was the danger of vulgarizing the
  lofty truths" (p. 180, folio 162); hence "cryptic languages, like the mystical Suryani", the
  "balabailiin language, or lisiin al-mu/Jyf [text-layer?: lisān al-muḥyī] from the sixteenth
  century", and a "'mystical kitchen vocabulary,' discussed by Hellmut Ritter" (p. 180, folio 162).
  She also proposes "intellectual shock therapy ... as is the case with the koan in Zen Buddhism"
  (pp. 180–181, folios 162–163). On the same ground mystics tend "either to resort to silence or,
  since complete silence would be destructive, to tum" [text-layer?: turn] "to oxymora and paradoxes"
  (p. 236, folio 218), and Ibn ʿAṭāʾ, asked why the Sufis "have invented expressions which sound
  strange to those who hear them", "answered that they did not want to acquaint everyone with things
  which are precious and dear to them" (p. 237, folio 219).
- **Her own linguistic argument, extended to the whole Islamic world.** "every word has a
  �iihir [text-layer?: ẓāhir] and a batin [text-layer?: bāṭin], an external and an interior sense,
  this holds true for mystical poetry in the whole Islamic world"; Arnold Steiger's simile of "a lyre
  in which the movement of one string makes all the others vibrate"; hence the vocabulary "can
  therefore barely be adequately translated into any other language" (p. 33, folio 23). She also
  endorses Temple Gairdner's question "Do we not take their language too seriously?" and notes that
  "'wilful paradox' plays an important role in this type of Arabic mystical poetry as it does in later
  Sufi writing" (p. 32, folio 22).
- **Readings of mystical language she reports rather than adopts.** Adonis calls early Arabic mystical
  poetry "surrealism before surrealism" and "stresses the surrealistic character of early Sufi
  literature" (pp. 32–33, folios 22–23; p. 181, folio 163); Massignon speaks of the "sapientele plus
  que philosophique [text-layer?: sapientiale plus que philosophique] quality of the Arabic language"
  and of "Taqmin, 'inclusion,'" and Paul Nwyia "has shown how Massignon himself translated Hallaj's
  line ya sirra sirri [text-layer?: yā sirra sirrī] … in three different ways, each of which can be
  defended" (pp. 32–33, folios 22–23); Henry Corbin supplies "paradoxes" for the shat'1iyat
  [text-layer?: shaṭḥiyāt] (p. 181, folio 163) and, quoted in full and with approval, the symbol as
  "le moyen, et le seul moyen, de penetration dans !'invisible"[text-layer?: l'invisible]
  (pp. 252–253, folios 234–235); Hellmut Ritter supplies the "Muslim mystics' strife with God"
  (p. 182, folio 164); D. T. Suzuki language becoming "warped" (p. 184, folio 166); Charlotte
  Vaudeville, Kabir's "ultabha11si [text-layer?: ultabhānsī] ('inverted') images" (pp. 182–183,
  folios 164–165).

#### The Arabic beginnings

- **Arabic religious poetry in classical forms was an Indian as much as an Arab activity — and the
  field overlooks it.** Such poetry "was not restricted to the Arab countries proper; Muslim India
  produced an enormous output of religious poetry in classical forms, faithful to the traditional
  poetical rules", the competition continuing "even in the eighteenth century" (p. 57, folio 47); and
  "The wealth of Arabic literature in classical forms that existed in the Indo-Pakistan Subcontinent
  is often overlooked in histories of Arabic litera­ture" (pp. 244–245, folios 226–227).
- **Why Arabic mystical poetry started late and why prose outran it.** "in the Arabic tradition proper
  religious prose literature is on the whole more expressive than most religious poetry", the
  masterpieces being "Hallaj' s Kitiib af-tawiisin" [text-layer?: Kitāb aṭ-Ṭawāsīn], "the Nahj
  al-balagha, and Ibn c Ata'ullah's Ijikam" [text-layer?: Ibn ʿAṭāʾullāh's Ḥikam] (p. 23, folio 13);
  "Louis Massignon has correctly underlined the central place of the icjiiz qur'iinique"
  [text-layer?: iʿjāz qurʾānique] (p. 23, folio 13). Her causal claim: the Koran's "linguistic
  unsurpassability" together with "its verdict against poets" and "the Arabs' pride in the great
  heritage of pre-Islamic poetry which slowed down the development of Islamic Arabic poetry in the
  full sense of the word" (p. 24, folio 14).
- **From asceticism to love mysticism, dated to c. 800.** "around the year 800. This was the period
  when early Islamic asceticism, with its austere and world-detesting outlook, began to turn into love
  mysticism", led by "Jae far as-Sadiq" [text-layer?: Jaʿfar aṣ-Ṣādiq] and "Rabic a al-c Adawiyya"
  [text-layer?: Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya] (p. 25, folio 15). Early Sufism was "Persiinlichkeitsmystik"
  [text-layer?: Persönlichkeitsmystik], "a personal relation between God and man", and "voluntaristic
  mysticism, aiming at the complete unification of man's will with the Divine will, not yet a gnostic
  approach as it became later" (p. 25, folio 15).
- **The Koran is the matrix of the mystical vocabulary.** Citing Père Nwyia, a complete "qoranisation
  de la memoire" [text-layer?: mémoire] was achieved (p. 25, folio 15); "The experience of the love
  that exists between God and man was found expressed in Sura 5/59: 'He loves them and they love Him';
  and slowly, 'the language of experience became,' as Nwyia says, 'the language of love.'" (p. 26,
  folio 16); the Moses episode "formed the first step to the Sufi theory of the shathiyat, the
  theopathic locutions" (p. 26, folio 16).
- **A three-part typology of early Arabic verse whose third term is the hinge of the whole book.**
  "descriptive poems, in which the Sufis tried to tell of their experiences and their mystical
  'states' (ahwtil [text-layer?: aḥwāl]); technical poems with often complicated word plays, puns, and
  allusions; and popular poems which, as will become clear, often prefigure the mystical poetry in the
  non-Arabic areas." (p. 28, folio 18); restated as "A third genre of early Sufi poetry is more easily
  accessible; that is what I would call the popular style" (p. 33, folio 23).
- **Form history and minor genres.** The Sufis adapted the short Abbasid city genres, not the qaṣīda:
  "shorter love poems and wine poems and especially the art of descriptive poetry (was.f
  [text-layer?: waṣf]) became fashionable in the cities of Iraq, Syria, and Egypt. It was this kind of
  poetry which the early Sufis adapted to their own purposes; as far as we can see the classical form
  of the qas.fda [text-layer?: qaṣīda] with its traditional framework was used by them only much
  later." (p. 29, folio 19). The `dalii'il` [text-layer?: dalāʾil] genre versifies "the signs by which
  the true mystical lover can be recognized" (p. 30, folio 20) from a prose model "sometimes regarded
  as a hadith or even a hadith qudsi" (p. 30, folio 20) — and her verdict is that the prose is "in
  much more poetical words than in most of these rhyming exercises" (p. 30, folio 20). A further genre
  played on the three radical consonants "to attain a highly complicated web of words which could be
  disentangled only by the initiated", so that "the experience of the central concept of Islam, that
  of taulJid [text-layer?: tauḥīd], was hidden under verbal play" (p. 31, folio 21).
- **Candid aesthetic verdicts of her own.** Of Rabiʿa: "Such lines cannot be called great poetry-the
  author's feeling is stronger than her art." (p. 28, folio 18); of the Cameldriver's Song: "This
  verse is certainly not great poetry" (p. 33, folio 23); of ad-Darimi's punning poem, it "belongs
  also to this little attractive genre of mystical verse" (p. 242, folio 224).
- **Hallaj: her strongest positive claims in the book.** "Indeed, Hallaj was the first to discover that
  the mystery of creation lies in the dynamic love which is the very essence of God. He was by no
  means a pantheist, as his teaching was interpreted later; rather, he maintained the Divine
  transcendence" (pp. 40–41, folios 30–31); Massignon is quoted that his poetical works are
  "conversations of his spirit with the Divine spirit about their common love" and that "There is no
  mystical poetry which is at the same time more ardent and more radically dematerialized than that of
  Hallaj" (p. 41, folio 31). **Against the later reception, including in South Asia:** the waḥdat
  al-wujūd reading of Hallaj is "an interpretation which is historically wrong but formed his image in
  the tradition of Turkish, Persian, and Indo-Muslim singers" (p. 176, folio 158); and "the idea that
  'Hallaj was a Brahmanical thinker of purest water' was popularized in Europe particularly by Max
  Horten, Indische Stramungen [text-layer?: Strömungen] in der islamischen Mystik" (p. 286,
  folio 268), against which she quotes Schaeder's article as "a diatribe against Max Horten's
  interpretation of Sufism as something exclusively Indian" (p. 237, folio 219). Tholuck's remarks on
  Hallaj are "rather misleading and invective" (p. 240, folio 222).
- **Periodisation of Arabic mystical poetry.** "With Hallaj, Sufi history and, in a certain way, Sufi
  poetry reached its first climax"; "in the following centuries we rarely find any great Sufi poetry
  in Arabic; rather, handbooks of Sufism were composed which constitute our main sources for early
  poetry"; "Poetry on a higher level appears again in the thirteenth century" (p. 45, folio 35); von
  Grunebaum is cited seeing there "a growing form of self-expression when the creative impulse in the
  other branches of Arabic literature had long been fading" (p. 46, folio 36). Separately, "Between
  900 and 1200 one witnesses a certain dearth of outstanding religious poetry in the Arabic world
  although im­portant theological works were composed" (p. 196, folio 178).
- **The generalisation that licenses the rest of the book.** ʿAli Safi Husain's classification of
  "practical" against "theoretical" Sufis, by ghazal style, by "the theories of the IJaqiqa
  mulJammadiyya [text-layer?: ḥaqīqa muḥammadiyya] and the Muhammadan Light (nur Muhammad)", and by
  Sunnite sobriety, is reported (pp. 46–47, folios 36–37); Schimmel then generalises from Egypt in her
  own voice: the *ḥaqīqa muḥammadiyya* poetry "is, in fact, typical not only of Egyptian or Arabic
  poetry but of virtually all poetry composed in the Sufi orders" (p. 47, folio 37), and "not only the
  contents but even formal characteristics of mystical folk poetry outside the Arabic world are
  preformed in thirteenth-century Arabic verse" (p. 47, folio 37).
- **Against reading Ibn ʿArabi as a pantheist, and against his influence.** "Ibn c Arabi's
  system-generally called wahdat al-wujud, 'Unity of Being' -is much more complicated and should not
  be interpreted as pure pantheism"; "the creatures in their actual existence are not identical with
  God but are solely the reflection of His attributes" (p. 48, folio 38); Corbin and Nasr are reported
  as having "interpreted Ibn c Arabi's theories of 'the Pathetic God Who yearned to be known'" and
  "the mystery of the unio sympathetica between the Divine Names and the named things" (p. 48,
  folio 38). Her own contrast: "there is a world of difference between his approach to religion and
  the dynamic, personal religion of Hallaj. With Ibn c Arabi, Islamic mysticism comes close to the
  mysticism of infinity, and his approach is theosophical or gnostic rather than voluntaristic … while
  in early Sufism the element of personal love between man and God was predominant" (p. 48, folio 38).
  The famous "My heart is capable of every form" poem "seems to express the 'tolerance' of the mystic
  in the most poetical words", but "The poem is, however, a glowing tribute to Islam", with Nicholson
  quoted that the station of perfect love is "a peculiar prerogative of Moslems" (pp. 48–49,
  folios 38–39).
- **Allegorical commentary as a survival strategy, with a South Asian consequence.** The Ibn ʿArabi
  method of interpretation "became a vehicle that carried the teachings of the Ibn c Arabi-school into
  every corner of the Muslim world, because it enabled poets to give even the most worldly poem a
  'spiritual' interpretation, and thus avoid the wrath of the orthodox" (p. 51, folio 41); she floats
  a genealogy for one motif: "one wonders whether the constant interpretation of barq, 'lightning,' …
  has not caused the extremely frequent use of the motif of lightning in Indo-Persian poetry" (p. 51,
  folio 41).
- **Ibn al-Farid, judged with the authorities kept in disagreement.** Massignon saw in the *Ta'iyya*
  "a lourde tapisserie de brocade mordante, a kiswa for the spiritual journey" reaching
  "transcendental esthetics"; Nicholson that "His style and diction resemble the choicest and finest
  jewel work of a fastidious artist rather than the first fruit of divine inspiration" (p. 52,
  folio 42); Nicholson also "rightly points out that the pantheistic trends which aroused the anger of
  the theologians are expressions of a state of feeling rather than a system of thought" (p. 54,
  folio 44). Nicholson "feels that 'the book is too abstract and remote from common experience to give
  pleasure to others'", and this "is gently but firmly contradicted by Martin Lings in his brief
  introduction to the new edition of the translation" (pp. 49–50, folios 39–40). Schimmel's own
  verdict: the odes are, "along with the poems of Hallaj, the only truly great mystical poetry in
  Arabic" (p. 55, folio 45); but set beside Rumi's Zulaikha monologue one can "feel the difference in
  poetical depth and warmth" (p. 50, folio 40).
- **Decline, and a modern political recovery she rates highly.** "While true inspiration seems to be
  lacking in most of the post-thirteenth-century mystical poetry, the Sufis indulged in writing
  extensions of and variations on classical poems" (p. 56, folio 46). Of post-1945 Arabic poets: they
  "by no means stand in the current of orthodox Islam but have tried to find a new religious base in
  the mystical tradition of their religion"; "The recourse of these poets, some of whom politically
  are leftists, to the Sufi tradition seems to me an important development even though it may not be
  more than a passing phase"; "We have here a first attempt to create a truly mythical poetry in
  Arabic, but also an example of sociopolitical criticism" (p. 57, folio 47); and it "shows that there
  is still a possibility of rescuing mystical poetry from the fetters in which it was kept more or
  less from the `tum`[text-layer?: turn] of the fourteenth century" (p. 58, folio 48).

#### Persian: forms, imagery, and how to read them

- **India outproduced Iran in Persian, and Urdu mystical poetry is derivative and thin.** "India too
  contributed to this `literatur�in`[text-layer?: literature—in] fact, the Subcontinent produced more
  Persian works than Iran proper" (p. 65, folio 55); the Indian Style, `sabk-i hindf`[text-layer?:
  sabk-i hindī], developed by poets "many of whom were immigrants from Safawid Iran", is "complicated
  and in the end almost incomprehensible" yet "has moments of extreme beauty" (p. 65, folio 55). And:
  "Poetry in Urdu-in[text-layer?: Urdu—in] which only `Mir Oard`[text-layer?: Mir Dard] in eighteenth
  `cenhtry`[text-layer?: century] Delhi composed truly mystical `vers�is`[text-layer?: verse—is]
  unthinkable without the Persian literary tradition" (p. 65, folio 55). One genre ran the other way:
  of the calendar poem, "In high Persian literature, the genre seems to have been used only in the
  Subcontinent" (p. 282, folio 264).
- **Persian mystical poetry rose as Arabic declined, in the east.** "Mystical ideas and feelings had
  been alive in Iran from the early days of Islam; eastern Iran, Khorasan, and present-day Afghanistan
  were the homes of the first ascetic movements in Islam, which perhaps were influenced in some way by
  Buddhism, whose old center was Bactria" (p. 61, folio 51) — the Buddhist influence given only as
  "perhaps". She rejects a standard attribution: "even though we can no longer accept the traditional
  view that the first Persian mystical quatrains were composed by `Abu Sac id-i
  Abu'l-Khair`[text-layer?: Abū Saʿīd-i Abu'l-Khair], the saint of Maihana (d. 1049) it is a fact that
  mystical literature in Persian developed first in the Eastern Lands of the Caliphate" (p. 61,
  folio 51).
- **The mathnawi is a Persian resource, not an Arabic one.** "The form of `mathnawf`[text-layer?:
  mathnawī], rhyming couplets, was unknown in Arabic writing circles", and "allows the poet to extend
  his thoughts and tales as long as he deems it necessary-from a couple of hundred verses to tens of
  thousands of lines" (p. 62, folio 52). The metre of ʿAttar's most famous epics "was taken over by
  Rumi for his great Mathnawi and subsequently became used in most mystical and didactic poems in the
  Persian and Persianate tradition" (p. 63, folio 53).
- **Judgements on the Persian canon.** Sana'i's `ljadfqat al-haqfqat`[text-layer?: Ḥadīqat
  al-ḥaqīqat] "has been considered by orientalists and even by modern Orientals a rather boring piece
  of literature", its language "not exactly 'poetic,' but rather earthbound and at times outright
  vulgar" (p. 62, folio 52), though "Sana'i's poetic skills are much more conspicuous in his lyrics
  and his panegyrics on the Prophet, a genre which he seems to have introduced into Persian literature"
  (p. 63, folio 53). ʿAttar's `Ushturntima`[text-layer?: Ushturnāma] "shows already some
  disintegration of style; ecstasy, it seems, overwhelms him often in his later compositions" (p. 63,
  folio 53). "in Jami's verse a stupendous rhetorical skill often hides the poet's real concern"
  (p. 65, folio 55). The change in Sufi thought "between the late eleventh and the late fifteenth
  centuries can be understood best by comparing Ansari's Munajat with … Molla Jami" in the same city
  of Herat: "the difference between the heartbeat of Ansari's warm orisons and Jami's more cerebral
  `Lawa'ih` is very telling" (p. 62, folio 52). On attribution she is sceptical where the tradition is
  not: of Auhaduddin Kirmani's quatrains, "it is not always certain whether the quatrains ascribed to
  him are really his" (p. 251, folio 233).
- **Ruzbihan-i Baqli is the key to reading the whole tradition, and ʿAinulqudat the key to India.**
  "his theories form the basis for our understanding of most Persian mystical poetry" (pp. 64–65,
  folios 54–55); Ahmad Ghazzali refined the language "to almost silken smoothness and transparency"
  (p. 64, folio 54); and ʿAinulqudat's work "became, from the early fourteenth century onward, one of
  the important stimulants of Indo-Muslim mystical literature" (p. 64, folio 54).
- **Form does interpretive work, and rhyme drives doctrine.** The monorhyme "like arabesque decorations
  or the pattern of a carpet, seems to be able to continue beyond the physical boundaries of the
  design", and "the rhyme determines, to a certain extent, the poet's choice of words and figures of
  speech and thus provides the poem with a basic unity" (p. 60, folio 50). Concretely: the rhyme of
  `mard` ("man") with `dard` ("pain") "was helpful" to the assertion "that love is only for the
  strong" (p. 82, folio 72); "It may be that the easy rhyme `gul`, ('rose') with `bulbul`
  ('nightingale') has contributed to the frequent use of this image" (p. 86, folio 76); and because
  "treasures are always found in ruins, the heart has first to be completely destroyed before the
  hidden treasure can be found", helped by "the rhyme of ganj (treasure) with ranj (pain)" (p. 141,
  folio 123). Genre is matched to theology: in a heroic epic God "would be called predominantly by
  names of wrath, like `al-qahhar` … while in romantic epics He would appear … as `al-latif`
  [text-layer?: al-laṭīf] (The Tender), or `al-wadud`[text-layer?: al-wadūd] (The Loving)" (p. 66,
  folio 56). The `qatiida`[text-layer?: qaṣīda] "was taken over without major change from the Arabs"
  (p. 66, folio 56); the ghazal "probably developed out of the erotic introduction of the
  `qa�ida`[text-layer?: qaṣīda]" (p. 67, folio 57).
- **Two rhetorical figures carry the mystical content.** `muriicat an-n�fr`[text-layer?: murāʿāt
  an-naẓīr] "requires that the images in a verse stand in a well-defined relation to each other. When
  the rose is mentioned, we can definitely expect a nightingale"; `husn-i taclfl`[text-layer?: ḥusn-i
  taʿlīl], "the fantastic etiology, allows the poet to make everything alive" (pp. 68–69,
  folios 58–59), and "was an ideal vehicle to make this poetical cosmos come alive" (p. 88, folio 78),
  a cosmos in which "everything in the world is in some mysterious way connected with Love"
  (pp. 87–88, folios 77–78). She reads the result as a visual analogue: "as in a perfect classical
  Persian miniature, everything has its place so that … one may speak of a two-dimensional system of
  signs that have equal, or near-equal, value" (p. 68, folio 58); "This poetry is like Persian
  miniature painting, which combines an inspirational quality with the minutest detail work" (p. 88,
  folio 78).
- **Ambiguity is the defining virtue, and is intended.** "The constant oscillation between the two
  levels of experience often makes it next to impossible to translate or even to understand a poem
  correctly. It is this ambiguity between the human and the superhuman levels which makes the Persian
  ghazal so delightful, like a two-faced brocade" — and she refuses the alternative, "Without
  returning to the heavy and usually quite tasteless theological interpretation of the various
  metaphors" (pp. 78–79, folios 68–69). "as I mentioned earlier, this double entendre is intended, and
  in the case of Hafiz one may even find a third level, that of panegyrics in the guise of love
  poetry" (p. 91, folio 81); a poem "that enthusiastically sings of the beauty and kindness of the
  beloved may be meant to be a eulogy on the Prophet" (p. 91, folio 81) — without disentangling the
  designs "one neither really understands nor enjoys verses which look … often quite repetitious"
  (p. 91, folio 81).
- **Against reading the lyric as autobiography.** Of Hafiz's *turk-i shīrāzī* line: it "is not
  primarily important because of its contents but rather because of the de­lightful rhetorical plays
  concealed in it" (p. 247, folio 229), the analysis naming "the contrast Turk-Hindu, extremely
  widespread in Persian and related poetry", three geographical names and three parts of the body, "so
  that a multilevel murli°dt an-111l4ir"[text-layer?: murāʿāt an-naẓīr] "is achieved which is
  immediately destroyed when the Shirazi Turk is trans­formed into a 'friend' or a 'barmaid'" (p. 248,
  folio 230); conclusion: "we should not look for Erlebnislyrik, in the postenlightenment sense, in
  this poetry" (p. 248, folio 230). The Western image of Persian poetry is likewise an artefact of bad
  translation: Hafiz "fascinated Europe from the late eighteenth century onward and was instrumental
  in shaping the general notion in the West of Persian poetry as something that expressed uninhibited
  sensual love, religious freedom if not outright heresy, and that sang mainly of wine, roses, and
  nightingales", while Goethe shows "a deeper understanding … than do the very free and often faulty
  'translations' and imitations by most other Western writers on Hafiz" (p. 59, folio not stated).
- **Against sorting mystics into types.** "A study of their imagery proves that the vocabulary of both
  personal and impersonal mysticism is used almost indiscriminately by them, and images from the three
  major spheres outlined by Evelyn Underhill can occur even in the same verse" (p. 89, folio 79);
  `via eminentiae` and `via negationis` appear in the same poet a few lines apart (p. 89, folio 79).
  The three spheres are Underhill's — pilgrimage, alchemy, and human love — and Persian poets "use and
  interweave them often in a single verse even though the center of their thoughts is always Love"
  (p. 74, folio 64).
- **Unity-language predates its own theory — and is feeling, not doctrine.** "The formula `hama ast`,
  'Everything is He,' was already in use before Ibn `c Arabi`[text-layer?: ʿArabī] laid down the
  foundations of the theory of `wahdat al-wujud`[text-layer?: waḥdat al-wujūd]; it is found more than
  once in ʿAttar's lyrics" (p. 71, folio 61); "in most cases this was rather the expression of an
  overwhelming feeling of God's omnipresence, not the kind of theoretical pantheism that became so
  prevalent in post-Ibn `c Arabi`[text-layer?: ʿArabī] poetry" (p. 70, folio 60). The same move is made
  for Rumi: Zulaikha's song "is not an expression of a the­oretical Unity of Being but rather
  translates into verse the psychological truth which the Sufis condensed in the sentence: 'Who loves
  something mentions it often.'" (p. 242, folio 224).
- **The decline thesis, and the modernists' verdict on the tradition.** Poets like `Maghribi (d.
  14o6)`[text-layer?: d. 1406] expressed Unity of Being "in thousands of lines whose imagery soon
  became stereotyped", and Ibn ʿArabi's method "made this kind of poetry easy to handle, and as the
  conventional topoi became more shallow and outworn, only very few poets were able to fill them with
  new life" (p. 73, folio 63); ʿIraqi's lines "form the model for thousands of verses, often by
  mediocre writers who were no longer aware of the secret of longing as the source of true poetry"
  (p. 80, folio 70). In this tradition "the inherited values of Islam were considered only as a
  support for the poor plebeians who had not yet reached the lofty heights of all-embracing unity";
  hence "the aversion of Muslim modernists to this poetry, which seemed to lull their compatriots into
  sweet slumber instead of inciting them to new activity on the Path of God. For the dynamic
  love-relation between God and man had been replaced, in most cases, by a theosophic
  `tauhid`[text-layer?: tauḥīd] expressed in sweeping statements" (p. 73, folio 63).
- **Anti-intellectualism as a deliberate posture.** "the mystics, anti-intellectual despite their great
  learning, repeated time and again that reason, or intellect, leads to talk while Love silently
  reveals the inside of the mysteries"; Sana'i, "probably following Ghazzali's attack on Avicenna in
  the `Ta -` / `hafut al-falasifa`[text-layer?: Tahāfut al-falāsifa]-was the first poet to condemn his
  compatriot Avicenna … as the paragon of love-less intellectualism" (p. 77, folio 67); and "Maulana
  Rumi continued in this antiphilosophical style, which is very typical for poets and mystics in the
  Eastern Is­lamic world" (p. 210, folio 192).
- **Pain is constructed, not merely suffered.** "The Persian poets invented an ingenious way to prove
  the value of pain and affliction by pointing to the central metahistorical event, the
  `rii.z-i alast`[text-layer?: rūz-i alast]" (p. 80, folio 70); "Destruction is necessary for building
  up: kenosis, emptying oneself, complete surrender, or ''being broken' is the prerequisite for a
  higher spiritual life" (p. 81, folio 71); and the imagery's "cruelty... shocked Goethe when he tried
  to give an impartial judgment of Persian poetry" (p. 81, folio 71). Related: "Sufism reached an
  equation of .faqr, 'poverty,' and .fanti'"[text-layer?: fanāʾ], "'annihilation,' which was
  poetically expressed by < Attar in the last valley of the Mantiq ut-tair" (p. 252, folio 234).
- **Self-effacement as the classical ideal, expressed through a fixed pair.** "the classical ideal of
  the Sufis that everything human has to diminish so that God's presence can increase … for the lovers
  are all petition, niytiz, the beloved all coquettish pride, ntiz [text-layer?: niyāz … nāz]"
  (p. 272, folio 254). Reported at second hand and not endorsed: "I was once told that niiz"
  [text-layer?: nāz] "and mal1brib"[text-layer?: maḥbūb] "(beloved) are as it were interchangeable
  because the two words have the same numerical value, namely 58!" (p. 251, folio 233).

#### Rumi

- **He is misread by his admirers, and most of all through Ibn ʿArabi.** "Rumi has also been
  misunderstood by his most ardent admirers, for nothing could be more alien to him than a
  systematization of his thought, particularly when this systematization is undertaken, as in hundreds
  of commentaries up to our day, in the light of Ibn `c Arabi`[text-layer?: ʿArabī]'s theosophy"
  (p. 93, folio not stated); he "was not interested in the theosophical aspect of mysticism which
  developed under lbn[text-layer?: Ibn] `c Arabi`[text-layer?: ʿArabī]'s influence" (p. 103,
  folio 93). "Translations of and commentaries on this work exist in almost every Islamic language and
  are available, at least in part, in Western languages as well" (p. 93, folio not stated).
- **Biography is the key to the lyrics, and the life is itself a paradigm.** "To understand properly
  Rumi's poetry, and particularly his lyrics, one has to know the major events of his life out of
  which his verse grew" (pp. 93–94, folios not stated / 84). "Maulana Rumi's life is a paradigm of the
  ideal mystical biography as drawn up by later Sufis, i.e., the semicircle": annihilation in Shams,
  then `baqii`[text-layer?: baqā], "of stabilization in and through Salahuddin", then the
  `sair ilii', 1-ashyii'`[text-layer?: sair ilā'l-ashyāʾ], "the return to the created things as
  manifested in his friendship with his disciple Husamuddin" (pp. 104–105, folios 94–95).
- **Dating the Diwan: a stated limit and a proposed method.** "At present it seems impossible to
  attempt an exact dating of Rumi's early verse. However, one can observe a certain change of emphasis
  in his lyrics, and a careful metrical analysis of the `Dfwiin-i Shams`[text-layer?: Dīwān-i Shams]
  is also helpful in placing at least some poems in their proper sequence" (p. 96, folio 86). Her own
  early markers: the `liilfs`[text-layer?: lūlīs] ("The `liilfs`, the charming, playful, and dangerous
  Gypsies, appear to me as part of the early vocabulary"), `�a]Jrii`[text-layer?: ṣaḥrā] "the wide
  desert", and address of the beloved as `khidfw`[text-layer?: khidīw] or with "the Greek terms
  `efendf`[text-layer?: efendī] or `aghap6s`[text-layer?: agapós]", which "are certainly early"
  (p. 97, folio 87); the three-stage use of Shams' name — absent, then mid-poem or second-to-last
  line, then as `takhallufi`[text-layer?: takhalluṣ] — structures the argument (pp. 96–99,
  folios 86–89). She rejects one proposal: `khiimush`[text-layer?: khāmūsh] "is used so frequently
  that some scholars have been inclined to regard it as Rumi's original nom de plume. However", it
  often stands in the second-to-last line with Shams' name in the last, and a number of such poems
  "can be dated to a later time" (p. 107, folio 97).
- **The Mathnawi's character.** "The Mathnawi, with its nearly 26,000 verses, which grew out of
  Maulana's loving relationship with his disciple, is a veritable encyclopedia of mystical lore. But
  it lacks a systematic structure, and some of Maulana's more philosophically minded compatriots
  blamed him because he did not enter into any theoretical discussions or use the technical vocabulary
  of the Sufis" (p. 103, folio 93); "Maulana Rumi could not finish the sixth book of the
  `Mathnawf`[text-layer?: Mathnawī]; its last story is open-ended" (p. 104, folio 94).
- **The lyrics are outcries, not doctrine.** "Rumi was well aware that his lyrics were born
  spontaneously out of the agitation of his heart and that they should therefore be taken as sudden
  outcries, not as theological statements which could be ruminated upon by generations of readers or
  commentators" (p. 106, folio 96). He is "one of the few poets in the Islamic world who have spoken
  often and openly about the secret of poetical inspiration" (p. 105, folio 95), and in `Fihi ma
  fihf`[text-layer?: Fīhi mā fīhi] said "he was basically not at all interested in poetry" (p. 105,
  folio 95) — which she sets in a wider pattern: "The alleged aversion to writing is common also with
  Sufi poets in the classical tradition, who were prolific writers and yet felt the constant tension
  between the impossibility of expressing their experience and the urge to convey their wisdom to
  their disciples" (p. 279, folio 261). Against the spontaneity, his craft: for "Rumi's technical
  skill, which enabled him to use complicated rhetorical devices in his spontaneous verse" she refers
  to J. Christoph Bürgel (p. 260, folio 242); and she can still judge that "the end of this
  1ong [text-layer?: long] ghazal is rather uninspired" (p. 274, folio 256).
- **The protean image is the chapter's methodological premise.** "The protean character of his images
  poses an additional difficulty to the interpreter", his verses being "often surprising in their
  frankness and sometimes coarseness" (p. 108, folio 98); "Seemingly banal sensual affects trigger off
  the highest inspiration, and out of elemental realism grows the most sublime symbolism", the sources
  being "the kitchen, the market place, hunting, medicine, children, the greengrocer with his sourmilk
  who toils for his daily bread, as well as obscene anecdotes and the stories of the Prophets, family
  quarrels" (p. 108, folio 98); scent is his "medium of reminiscence" (p. 108, folio 98). Love itself
  is unstable: "the center of his thoughts is Love, which is sometimes interchangeable with the
  Beloved, and one often wonders whether it is Love or the Beloved that is intended by his words"
  (p. 111, folio 101); "There is such variety in his poetical descriptions that they seem to include
  all the symbols ever used by mystics in East and West" (p. 111, folio 101); "at times, mainly in the
  mid-sixties, he tries to discuss philosophical topics which soon dissolve again into poetical
  musings" (p. 111, folio 101); and Love is a `coinddentia` [text-layer?: coincidentia] oppositorum
  (p. 113, folio 103).
- **Which images are inherited and which are his own.** Inherited: the amber/straw comparison with the
  `kuh`/`kiih` [text-layer?: kūh/kāh] pun (p. 114, folio 104); the school of love, where "he is not
  unprecedented, for the Sufis (and particularly Ruzbihan-i Baqli) had spoken of the educational value
  of human love" (p. 115, folio 105). Rare in him: "Maulana does not frequently use the favorite image
  of most Persian poets, that of 'moth and candle,' but when it occurs is has a special depth"
  (p. 117, folio 107). His own: "other images in Rumi's work are uncommon and verge on the grotesque.
  Who else would see Love as the police chief, `shi]Jna` [text-layer?: shiḥna] …?" (p. 126,
  folio 116); the unicorn is "Unusual in the context of Persian poetry" (p. 124, folio 114); and he
  "uses the imagery of 'becoming naked' more frequently than any other poet", following his father's
  example (p. 259, folio 241).
- **Fire, wine, water, sun.** "Thrown out of the well-established professorial way of life by the heady
  wine of Love, he uses this imagery more frequently than any other metaphor, except for fire"
  (p. 142, folio 124), and "Maulana had probably read Ibn al-Farid's great ode to wine, allusions to
  which can be detected in some of his verses" (p. 142, folio 124) — the hedge is hers. Fire images
  "go back to ancient rites of purification through fire, and lead, `in tum` [text-layer?: in turn] to
  the vocabulary of alchemy" (p. 116, folio 106); "Understandably, the positive, wholesome aspects of
  Love are more prominent in the watery than in the fiery sphere" (p. 119, folio 109); the sun is "a
  perfect manifestation of God's `jamal` [text-layer?: jamāl] and `jaltil` [text-layer?: jalāl], the
  mysterium fascinans and the mysterium tremendum" (p. 118, folio 108). **Transmission to South
  Asia:** the fiery-reed lines opening the `Mathnawf` [text-layer?: Mathnawī] are "an image that was
  taken over by numerous later Persian poets, particularly in the Indo-Persian tradition" (p. 116,
  folio 106), and "Rumi, prefiguring scores of later Persian and Urdu poets, can claim that 'Love has
  rosegardens in the midst of veils of blood'" (p. 120, folio 110).
- **Love against Intellect, read as satire on legalistic Islam — and the topos it founded.** "In
  Maulana's favorite, and most convenient, comparison Intellect is the qiiqi [text-layer?: qāḍī], the
  judge representing legalistic Islam" (p. 146, folio 128); the ghazal stages "Mufti First Intellect"
  issuing a fatwa and "The preacher Love" arriving at "the c;dgah [text-layer?: ʿīdgāh] of Union"
  (p. 146, folio 128). The pulpit-versus-gallows contrast is "a topos which was to become commonplace
  for almost all later Persianate poets" (p. 145, folio 127), resting on the pun "Only by becoming a
  martyr (shahid) man can be a witness (shiihid [text-layer?: shāhid])" (p. 145, folio 127).
- **Love outside law and ritual — with the biographical qualification kept.** "Maulana in his personal
  life continued to follow the injunctions of religious law, very carefully observing fasting and
  prayer. Yet all these lively images point to the fact that Love is outside the religious rites and
  the seventy-two sects of Islam" (p. 147, folio 129); "Love is free from the narrowness of prayer
  niche and cross" (p. 148, folio 130). She dates the position earlier than him: "Such ideas were
  expressed more prosaically as early as c. 900 by some Baghdadian Sufi leaders" (p. 147, folio 129).
- **The dance is the practice the poetry came out of.** "Rumi, whose poetry was born out of the
  whirling movement of ecstatic dance, often saw Love as the great musician" (p. 150, folio 132); "In
  this whirling movement the lover experiences the secret of fanii' and baqii' [text-layer?: fanāʾ and
  baqāʾ]: he is taken out of the gravitational field of the material world and annihilated in the
  spiritual sun's magnetic field" (p. 151, folio 133).
- **A deliberate refusal to read the ascent philosophically.** Of the movement "from mineral and plant
  to man and angel" ending in "cadam [text-layer?: ʿadam], 'positive Not-being,'" she insists "one
  should interpret these verses not as pertaining to certain philosophical theories or to
  metempsychosis but rather as another expression of his conviction that no part of creation can reach
  a higher level of existence without sacrificing itself in Love" (p. 141, folio 123); "Love is the
  great alchemy. Its power can transform the base copper of man's lower potencies into gold, provided
  he endures all afflictions in its crucible" (pp. 141–142, folios 123–124).
- **Ethnonyms in his verse are conventions, not ethnography.** "`riimi` [text-layer?: rūmī], Byzantine,
  and `zanji` [text-layer?: zanjī], Ethiopian, form a traditional contrasting pair as do turk and
  `h indii` [text-layer?: hindū]" (p. 124, folio 114); "Turk is here, as elsewhere, the designation of
  the powerful, radiant, and cruel beloved, the princely rider" (p. 126, folio 116). Love's sword
  cutting off every head is "the traditional image of the negation, `Iii` [text-layer?: lā], with
  which the profession of faith begins and which in its graphic form is interpreted as a double-edged
  sword. It is comparable to `c Ali`'s [text-layer?: ʿAlī's] famous Dhu'l-fiqar" (p. 125, folio 115).
- **Stations, robes, and a feminine Love.** "fear and hope are stages of the beginners; when they are
  overcome man will reach annihilation in love-intoxication" (p. 137, folio 119); Love is "the robe of
  honor for the soul and the necklace of `karramnd` [text-layer?: karramnā] (`Sura 17170`
  [text-layer?: Sura 17/70])" (p. 137, folio 119); and "Love, cruel and overpowering as it generally
  is, can at times be a feminine concept, for it is the mother who gives birth to man and to the four
  elements", Rumi going "so far as to speak of Love as being the pre-eternal Mary", against the harlot
  World "who devours her children!" (p. 139, folio 121).
- **Questions she leaves open on Rumi, flagged as gaps.** "A full study of this aspect of Rumi's
  prophetology, which again leads back to Shamsuddin's connection with the Prophet, is yet to be done"
  (p. 139, folio 121); "It would be interesting to find out whether Rumi's choice of images was
  influenced by the objects of art that surrounded him", possibly "reliefs representing the
  Tree-of-Life or dragons on Seljukid architecture" — put as a question, not a finding (p. 267,
  folio 249); of Baghdad in his imagery, "several times this Baghdad is contrasted, for reasons
  unknown to me, with Hamadhan" (p. 265, folio 247); on `parda-i <Jraqi` she declines an attribution
  Schaeder floated in a seminar, allowing only that "the poem is indeed quite close in Stimmung to
  verses of Rurni's [text-layer?: Rumi's] younger contemporary" (p. 273, folio 255); and "A study of
  Rumi's paradoxes would be most welcome." (p. 288, folio 270).

#### The Turkish material

- **Divan poetry dismissed as imitation — and the charge extended to Persian.** "Turkish 'Divan
  Poetry' was often regarded as a mere imitation of Persian models, and E. H. W. Gibb's introduction
  to his useful History of Ottoman Poetry is valid for most of Persian poetry as well" (p. 65,
  folio 55).
- **Attribution in Turkish folk verse is irrecoverable because the verse was performance material —
  and the same holds in Sindhi and Panjabi.** "The fact that such poetry was meant to be recited in
  the gatherings of dervishes accounts for the difficulty of finding which poems are really by
  Yunus . ... A similar phenomenon can be observed in Sindhi and Panjabi religious literature."
  (p. 168, folio 150). The credit for first seeing the samāʿ use of Yunus' verse goes to "John R.
  Walsh" (p. 167, folio 149).
- **ʿAli and the alphabet poem.** ʿAli "plays a particularly important role in Turkish Bektashi poetry
  because the Bektashis are largely Shia-oriented", yet "as the early hero of Islam ... c Ali also
  looms large in Sindhi, Panjabi, and related popular poetry, even in a non-Shiite environment."
  (p. 282, folio 264); in the Golden Alphabet the `cain` [text-layer?: ʿain] "was associated with
  cishq [text-layer?: ʿishq], love, or with c Ali ibn Abi Talib … whose veneration plays an important
  role particularly in Turkish Bektashi poetry" (p. 165, folio 147). The form "is also common in
  Turkish" and "occurs rarely in classical Persian" (pp. 281–282, folios 263–264).
- **A poet's own exegesis preserved.** Niyazi Misri glosses his own verse — "'Poplar' is a fragrant
  tree without fruit. Intended is the dry ascetic who claims to possess gnosis, and who is captured by
  the wish for authority ... and sells these wise words as his own goods" — and the closing Arabic
  verse "has been quoted time and again by Sufis when they complained about the degeneration of
  Sufism, of which only the outward traces are left" (p. 290, folio 272).

#### The vernaculars of the Subcontinent and mystical folk poetry

- **The claim that most concerns this archive: the folk-tale-as-mystical-vehicle technique is a Sind
  and Punjab invention, and a late one.** Of the regional epics that took up "popular love tales and
  transformed them into vehicles for mystical teaching": "But only in the centuries after his death
  did mystical poets in Sind and the Punjab develop the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media
  for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136) — "his" being Bada'uni's.
- **Chronology of the vernaculars.** "About the same time-the late thirteenth and the early fourteenth
  centuries-we find the first songs about mystical love in Anatolia in simple Turkish verse and in
  India in Hindwi. In the Arab world too popular mystical poetry developed during that period.
  Somewhat later Panjabi, Sindhi, and Pashto followed" (p. 155, folio 137). Maneri's remark is "one of
  the first known remarks by a mystical master about religious poetry in the folk idiom, although the
  use of local languages in Sufi circles goes back to the thirteenth century"; and if Baba Tahir's
  quatrains count, "mystical poetry outside the 'high' literary traditions began as early as the
  eleventh century in Iran, and a century later in Central Asia" (p. 153, folio not stated). A
  consequence she draws from the dating: "since folk poetry developed only after 1300, the influence
  of lbn c Arabi's theories of wa]Jdat al-wujud [text-layer?: waḥdat al-wujūd], Unity of Being, as
  well as the typical vocabulary of the Sufi fraternities, form an integral part of this literary
  genre." (p. 169, folio 151).
- **One trans-regional idiom from Istanbul to Delhi.** "The contents of mystical poetry between
  Istanbul and Delhi are therefore so similar that one can almost translate a Sindhi poem by Sachal
  Sarmast into Turkish ... or transplant some Bektashi verses into Panjabi and declare them to be
  compositions of Bullhe Shah." (p. 169, folio 151); the borders "between 'high' and popular poetry
  became blurred" (p. 169, folio 151).
- **But the vernaculars differ from each other.** "However, Pashto religious poetry is on the whole
  like Balochi, more ascetic in outlook, and it rarely uses the sweet generalizations of all-embracing
  unity which are common to Sindhi, Panjabi, and Bengali Sufi songs" (p. 155, folio 137).
- **Vernacular mysticism bypasses a priestly class, and is anti-clerical by design.** "The use of the
  folk language enabled the mystics to teach the core of religion without the mediation of a priestly
  cast (as in the Hindu case where in fact the most ardent poets in the folk idioms were shudras), and
  without relying upon the writings of erudite scholars" (p. 156, folio 138) — the scholars being
  those Ghazzali said were "well informed about the most complicated forms of divorce but could not
  tell what 'trust in God' or 'piety' really meant" (pp. 156–157, folios 138–139). Hence "mystical
  folk poetry throughout the Muslim world has a strongly anti-intellectual bias and is often directed
  against the ulema and their claim to be the true depositaries of the Divine Law" (p. 157,
  folio 139), anchored doctrinally in "cilm ladunni [text-layer?: ʿilm ladunnī] (5ura 18/65
  [text-layer?: Sura 18/65])", in the Prophet "who was called in the Koran ummi (Sura 7'157-158
  [text-layer?: Sura 7/157–158]), generally translated as 'illiterate'", and in the folk mystics'
  claim "that their poetry consisted of waridat, inspired words, which descended upon them without any
  intellectual activity" (p. 157, folio 139).
- **Koranic Arabic is what makes the Muslim vernaculars Muslim.** "These commonly used words and
  sentences are found practically all over the Islamic world, and it is this Koranic heritage that
  gives the Muslim branches of Indian languages their distinct flavor" (p. 157, folio 139).
- **Folk poetry is not exempt from fossilization** — a qualification she states against her own
  enthusiasm. "Yet, in the history of mystical folk poetry too the process of repetition and
  fossilization of forms, topics, and images is as visible as in high poetry" (p. 160, folio 142), set
  against most Persian mystics, who "composed their verse in the precious style, which, though often
  developed out of generally used images, soon crystallized into more and more abstract patterns"
  (p. 159, folio 141).
- **Imagery is read off the landscape, and local knowledge is made a condition of reading.** The poets
  "resorted to an imagery taken from daily life and from the landscape that surrounded them" (p. 159,
  folio 141), following "the Koranic injunction to find God's signs 'in the earth and in
  yourselves-do you not see?' (Sura 51'21 [text-layer?: Sura 51/21])" (p. 160, folio 142); "One enjoys
  these poems best when one knows the country and realizes how true to life their imagery is" (p. 163,
  folio 145). Worked examples: the tree of life (p. 160, folio 142); the water wheel "doliib
  [text-layer?: dūlāb]", whose shriek is "the tree's complaint about its separation from the forest"
  (p. 161, folio 143); the yellow flower as "a perfect dervish who expects nothing from this world",
  and the fading "color saffron" against "the red dye (lak) that stays even when the cloth is worn to
  rags" (p. 162, folio 144); and the substitution of birds by region — "In Sindhi and Panjabi the
  kang, crow, serves as messenger instead of the more urbane pigeon", "the Papiha bird in the Indian
  plains asks during the rainy season in Hindi, piu kahiiii [text-layer?: piu kahāṇ?], 'Where is the
  beloved?'", and "Sindhi poetry recognizes the swan that feeds on pearls as the symbol of the true
  Sufi" (pp. 162–163, folios 144–145).
- **Form is explained functionally, by memory and by music.** "Poetry that was meant for the masses had
  to be memorable and had to have simple rhyme patterns. For this reason certain forms were favorites
  with the mystical folk poets" (pp. 164–165, folios 146–147); the Golden Alphabet "helped to memorize
  the sequence of the alphabet, and each letter was charged with a certain meaning" (p. 165,
  folio 147) — "The alif for every Muslim is the letter pointing to God, the One and Unique. Them
  [text-layer?: The m] was usually connected with Muhammad" (p. 165, folio 147). Regionally: "In
  Panjabi, Indian words are often used in Siharfis, not so much Arabic expressions; thus alif may
  begin the line ek alak'h be-ant data, 'One endless, unlimited Lord,' d may stand for dikh, 'see,'
  sh for shih, 'lion,'" (p. 281, folio 263). And music governs the rest: "Its use in music is the
  reason for grammatical irregularities, strange forms, and unusual rhymes and wordings which at times
  defy logical analysis . Its logic is peculiar because it aims to create a certain mystical mood
  rather than to explain, in plain words, rational facts ." (pp. 166–167, folios 148–149); "It is the
  mnemonic aspect of poetry which makes it a much more useful means of instruction than prose."
  (p. 166, folio 148).
- **The calendar poems are regionally differentiated.** "In Panjabi, I found the Indian months almost
  exclusively, while Sindhi uses both Indian and Muslim and lately even Christian months (as does the
  contemporary poet Talib al-Maula)" (p. 282, folio 264); the Barahmasa's soul hopes for union "either
  with the Divine Beloved or with the haqfqa muhammadiyya [text-layer?: ḥaqīqa muḥammadiyya], the
  archetypal Muhammad" (p. 165, folio 147).
- **The woman soul is the Indo-Muslim specialty, and she attributes it to Hindu influence.** "the
  Indo-Muslim folk tradition has developed another peculiar facet under the influence of Hindu
  neighbors-the symbol of the woman soul ." (pp. 169–170, folios 151–152), contrasted with Arabic
  usage (a beautiful maiden) and Persian (a "beautiful young shiihid" [text-layer?: shāhid]) (p. 169,
  folio 151). Classical Sufism had made the nafs feminine "since the word nafs is, luckily for the
  ascetics, feminine in Arabic", which "leads generally to a most deprecatory description of
  everything feminine, women being on the lowest rung of the human race" (p. 170, folio 152); the
  heroines are read both as souls and as allegories of the nafs' education, "from lowly a beginning as
  nafs ammiira [text-layer?: nafs ammāra], 'inciting to evil' (Sura 12/59)" to "nafs mutma'inna 'the
  soul at peace' (Sura 89'27 [text-layer?: 89/27])" (p. 170, folio 152); and the poets defend their
  own choice — "they therefore repeatedly state that these women were in reality 'men,' true 'men of
  God .'" (p. 173, folio 155). The same gendering is carried by the saying "talib ad-dunya
  mu'annath-talib al-akhira mukhannath-talib al-maula mudhakkar, 'Who seeks the world, is a female;
  who seeks the Hereafter, is a catamite; who seeks the Lord, is a man,'" (p. 284, folio 266).
- **The letter-writing motif and the imprisoned soul.** "The imagery of tears as ink is very common in
  both high and popular Sufi poetry ... It is indeed amazing to see the illiterate women in these
  poems complain time and again that no letter has reached them", and "in mystical interpretation the
  girl is the soul, imprisoned (like Marui in the Sindhi tradition) in this colorful but treacherous
  world, who longs for a sign of Divine grace." (p. 284, folio 266). A speculation she marks as her
  own: "I wonder if in some of the spinning songs an implicit allusion to Hallaj, the cotton-carder,
  may be intended; in high poetry, thus in Rumi's Diwan, he is sometimes connected with cotton; that
  is the case especially in later times, after Shabistari had spoken of the 'cotton of existence'"
  (p. 281, folio 263).
- **Against reading this poetry as pantheism, with three alternatives offered.** "Sufism is nothing but
  measureless pantheism-an impression which is certainly not correct but can easily be deduced from
  this poetry if its images are taken at face value." (p. 176, folio 158). For the ecstatic
  identification verses she offers instead the soul traversing "the stations of all prophets on its
  way towards the haqfqa mufiammadiyya [text-layer?: ḥaqīqa muḥammadiyya]" (p. 177, folio 159); the
  state of "bast, expansion, which may amount to a kind of cosmic consciousness" (p. 177, folio 159);
  and the caution that "probably not every illiterate or semiliterate poet was fully acquainted with
  these speculations" (p. 177, folio 159). The ground of all three is the topos thesis: "In later Sufi
  tradition, the multicolored imagery of all-embracing unity and indifference toward ritual became a
  mere topos and could be used even by otherwise perfectly legalistic Muslims" (p. 178, folio 160).
- **Non-Muslim participation is recorded as a fact of the record.** The Islam taught to the masses "was
  not the exclusive religion of the immutable Law but rather a religion of warm love-a love in which
  non-Muslims might also share, as in India Hindus participated in writing mystical poetry in honor of
  the Prophet and the saints." (p. 186, folio 168); concretely, "Even Hindus joined the Muslims in
  composing `na<ts` [text-layer?: naʿts], thus Sufi Bhai Asuram (d. 1941) in Sindhi … The first
  scholarly biography of the Prophet in Sindhi was also written by a Hindu teacher, Lalchand
  Jagtiani." (p. 307, folio 289). She nonetheless rejects the Hindu commentarial reading of Shah
  ʿAbdul Latif outright: "These patronizing remarks (pp. 37-38) are certainly out of place." (p. 308,
  folio 290).
- **"Turk" in Indian popular usage is a religious, not an ethnic, label.** "Turk is the general
  designation of the Muslim in Indian popular tradition ... After all, the first conquerors of North
  West India and many of the later rulers, including the Moghuls, were of Turkish extraction; besides,
  the motif of the beautiful but cruel Turk was commonplace in higher Persian poetry." (p. 286,
  folio 268); the index keeps both senses side by side — "Turk, symbol of the radiant, powerful, and
  cruel beloved in contrast with the Hindu … as general designa­tion for Muslim" (p. 376, folio 358),
  against "Hindu, as symbol of lowliness and contrasted to Turk" (p. 368, folio 350).
- **The inverted world, and a caution against reading it as Indian.** The topsy-turvy world "is common
  to all peoples", found in English and German lying songs and "in pictorial representations from the
  Middle Ages onward, as in thirteenth-century English tiles that show a hare riding out to hunt"
  (p. 290, folio 272); Das Gupta "combines this inverted, u/ta [text-layer?: ultā] way of speaking
  with the Yoga ideal of the inversion of the bodily functions." (pp. 290–291, folios 272–273).
  Similarly on "mystical philology": "given the connection of the East, sharq, in the tradition of
  Sufism, with the area of illumination, ishriiq [text-layer?: ishrāq], this Divine pfirbi
  [text-layer?: pūrbī] may also belong to the 'inspirational' idioms rather than being a veritable
  Indian dialect." (p. 288, folio 270).
- **Vernacular idiom and women, at the tradition's origin.** "An interesting account of the relation
  between the use of the indigenous idiom and women in c. A . o . 850 [text-layer?: c. A.D. 850] is
  found in Tirmidhi's Arabic autobiography ... when the angel speaks to the Sufi's wife in Persian in
  her dream"; with Dammann's remark "that popular religious poetry in Swahili is often sung by women"
  (p. 281, folio 263).
- **Research proposals she names, most of them pointing at Pakistan.** "It would be worthwhile to study
  the relationship between poetry used in the samac and the remarkably rich tradition of imagery
  connected with music." (p. 168, folio 150); "it would be worthwhile to study Sindhi or Pashto
  riddles and conundrums with a view to the paradoxical (and probably often mystical) meanings which
  they may convey to the initiated." (p. 183, folio 165); "It might be interesting to study Sufi
  legends as projections of ideas expressed in such paradoxical verses and riddles ." (pp. 183–184,
  folios 165–166); and, testing Victor Turner's "Mystical rhetoric . . . is very often characteristic
  of movements of egalitarism, popular protest during liminal periods of history", "It would be
  worthwhile to explore to what extent [it] ... is valid for Sufism; it is certainly relevant for the
  recently growing interest of progressive writers in Islamic countries in the mystical tradition, in
  which they discover the predominance of Love over law" (p. 288, folio 270).
- **Two textual problems on the Pakistan side she leaves open.** On the Sindhi edition: "Qa41 Qtidan jo
  kaltim gives the seven verses which were hitherto known only in the appendix because they are not
  mentioned in the Hariyana manuscript on which this edition relies" (p. 279, folio 261). On a
  sixteenth-century dating: "although the mention of the Kanz in a poem from the first half of the
  sixteenth century is strange; one would have to find out when this collection was actually
  introduced in theological courses in India in order to date the verse properly" (p. 279, folio 261).
  A third, given as her gloss on a title: the Rashdi article argues "('Shah c Abdul Latif did not
  invent the kaff [text-layer?: kāfī],' see pp. 215-24 .)" (p. 334, folio 316).

#### Poetry in praise of the Prophet

- **The chapter's premise.** "The love of the Prophet has been called the strongest binding force in
  Islam" (p. 189, folio not stated), and "Wilfred Cantwell Smith has lucidly shown how important the
  person of the Prophet became particularly during the last hundred years." (p. 190, folio 172).
- **The South Asian record is the chapter's most detailed.** The Indian tendency is Arabising: Qudsi's
  poem "is a typical expression not only of the veneration of sacred names but also of the Indian
  tendency to stress the Arabic aspect of Muhammad's biography" (p. 218, folio 200); more broadly,
  "this is typical of the attitude of many Indian Muslims who often felt like foreigners in the
  Subcontinent and found that the remedy for all ills is the Arabic tradi­tion, as the great
  theologian of Delhi, Shah Waliullah, remarked." (p. 305, folio 287), with his own words: "Arabs
  should be given mastery of [people of all] reli­gions, and their customs should be substituted by
  the customs of the Arabs" (pp. 305–306, folios 287–288). Against that urban register stands the
  rural one: "While the urban poets in India expressed their veneration of the Prophet in grandiose
  hymns to display their erudition, their contemporaries in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote
  tender little poems" (p. 222, folio 204); "The comparison of the Prophet with the rain seems to
  occur more frequently, however, in folk poetry than in high poetry" (p. 220, folio 202), the
  rain-clouds distributing "ra/Jma [text-layer?: raḥma], mercy" being "ingeniously connected with
  praise of the Prophet who too was sent rafJmatan lilciilamfn [text-layer?: raḥmatan lil-ʿālamīn],
  'as mercy for the worlds' (Sura 21'10'7 [text-layer?: Sura 21/107])" (p. 164, folio 146). The
  tradition polices its own registers: "Urdu writers do not in vain distinguish between the
  traditional nact and a more existential kind of poetry the depth of whose contents can barely be
  grasped by the uninitiated" (p. 221, folio 203).
- **Devotion outranks historicity, and the book says so without complaint.** Of Makhdum Muhammad
  Hashim, "The loving portrayal of the Prophet was more important for him and his followers than
  historical truth, information about which, in any case, was difficult to obtain from the literature
  at their disposal" (p. 223, folio 205). She defends the same licence in the nativity poems by a
  European analogy: "This attitude is not different from that of German or Dutch painters who placed
  the Nativity in a northern European landscape, or that of popular Christmas carols where Bethlehem
  is transferred into a Ger­man rural environment"; it expresses "the feeling of being very close to
  the blessed child who will emerge as the savior of all those who follow him." (p. 298, folio 280).
- **Two live South Asian disputes reported.** Whether blessings on the Prophet are meaningful "since he
  is already perfected", resolved by Shah Waliullah with a Burda citation (p. 300, folio 282); and a
  second in which "Fazlul Haqq Khairabadi, who defended the first possibility, died in 1862 in the
  Andamans where he was exiled after his participation in the 'Mu­tiny' of 1857" (p. 306, folio 288).
- **Hassan ibn Thabit is the seed of the whole genre.** "Thus, Hassan ibn Thabit's verse indeed contains
  the germs of most later poetry in honor of the Prophet" (p. 194, folio 176), with the `hilya`, the
  intercession motif and the light motif traced back to him (pp. 193–194, folios 175–176); his verse
  is "an important constituent of the srra [text-layer?: sīra], Muhammad's biography" (p. 191,
  folio 173).
- **The veneration is chiefly a Sufi development.** "While the common Muslims deeply trusted in the
  Prophet and his message... the veneration of Muhammad as an almost super­human figure grew
  predominantly in Sufi circles" (p. 195, folio 177), and the Light of Muhammad was elaborated
  "possibly in connection with trends in Shia imamology" (p. 195, folio 177) — her own hedge. Bowering
  is cited "particularly about the elaboration of the theory of the nar Muf]ammad [text-layer?: nūr
  Muḥammad] by Sahl at-Tustari" (p. 296, folio 278); the Prophet "can be seen in dreams by many
  different people in the same hour . . . because he is like the sun whose light covers many
  countries, east and west" (p. 296, folio 278).
- **This love is not erotic love.** "this love never enters the realm of cishq [text-layer?: ʿishq],
  erotic love, but remains in the sphere of respect. It resembles the loving veneration one feels
  toward a senior member of the family" (p. 207, folio 189).
- **The maulid was deliberately deregionalised, and comes closest to myth.** "allusions to the local
  Arabic environment and typically Arabic traditions, as they are found in the oldest part of the srra
  [text-layer?: sīra], were left out in order to give the maulid literature a more universal appeal"
  (p. 200, folio 182); "it is common to surround the birth of the founder of a religion with garlands
  of wonder­ful events. The motif of light is central in them because often the birth of a saviour
  (Hei/sbringer) [text-layer?: Heilsbringer] and his epiphany fall to­gether" — with parallels to "the
  story of Bethlehem", "the wonderful birth of the Buddha as told in the Lalita Vi1?tara [text-layer?:
  Lalita Vistara]", Zoroaster, and "the fanciful stories of Krishna' s birth" (p. 200, folio 182); and
  "Nowhere else does Islamic religious poetry come so close to myth as in these poems" (p. 201,
  folio 183).
- **Persian eulogy outweighs Arabic and shaped Turkish and Urdu.** "There are an even greater number of
  nac tiyya poems in the Persian than in the Arabic tradition. The descriptions are cer­tainly more
  colorful", and the genre "largely influ­enced the Ottoman Turkish and the Urdu tradition" (p. 207,
  folio 189); Sana'i "seems to have introduced" it into Persian (p. 63, folio 53). A scholarly
  identification she claims: "Thanks to Sacdi's use the mutiic [text-layer?: muṭāʿ], whose identity
  had caused some embarrassment to scholars, is indeed identified as the Prophet Muhammad" (p. 214,
  folio 196).
- **The prophetology of balance.** "Moses sym­bolizes the predominance of the Law, while Jesus
  represents the predomi­nance of love and otherworldliness, while Muhammad is regarded as the prophet
  who preached the healthy equilibrium between these two extremes." (p. 305, folio 287).
- **The names, the Burda, and the reference works.** The name Ahmad, "considered to be the Prophet's
  heavenly name, was elaborated by pious writers since it was connected in its assumed meaning,
  perikletos, with the parakletos of the Christian tradition; hence the importance of Sura 61/6"
  (p. 293, folio 275); "slowly the number of names grew to more than two hundred, and Horten …
  mentions a thousand names" (p. 294, folio 276). Of the Burda: "Some orientalists have doubted the
  authenticity of this story because it is not found in lbn [text-layer?: Ibn] Ishaq's sira", and it
  "was one of the first Arabic poems ever edited and translated in the West: G . J. Lette, Cannen
  [text-layer?: Carmen] panegyricum in /audem [text-layer?: laudem] Muhammedis (Leiden 1748)" (p. 293,
  folio 275). "The most important study of the development of the veneration of the Prophet is still
  Tor Andrae, Die person Mohammeds in glaube und leh re seiner gemeinde" (p. 292, folio 274); "The
  basic Arabic source of na<tiyya [text-layer?: naʿtiyya] poetry is Yusuf an-Nabhani, Al-majmaca
  an-nabhtiniyya fi'l-madti'i/1 an-nabawiyya" (p. 292, folio 274), and "The ever-growing in­terest in
  the technical aspects of nac t poetry cuhninates [text-layer?: culminates], I think, in Yusuf
  an-Nabhani's attempt to write sixteen praise poems on Muhammad in the sixteen classical Arabic
  meters" (pp. 205–206, folios 187–188). The chapter closes on a universality claim: Knappert's
  sentence on Swahili pious literature "can be applied to poetry in honor of Muhammad in every corner
  of the Muslim world" (p. 229, folio 211).
- Minor but usable: the "flag of Love" was probably associated by the medieval listener "with the flag,
  li'wti' [text-layer?: liwāʾ], of the Prophet" (p. 268, folio 250); Khaqani's poetry "deserves a
  spe­cial, extensive study" and Jami's "would deserve a special discussion" (p. 215, folio 197;
  p. 213, folio 195).

#### The scholarship: her verdicts on other readers, and on her own evidence base

- **A corrective the field lost.** Hammer-Purgstall "has clearly stated the nonpantheistic character of
  the Matlmawi"[text-layer?: Mathnawī]; "Unfortunately, this interesting study has never been
  mentioned by any later student of Rumi in Europe" (p. 254, folio 236); the same scholar's *Das
  arabische Hohelied der Liebe* "was dismissed at"[text-layer?: by] "R. A. Nicholson with the remark
  that 'the ta'iyya had the misfortune of being translated by Hammer'" (p. 243, folio 225).
- **Her endnote judgements on the Arabists, compressed.** Ritter "stresses the fact that 'poetry, even
  much later . . . has remained a-religious'" and "also highlights the lack of 'mythical apperception'
  in Arabic poetry, a characteristic which is certainly not helpful for the growth of truly
  'religious' verse" (p. 234, folio 216); von Grunebaum "rightly draws attention to the surprising
  fact that 'Arabic literary theory never has included religious elements among the recognized
  motives'" (p. 235, folio 217); of Nicholson's view that Dhu'n-Nun first gave asceticism a
  "definitely pantheistic bent", "This statement seems to go too far, even though Dhu'n-Nun certainly
  inaugurates a new period in Sufi literature" (p. 236, folio 218); "Hans Heinrich Schaeder was right
  when he wrote: 'Islamic mysticism is the attempt to reach individual salvation through attaining the
  true tauhid'" (p. 237, folio 219).
- **Her verdicts on sources a researcher would otherwise trust.** Abdul Ghani's volume is cited
  "unfortunately with wrong trans­lations of the key concepts" (p. 305, folio 287); Rasheed's article
  "is a useful compilation of material" (p. 302, folio 284); A. S. Husain "highly praises lbn Daqiq
  al-e Id al-Manfaluti, probably because he belongs to the Egyptian tradition" (p. 301, folio 283);
  Nicholson's *Literary History of the Arabs* "devotes only a brief paragraph to the poem" (p. 300,
  folio 282); Cejpek's "Iranian Folk-Literature" is annotated "[mentions poetry only in passing . ]"
  (p. 317, folio 299); Furuzanfar "is the best introduction to the tra­ditions used by Rumi" (p. 304,
  folio 286); Christopher Shackle, "The Multani marsiya," "offers an excellent analysis" (p. 285,
  folio 267). On an edition she directs the reader past it: "Cf. the review by Ewald Wagner, Der Islam
  36 (1960), pp. 78--98, for additional material." (p. 324, folio 306).
- **The evidential base for the book's South Asian material is heavily her own prior work.** The run of
  entries under her own name fills pp. 336–339 (folios 318–321), the Sind/Indus/Pakistan items
  including "'Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal.'", "Islam in the Indian Subcontinent", "'The
  Marthiya in Sindhi Poetry'", "'The Martyr-mystic l;lallaj [text-layer?: Ḥallāj] i n Sindhi Folk
  Poetry.' Numen 9 , no. 3 (1962)" (p. 337, folio 319), "'Shah ' lnayat of JhOk…'", "'Sindhi
  Literature'", "'Sindhi Translations and Commentaries of the Qur'an.' Oriens 16 (1963)" (p. 338,
  folio 320), and "'The Veneration of the Prophet Muhammad, as Reflected in Sin-/dhi poetry'" (p. 339,
  folio 321); elsewhere she cites her own *Triumphal Sun*, *Mystical Dimensions of Islam*, *Pain and
  Grace*, "'Classical Urdu Literature'" and "'Turk and Hindu, a Poetical Image and Its Application to
