  Historical Fact'" as the authority for particular readings (pp. 241–248, folios 223–230; p. 278,
  folio 260; p. 279, folio 261).
- **The other scholars she leans on for the region, as printed:** Rizvi/Rizwi, *A History of Sufism in
  India* (p. 277, folio 259; p. 335, folio 317); T. G. Bailey, *A History of Urdu Literature* (p. 278,
  folio 260); Köprülü and Boratav for Turkish (p. 277, folio 259); Knappert and Dammann for Swahili,
  Algar for Bosnia (p. 278, folio 260); Qadi Abdul Mannan for Bengal and Maulwi ʿAbdul Haqq on "(The
  role of the noble Sufis in the development of Urdu)" (pp. 278–279, folios 260–261); Carl W. Ernst's
  Harvard thesis *Faith and Infidelity in Sufism* (p. 319, folio 301); Bruce B. Lawrence, *Notes from
  a Distant Flute* and *The Rose and the Rock* (p. 328, folio 310); Lajwanti Ramakrishna, *Panjabi
  Sufi Poets* (p. 334, folio 316); Jethmal Parsram, *Sind and its Sufis* (p. 333, folio 315); Johann
  Fück on "'Die sufische Dichtung in der Landessprache des Pan-/jab.'" (p. 320, folio 302).
- **The comparative-religion apparatus is carried by a small, repeated set of authorities** — Heiler's
  `Erscheinungsfonnen` [text-layer?: Erscheinungsformen] (pp. 260, 262, 263, 264, 270, 280), van der
  Leeuw's `Phiinomenologie` [text-layer?: Phänomenologie] and `Religionsphiinomenolo-gie`
  [text-layer?: Religionsphänomenologie] (p. 262, folio 244; p. 273, folio 255), Underhill's
  *Mysticism* (p. 270, folio 252), Buber's *Ekstatische Konfessionen* (p. 276, folio 258),
  Wheelwright's *Burning Fountain* (pp. 276–277, folios 258–259), the *Oxford Book of English Mystical
  Verse* (pp. 262 and 268, folios 244 and 250), with Ettinghausen and Welch supplying the art-historical
  parallels (p. 267, folio 249; p. 260, folio 242).
- **Her own cross-cultural comparisons are frequent and deliberate,** and a consolidator should read
  them as her argumentative moves rather than as documented influence: Juan de la Cruz's *Llama de
  amor viva* for Rumi's fire (p. 116, folio 106), Mme. Guyon's *Torrents spirituels* for Love as
  torrent (p. 119, folio 109), Suso for the dance of the soul and for suffering (p. 151, folio 133;
  p. 40, folio 30), Mechtild of Magdeburg for nakedness and for the calendar poem (p. 260, folio 242;
  p. 282, folio 264), Meister Eckhart on sinking "from nothing to nothing" (p. 75, folio 65), Goethe's
  *Selige Sehnsucht* and "the secret of Stirb und werde" for the moth and candle (p. 44, folio 34;
  p. 42, folio 32), Rudolf Otto's "fascinans and the tremendum" for `jamlil and jaltil` [text-layer?:
  jamāl and jalāl] though Otto is not named (p. 69, folio 59), "yang and yin" (p. 69, folio 59),
  ancient Egyptian and Assyrian sun hymns and the medieval *sol verus et dies verus* (p. 118,
  folio 108), the Buddha compared with the rain cloud in the *Saddharmapundarika* and "Christian
  Rorate hymns" (p. 220, folio 202), Psalms 1.3 and 92.13 for the tree (p. 280, folio 262), and St.
  Teresa's arrow beside the Hindu poets' *viraha* — "The arrow is the most commonly used metaphor for
  love in both Islamic and non-Islamic literatures" (p. 268, folio 250). One motif she traces as
  actual transmission rather than parallel: the moth-and-candle allegory reaching "Europe around 1800
  to find there its most famous expression in Goethe's poem Selige Sehnsuch t [text-layer?: Selige
  Sehnsucht]" (p. 44, folio 34).

#### What the book's own apparatus argues

- **The index's centre of gravity is the Prophet, not the saints.** Muhammad-related headwords and
  glosses run to dozens — `ljaqiqa mubammadiyya, the Archetypal Muhammad, principle of the world`
  [text-layer?: ḥaqīqa muḥammadiyya] (p. 368, folio 350); `Nar Mubammad, the light of Muham­mad`
  [text-layer?: Nūr Muḥammad] and `Na't, na'tiyya, poetry in honor of the Prophet` (p. 372,
  folio 354); `Seal of the Prophets, of Prophetship` (p. 375, folio 357); plus a run of "surname of
  the Prophet" entries (pp. 372–377, folios 354–359). By contrast **`saint` is not a headword at
  all**, appearing only inside two glosses (`curs`, p. 377, folio 359; `Munaqib6`, p. 372, folio 354)
  and one sub-entry (`Lion … saint as`, p. 370, folio 352).
- **The book organises its material by image rather than by person or place.** A distributed
  `Love as …` taxonomy runs right through the index — `Baker`, `Bleacher`, `Carpenter`, `Dyer`,
  `Falcon`, `Ghiizi`, `Imam`, `Kitchen`, `Ladder`, `Lightning`, `Lion`, `Magnet`, `Mi<rilj`, `Mother`,
  `Musician`, `Ocean`, `Physician`, `Rain`, `School`, `Shibna`, `Tailor`, `Torrent`, `Tree`,
  `Unicom`, `Washerman`, `Weaver`, `Wine`, `Worm` (pp. 365–377, folios not stated to 359) — and a
  parallel `Love as X` sub-entry recurs under nine prophet and philosopher headwords in the Index of
  Proper Names, from `Abraham, Ibrahim al-Khalil` (p. 349, folio not stated) to `Yusuf (Joseph)`
  (p. 364, folio 346).
- **The index admits non-Islamic comparanda as headwords**, which is itself a statement of the book's
  comparative reach: `Bhakti` (p. 366, folio 348), `Guru` and `Neti neti, negative description of the
  Divine in the Upanishads` (pp. 368 and 372, folios 350 and 354), `Tantric tradition` (p. 376,
  folio 358), `Prema, unfulfilled love` (p. 373, folio 355), `Yang and yin` (p. 377, folio 359),
  `Koan (in Buddhism)` (p. 367, folio 349), `Namu Amida Butsu` (p. 372, folio 354), `Eros`, `Nomos`
  (p. 367, folio 349), and `Stirb und werde, "Die and become," Goethe's transformation of the Sufi
  saying "Die before ye die"` (p. 375, folio 357). The `India` headword is the one entry large enough
  to need the book's `(Continued )` catchline, its sub-entries running `Muslim`, `Northwest`, `South`,
  `North`, `Indian`, `Sufis`, `style`, `sword`, `languages`, `tales`, `words` (p. 355, folio 337) and
  `months`, `l ndo-Muslim` [text-layer?: `Indo-Muslim`], `reformers`, `lndo-Pakistan` [text-layer?:
  `Indo-Pakistan`], `lndo-Persian` [text-layer?: `Indo-Persian`] (p. 356, folio 338).
- **The book disagrees with itself in miniature over the three-stage path.** `Sharf"a, the divine law`
  is `sym­bolized in the prune` (p. 375, folio 357), `Tariqa` is `compared to grapes` (p. 376,
  folio 358) and `ljaqrqa, "Reality," last goal on the path` is `comparable to walnut` (p. 368,
  folio 350); the three glosses sit on three different pages and the index does not reconcile them.
- **The citation apparatus keeps three classes of tradition apart.** The `Index of Selected Quotations`
  is classified, not alphabetical, under `THE KORAN` (p. 345, folio not stated), `THE PROPHETIC
  TRADITIONS` and `THE fIADITH QUDSI` [text-layer?: `THE ḤADĪTH QUDSĪ`] (p. 346, folio 328), and
  `THE IjADITH AN-NAWAFIL` [text-layer?: `THE ḤADĪTH AN-NAWĀFIL`] (p. 347, folio 329) — prophetic
  hadith, *ḥadīth qudsī* and *ḥadīth an-nawāfil* as three separate citation classes. Within the hadith
  sections the order is alphabetical by the opening words of Schimmel's English translation, making it
  a finding aid by wording (pp. 346–347, folios 328–329); the `Index of Proper Names` doubles as a
  title index by cross-reference, e.g. `Risalo, see ' Abdul Latif` (p. 363, folio 345).
- **pp. 379–382 are not Schimmel's book.** After the blank p. 378 the file carries four pages of
  Sindhi-language matter headed and footed on each page with the bilingual line `The Reading
  Generation` beside a Sindhi phrase (pp. 379, 380, 381, 382, folio not stated); its only Latin-script
  words are `e-books`, `1960`, `70` (p. 379), `Exclusive Club`, `digitize`, `non- commercial`,
  `distribution` (p. 380), and `policies`, `unavoidable necessity` (p. 382). Nothing on these pages
  may be cited as Schimmel.

## Figures and sites not in the archive

*Ordered for use: first the entries that could plausibly become archive rows — a tomb, dargah, ʿurs,
pilgrimage or standing building is named, or the figure belongs to the Subcontinent — then the
book's wider Islamic-world cast, compressed into regional runs. Page references are reproduced
exactly as the chunk notes give them; transcriber flags are kept on their words.*

### A. Candidates with a tomb, shrine, dargah, ʿurs or building named

- **Gesudaraz / Bandanawaz** — the strongest candidate in the book: his poem "is inscribed around the interior of the grand dome over his tomb in Gulbarga" (p. 254, folio 236; earlier citation p. 250, folio 232); qawwals "repeat his name in ecstatic songs" in "the dargah of Gulbarga" (p. 175, folio 157); a Gulbarga ʿurs is recorded (p. 301, folio 283). Called "the Chishti saint", still enjoying singing of his love at an age printed as a damaged number for which the notes give no reading (p. 77, folio 67); greeted in the formula "as-saltimu calaika ya Bandanawaz, assalamu calaik mere Gesildartiz [text-layer?: Gesūdarāz]" (p. 179, folio 161); the *Chistān-i rāz / Shikārnāma* attributed to him (p. 185, folio 167; p. 289, folio 271), his *Dīwān Anīs al-ʿushshāq* (p. 321, folio 303), and verses for him in Sultan Ibrahim II Adilshah's *Kitab-i Nauras* (p. 288, folio 270). Also named in Delhi before his return to the Deccan (p. 153, folio not stated) and quoted on the wine of *alast* (p. 88, folio 78).
- **Sharafuddin Yahya Maneri** — "the dargah of the Firdausiyya saint, Sharafud­din Maneri" in "mid-fourteenth-century Bihar" is the setting of a chapter's opening anecdote: a named dargah with a named order (p. 153, folio not stated); his *Mukh al-maʿānī* and *The Hundred Letters* cited (p. 277, folio 259; p. 329, folio 311). Name printed two ways in one note.
- **Burhanuddin Muhaqqiq** — "Burhanuddin later settled in Kaiseri where his modest tomb beneath the majestic Mt. Erjiyas is still visited by the pious": the only tomb in the book with continuing visitation explicitly recorded (p. 94, folio 84); Rumi's first master, spiritual predecessor of Salahuddin Zarkub (p. 100, folio 90).
- **Shams-i Tabriz / Shamsuddin of Tabriz** — carries a Subcontinental martyrdom tradition, "flayed alive in Multan per the Indian tradition" (p. 175, folio 157), and a burial: "was called out one night in early December 1248, stabbed to death, and hastily buried close to the house" in Konya (p. 96, folio 86). The chunk notes claim no archive Shams row; a Multan attachment would need checking.
- **ʿAbdul Qadir Gilani** — "Among the founder figures, c Abdul Qadir Gilani is certainly the most popular saint, and he was extolled everywhere from West Africa and Turkey to India and I ndonesia [text-layer?: Indonesia]" (p. 179, folio 161); the saying "My foot is on the neck of every saint" ascribed to him, with Pashto eulogies for him catalogued (p. 287, folio 269); indexed for *baraka*, "blessing power … of Gilani", with no place, date or order given (p. 366, folio 348). Distinguish from "the Algerian freedom fighter and mystic c Abdul Qadir (d. 1882)" (p. 291, folio 273).
- **Ahmad al-Badawi / "Ahmad il-Bedawi"** — founder of an Egyptian fraternity whose members claim "to be the qutb or ghauth, the highest member of the mystical hierarchy" (p. 47, folio 37); the subject of a modern Egyptian song "auf den iigyptischen [text-layer?: ägyptischen] Nationalheiligen" with "a colorful account of the saint's miracles" (p. 287, folio 269). **Ibrahim ad-Dasuqi** stands beside him as a fraternity founder (p. 47, folio 37; a poem of his, p. 242, folio 224).
- **Hajji Bektash, Hajji Bayram, Achi Evran** — living cult figures of Turkey: "In Turkey, one finds poems addressing Hajji Bektash and the patron saint of Ankara, Hajji Bayram", and Achi Evran is "den Heiligen von Kirschehir und Patron der tiirkischen Ziinfte [text-layer?: türkischen Zünfte]" (p. 287, folio 269).
- **Abu Saʿid-i Abu'l-Khair** [text-layer?: Abū Saʿīd-i Abu'l-Khair] — "the saint of Maihana (d. 1049)", named as saint of a named place, in an attribution the author rejects (p. 61, folio 51).
- **Uwais al-Qarani** — "a legendary contemporary of the Prophet, who lived in Yemen and, becoming a pious Muslim without ever having met the Prophet, is the prototype of the uwaisi mystic, who enters the Path without formal initiation through a living master" (pp. 249–250, folios 231–232).
- **Sultan ʿAlaʾuddin Kaikobad** — a dated building still standing: "the mosque which he erected in 1220 close to the fortress is still in use" at Konya (p. 94, folio 84).
- **Arbela** — site of "the maulid celebration in Arbela in 1207" and of "The first recorded maulid celebrations" (p. 297, folio 279): a dated event at a named place.
- **Sidi Heddi and al-Hajj Omar Seydu** — saints of popular Maghribi and West African verse: Sidi Heddi promising "When you roam about, / I'll fly over your heads . . ." to his perambulatory disciples, and "the Tijani saint, al-Hajj Omar Seydu (d. 1864)" of the Fouta-Djalon (p. 287, folio 269).
- **Nesimi** — "the Hurufi poet" "whose religious claims … led to his execution in Aleppo in 1417" in conscious imitation of Hallaj (p. 247, folio 229): a martyrdom at a named place.

### B. Candidates in the Subcontinent (no site given in the book)

- **Pir Murad of Thatta** — "a saint honoured in Balochi verse alongside Lal Shahbaz" (p. 287, folio 269), indexed as `Pir Murad of Thatta (d. 1488)` (p. 360, folio 342). The chunk notes observe that no archive row is in Thatta.
- **the Pir Pagaro** — "in more recent times, verses devoted to the Pir Pagaro" in Sind; no shrine, place or personal name given (p. 287, folio 269), indexed as `Pir Pagaro` (p. 360, folio 342).
- **Pir Imam Shah** — index headword only, in the Sind grouping (p. 360, folio 342).
- **Bedil Rohriwaro** — named once in the list of alif saints, nothing else said of him, with the nisba (Rohri) queried in the chunk's Doubts (p. 157, folio 139); named again among Sindhi poets (p. 306, folio 288).
- **ʿAinuddin Ganj ul-ʿilm** — "The first known Sufi treatises in Hindwi were composed by c Ainuddin Ganj ul-c ilm (d. 1396 in Gulbarga) [text-layer?: ʿAynuddīn Ganj ul-ʿilm]" (p. 277, folio 259, running onto p. 278): a dated death at a shrine town.
- **ʿUmar of Omar-kot** — "the powerful c omar [text-layer?: ʿUmar] of Omar-kot" who captured Marui; the chunk flags a possible archive correspondence but does not claim it (p. 172, folio 154).
- **Bakhtiar Kaki** — "the Chishti leader of Delhi, who died in 1235 after listening for days to a verse by Ahmad-i Jam Zandapil" (p. 238, folio 220): a dated death in a *samāʿ* setting.
- **Nizamuddin Auliya** — the Chishti master who initiated Amir Khusrau into the order (p. 214, folio 196); Khusrau's eulogies for him (p. 287, folio 269); "the Pfirbi [text-layer?: pūrbī], 'eastern' language … in which God according to legend addressed Nizamuddin Auliya" (p. 288, folio 270); a Persian *naʿt* "ascribed to Nizamuddin Auliya" (p. 304, folio 286).
- **Amir Khusrau** — with Hasan Dihlawi the creator "In the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries" of "a highly refined lyrical poetry" (p. 65, folio 55); "the prolific Amir Khusrau (d. 1325)" initiated into the Chishtiyya (pp. 214–215, folios 196–197); at qawwali "almost always a ghazal is sung that is usually attributed to Amir Khusrau" (p. 189, folio not stated); quoted p. 70 (folio 60).
- **Jamaluddin Hanswi** — "the thirteenth century Chishti saint", quoted by Shah ʿAbdul Latif (p. 173, folio 155).
- **Masʿud Bakk** — "the North Indian Chishti mystic Mascud Bakk [text-layer?: Masʿūd Bakk] in the late fourteenth century", on "the growth of the Divine plant in man's heart" (p. 160, folio 142).
- **Ziaʾuddin Nakhshabi** — "the Chishti Sufi" who "reworked" the *Ṭūṭīnāma* "in North India in 1330" (p. 253, folio 235).
- **Khub Muhammad Chishti** — Gujari/Urdu author, cited via "M. J. Dar, 'Gujarat's Contribution to Gujari and Urdu,' p. 30" (p. 279, folio 261).
- **Shams ul-ʿushshaq Miranji** — "The first of the Sufi writers of Bijapur, Shams ul-' ushshaq Miranji (d. 1496)" (p. 283, folio 265), "credited with some of the earliest works in Dakhni Urdu" (p. 156, folio 138).
- **Qadi Mahmud of Gujarat** — "its most famous author was Qadi Mahmud (d. 1534)", "also credited with a mystical Jove [text-layer?: love] song put in a girl's mouth" (p. 283, folio 265); indexed `Qagi Mal;lmud (d. 1534)` [text-layer?: `Qāḍī Maḥmūd`] (p. 360, folio 342).
- **Qadi Qadan** — "Qadi Qadan (d. 1551) in Sind", whose verses the book calls "the first known Sindhi poems" (p. 158, folio 140; p. 161, folio 143); early veiled allusions to the folk tales in his verse "in the first half of the sixteenth century" (p. 171, folio 153); subject of a Harvard seminar (pp. 9–10); his *kalām* cited under several spellings (pp. 279–280, folios 261–262; pp. 281, 284, 286, 287, 292, folios 263, 266, 268, 269, 274; p. 333, folio 315; p. 360, folio 342). No place or shrine is given for him.
- **Makhdum Muhammad Hashim** — author of "the Sindhi classic Qiit al-ciishiqfn" [text-layer?: Qūt al-ʿāshiqīn], "in the early eighteenth century" (p. 223, folio 205); indexed `Makhdum MuJ:iammad Hashim (d. 176o)` [text-layer?: `Makhdūm Muḥammad Hāshim`] (p. 358, folio 340).
- **Mir ʿAli Shir Qaniʿ** — "An eighteenth-century Persian poet of Sind" on the importance of a proper *naʿt* (p. 207, folio 189); the one writer in that chunk attached to a place inside Pakistan.
- **Shah Waliullah of Delhi** — "the great theologian of Delhi", writing "in 1740" (p. 305, folio 287); his warning that Sufi books are "an elixir for the elite, but dangerous poison for the uninitiated masses" (p. 180, folio 162); on the Splitting of the Moon (p. 196, folio 178); works cited pp. 288, 296, 300–301, 326, 343 (folios 270, 278, 282–283, 308, 325); indexed `Waliullah, Shah (d . 1762)` (p. 364, folio 346). Two spellings in the notes.
- **Khwaja Mir Dard** — "Mir Oard [text-layer?: Mir Dard]", the only Urdu poet who "composed truly mystical verse", "in eighteenth cenhtry [text-layer?: century] Delhi" (p. 65, folio 55); "Khwaja Mir Dard in Delhi (d. 1785)" (p. 234, folio 216); quoting a last prayer of his life (p. 296, folio 278); cited pp. 243, 318, 337 (folios 225, 300, 319).
- **Nasir Muhammad ʿAndalib** — founder of the *ṭarīqa muḥammadiyya* "in Delhi in the early eighteenth century", who "had-or claimed to have-reached fana' fi'r-rasal [text-layer?: fanāʾ fi'r-rasūl]" (p. 221, folio 203); the order "founded about 1734 … in Delhi, politically important in the beginning of the 19th century in India" (p. 376, folio 358).
- **Sarmad** — "Dara Shikoh's friend, whose daring mystical quatrains, combined with the fact that he, a convert from Judaism to Islam, walked around stark naked, aroused the wrath of Aurangzeb's judges" (p. 175, folio 157); one of the Sufis who took up Hallaj's rehabilitation of Satan, "Sarmad in Delhi" (p. 43, folio 33); cited pp. 285, 323 (folios 267, 305).
- **Ahmad Sirhindi** — "Ahmad Sir-hindi's mystical theories about the two m's of Muhammad's name", which "form the basis of his revivalist theol­ogy" (pp. 211–212, folios 193–194); Friedmann's study cited (p. 320, folio 302).
- **Pir-i Roshan** — "the Afghan religious leader at Akbar's time": "God speaks in every language, be it Arabic, Persian, Hindi, or Afghani" (p. 156, folio 138); named as a type of the vernacular mystic "in the Afghan-Indian border zone" (p. 18, folio 8); wrote his *Khair ul-bayān* "in rhymed prose in the four languages mentioned" (p. 278, folio 260).
- **Sarfaraz Khan Kalhora** — "one of the last members of the Kalhora dynasty, Sarfaraz Khan, who while imprisoned put in simple words his grief and fear before the 'good Lord,' bhalii jiim [text-layer?: bhalā jām]" (p. 222, folio 204); indexed with the `Kalhora dynasty (1737-1786)` (pp. 361 and 357, folios 343 and 339).
- **Sindhi and Panjabi poets of the index and bibliography** — one line each in the source, all matching no archive row: `c Ali l;laidar (Panjabi, 19th cent.)` and his *Sīharfī* (p. 305, folio 287; p. 350, folio 332); `l;fammal Faqir Laghiiri (d . 1887)` [text-layer?: `Ḥammal Faqīr Laghārī`] (p. 308, folio 290; p. 354, folio 336); `Ghani Tattawi (d. 1723)` / `Ghani Thattawi` (p. 306, folio 288; p. 353, folio 335); "Hajji Faqir Muhammad, son of Fath Khan Macchi" (p. 307, folio 289; p. 354, folio 336); `Madan Faqir (18th cent.)` (p. 357, folio 339); `Talib (Panjabi poet)` and `Talib al-Maula` (p. 363, folio 345); "Maulawi Ghulam Rasul", quoted on the morning breeze at the Prophet's resting place (p. 301, folio 283; p. 353, folio 335); "Jaman Charan ( d . 1738)" (p. 287, folio 269); `MuJ:iammad ad-Din, l:fajji` and `Mirza Shakur Beg (Madina ka ?adaqa)` [text-layer?: `Madīna ka ṣadaqa`] (p. 358, folio 340); and the anonymous "Matlrnawiyiit-i Hir Ranjhii" [text-layer?: Hīr Ranjhā], for which no author is named (p. 324, folio 306).
- **Sindhi Hindu authors of Muslim devotional and biographical work** — "Sufi Bhai Asuram (d. 1941) in Sindhi", "Lalchand Jagtiani", "Hotchand M . Gurbaxani", "Lilaram Watanmal" (pp. 307–308, folios 289–290); the first two also indexed (p. 350, folio 332; p. 356, folio 338). Parked, not mapped, by the chunk notes.
- **Mulla / Maulana Da'ud** — "Maulana Da'ud's Lor Chanda, written for Feroz Shah Tughluq's vizier around 1370", "the first romantic tale with religious flavor in Hindwi", "composed in this very city of Delhi" (p. 154, folio 136); named as a type of the vernacular mystic (p. 18, folio 8); with "Sadhan" in a cited study (p. 278, folio 260).
- **Malik Muhammad Jaʾisi** — "the Chishti writer Malik Muhammad Ja'isi's Awadhi poem Padmiivati [text-layer?: Padmāvatī], which belongs to the early Moghul period" (p. 154, folio 136); "probably begun in 1569" (p. 278, folio 260); also p. 176, folio 158.
- **The Bijapuri Sufis and Sultan Ibrahim II Adilshah** — among the Bijapuri Sufis "of the late sixteenth and seventeenth centuries" the spinning songs "were popular, as Richard M. Ea­ton has shown", with a *chakki* verse given (p. 163, folio 145; p. 164, folio 146); the Sultan's *Kitab-i Nauras* honours Gesudaraz (p. 288, folio 270).
- **Bengali Muslim writers** — "Muhammad Saghir, in his Bengali version of a Persian epic, Yusuf Jal ikhti [text-layer?: Yūsuf Zalīkhā]" (p. 156, folio 138); "a medieval Bengali mystic, Shaikh Chand" and his *Talibname* (p. 195, folio 177; p. 196, folio 178); "the Bengali Micriijniima [text-layer?: Miʿrājnāma] of Sayyid Sultan in the sixteenth century" (p. 201, folio 183; p. 222, folio 204); "the Bengali poet Madan" (p. 178, folio 160); "the so-called 'Riddles of Goraknath' … still popular among the Hindu villagers" in Bengal (p. 183, folio 165 — the notes flag two archive rows for Goraknath).
- **Muhsin Kakorawi** — "the famous Urdu poet of the late nineteenth century" (p. 219, folio 201).
- **Jamali Kanboh and Gada'i** — "an Indian poet of the early sixteenth century, Jamali Kanboh" (p. 202, folio 184); "Gada'i, the son of the noted Persian poet Jamali Kanboh (d. 1535), who also wrote religious songs in Hindi", with "c Abdul Wahid Bilgrami" among Sufis who "used to sing ecstatic songs in Hindi" (p. 278, folio 260).
- **Azad Bilgrami** — of "the northern Deccan", "acclaimed as l:fassdn al-Hind [text-layer?: Ḥassān al-Hind] due to his glowing Arabic encomia" (p. 194, folio 176; p. 57, folio 47).
- **Muslim rulers of the Deccan and Delhi named as poets** — "The greatest ruler of Golconda, Muhammad-Quli Qutbshah … the last Nizam of Hyderabad and the last Moghul emperor of Delhi, Bahadur Shah Zafar" (p. 219, folio 201).

*Caution, not a candidate:* "Makhdum / prophet Zakariya" at p. 285, folio 267 is the prophet of the *Tales of the Prophets*, and the chunk notes state explicitly that this is **not** the Multan saint.

### C. Places in the Subcontinent named but carrying no archive row

- Sind as a region (pp. 218, 222, 223, folios 200, 204, 205; pp. 333, 336, 338, 340; p. 362, folio 344), the Punjab / Panjabi (pp. 222, 226, folios 204, 208; p. 360, folio 342), Pakistan and "its north western part (now Pakistan)" (p. 65, folio 55; p. 360, folio 342), Thatta (p. 363, folio 345), Rohri and Jaisalmer (p. 224, folio 206; p. 356, folio 338), Hyderabad/Sind as a publishing centre (p. 326, folio 308; p. 343, folio 325) as against "Hyderabad AP" (p. 355, folio 337), Lyallpur (p. 320, folio 302).
- Delhi (pp. 43, 154, 219, 221, 234, 238, folios 33, 136, 201, 203, 216, 220; pp. 318, 326, 327, 331, 333, 334, 335, 337), Gulbarga (p. 254, folio 236; p. 278, folio 260; p. 353, folio 335), Bijapur (p. 319, folio 301; p. 351, folio 333), Burhanpur — "The Sindhi mystics who migrated to Burhanpur at the beginning of the sixteenth century" (p. 168, folio 150; p. 351, folio 333), Golconda and Hyderabad, Benares and Mathura (p. 219, folio 201; p. 358, folio 340), Bihar and Patna (p. 351, folio 333; p. 277, folio 259; p. 360, folio 342), Bengal / East Bengal / Dacca / Mymensingh (p. 222, folio 204; p. 329, folio 311; p. 340, folio 322; p. 359, folio 341), Kashmir (p. 247, folio 229), Gujarat (p. 318, folio 300), the Deccan, the Carnatic and Madras (p. 57, folio 47; p. 327, folio 309), South India and Tamil (pp. 218, 222, folios 200, 204), the Andamans (p. 350, folio 332).

### D. The wider Islamic world — context, not candidates

- **Early Arabic and Baghdad, 7th–10th c.** — Kaʿb ibn Zuhair and his *Bānat Suʿād*, and Hassan ibn Thabit, the Prophet's two earliest panegyrists (p. 24, folio 14; pp. 191–194, folios 173–176); Qaṭarī ibn al-Fujāʾa (d. 697), al-Kumait ibn Zaid (d. 743), Kuthayyir ʿUzza (d. 723), Di'bil, Sabiq al-Barbari (p. 24, folio 14; p. 235, folio 217); Abū'l-ʿAtāhiya on *zuhdiyyāt* (pp. 24–25, folios 14–15); ash-Shāfiʿī and Jaʿfar aṣ-Ṣādiq (p. 25, folio 15); Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya (d. 801) and Rābiʿa ash-Shāmiyya, the earliest mystical verses (pp. 25, 27, 28, folios 15, 17, 18; p. 236, folio 218); Dhu'n-Nun (d. 859) (pp. 29, 34–35, folios 19, 24–25; p. 185, folio 167); Sari as-Saqati (d.c.867) (pp. 30–31, 33, folios 20, 21, 23); Yaḥyā ibn Muʿādh, Abu Turab an-Nakhshabi, Ibn Mubarak, Ruwaim (d. 915), Ibn ʿAṭāʾ, Abu'l-Hasan an-Nuri, Ibn al-Khawass, Sumnun the Lover (pp. 26, 34, 37, 39, folios 16, 24, 27, 29; pp. 236–239, folios 218–221); Junaid (d. 910), "the leader of the Baghdadian school around 900", and his teacher Kharraz (pp. 30, 32, 39, folios 20, 22, 29; p. 238, folio 220); Sahl at-Tustari (p. 40, folio 30; p. 195, folio 177); Shibli, who threw the rose at the gallows (pp. 37–38, 45, 54, folios 27, 28, 35, 44; p. 195, folio 177; p. 241, folio 223; p. 374, folio 356); Niffarī and his *Mawāqif* (pp. 44–45, folios 34–35); Bishr al-Hafi, Ibn ar-Rūmī (d. c. 899), Tahir al-Maqdisi (p. 236, folio 218; p. 239, folio 221); Mutanabbi and Maʿarrī as the last masters of profane Arabic verse (p. 46, folio 36; p. 97, folio 87).
- **Hallaj** — by far the book's most-cited figure and, though not an archive candidate, the reference point for much of it: executed "in Baghdad on March 26, 922" (p. 61, folio 51), "a disciple of Sahl at-Tustari and a contemporary of Junaid" (p. 40, folio 30), "usually called by his father's name Man11ur [text-layer?: Manṣūr]" (p. 175, folio 157), "whose name means 'cotton carder'" (p. 117, folio 107); *anā'l-ḥaqq* printed "anii'l-l]aqq" and "ana'l-haqq" [text-layer?: anā'l-ḥaqq] (pp. 40, 44, folios 30, 34; p. 365, folio not stated); "uqtuliinf ya thiqatf [text-layer?: uqtulūnī yā thiqātī]" (p. 42, folio 32; p. 184, folio 166; p. 377, folio 359); *Kitāb aṭ-Ṭawāsīn* (p. 23, folio 13; p. 195, folio 177); "the country which he traversed shortly after 900 to call people to God" is Sind (p. 175, folio 157).
- **Egypt, Syria, the Maghrib and al-Andalus** — Ibn ʿArabī (d. 1240), "ash-shaikh al-akbar", with the *Tarjumān al-ashwāq*, *Futūḥāt al-makkiyya* and *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* (pp. 18, 45, 47–49, folios 8, 35, 37–39; p. 242, folio 224); Ibn al-Fāriḍ and his *Tāʾiyya* and *Khamrīyya*, with a posthumous heresy trial (pp. 15, 51–55, folios 5, 41–45; p. 88, folio 78; p. 142, folio 124; p. 243, folio 225); Ibn ʿAṭāʾAllāh's *Ḥikam*, "the last miracle of Sufism on the shores of the Nile" (pp. 46–47, folios 36–37); Busiri and the *Burda* / *Al-kawākib ad-durriyya*, with his *Hamziyya* and his master Abū'l-ʿAbbās al-Mursī (p. 47, folio 37; pp. 203–206, folios 185–188); Muhammad Abu'l-Wafa ash-Shadhili and his sons (p. 47, folio 37; pp. 242, 244, folios 224, 226); Abu Madyan of Tlemcen, printed once "Abu Madyav" [text-layer?: Abu Madyan] (p. 56, folio 46; p. 244, folio 226); ʿAfīfuddīn at-Tilimsānī (d. 1289) (p. 55, folio 45; p. 193, folio 175); ʿAbdul Ghanī an-Nābulusī (d. 1731) (pp. 51, 56, folios 41, 46); al-Jazuli and his *Dalāʾil al-khairāt* (p. 285, folio 267; p. 295, folio 277); al-Harraq (d. 1846-47) (p. 244, folio 226); ad-Darimi the Rifāʿī (p. 242, folio 224); Abu'l-Wafa Kurd, "died in Baghdad in 1107" (p. 249, folio 231); Ibn Khallikan, ʿAbdur Raʾūf al-Munāwī (d. 1621), al-Bajuri of al-Azhar, Qāḍī ʿIyāḍ of Ceuta, Lisanuddin ibn al-Khatib, Ibn Khaldun, Tirmidhi, Abū Nuʿaym, Hafiz ibn Dihya, Ibn al-Jauzi, Ibn Kathir, Ibn Daqiq al-ʿĪd (d. 1302), ath-Thaʿlabī, Ibn Nubata (d. 1368), Yusuf an-Nabhani (pp. 51–52, 55, folios 41–42, 45; pp. 193–206, folios 175–188); the Mamluk sultans Qaitbay (d. 1496) and Qansauh al-Ghuri (d. 1516) and the Merinid Abū Yaʿqūb Yūsuf as poet-patrons (p. 193, folio 175; p. 197, folio 179; p. 278, folio 260).
- **Persia and Central Asia** — Sana'i of Ghazna (d.c. 1131), "the first poet to compose a mathnawī on mystico-didactic lines" (pp. 62, 82, 85, folios 52, 72, 75; pp. 207–210, folios 189–192); ʿAṭṭār of Nishapur, *Manṭiq uṭ-ṭair* and *Ushturnāma* (pp. 54, 63, 85, folios 44, 53, 75; pp. 210–212, folios 192–194); Rumi, the book's central poet, born Balkh 1207, died 17 December 1273, with his father Baha'uddin Walad, Salahuddin Zarkub (d. 1258), Husamuddin Chelebi (d. 1284), Sultan Walad, ʿAlāʾuddīn, Sadruddin Qonawi, and the biographers Aflaki and Faridun Sipahsalar (pp. 93–104, folios 84–94; pp. 111–124, folios 101–114; pp. 254–258, folios 236–240); Ruzbihan-i Baqli and Ibn-i Khafif of Shiraz (pp. 61, 64, 86, 115, folios 51, 54, 76, 105); Ahmad Ghazzali and Abu Hamid al-Ghazzali, *Iḥyāʾ* and *Mishkāt al-anwār* (pp. 39, 64, folios 29, 54; p. 214, folio 196; p. 280, folio 262); ʿAinulquḍāt Hamadhānī, author of the book's epigraph (pp. 6, 43, 64, folios not stated, 33, 54); Bayezid Bisṭāmī and his *Subḥānī* (p. 89, folio 79; p. 375, folio 357); Najmuddin Kubra, Majduddin Baghdadi (d. 1209), Auhaduddin Kirmani (p. 239, folio 221; pp. 250–251, folios 232–233); Baba Tahir ʿUryan of Hamadan and Ahmad Yasawi's *Ḥikam* (p. 153, folio not stated; p. 277, folio 259); Jami, Hafiz, Saʿdī, Nizami of Ganja, Firdausi, Anwari, Khaqani (d. 1199), Shabistari, Fakhruddīn ʿIrāqī, Maghribi, ʿĀrifī, Hilali, Daqqaq, Rudagi, Saʿib (d. 1678), ʿUrfī, Faizi, Qudsi Mashhadi, Hatif, Avicenna, Nasir-i Khusrau, Muhsin Faid Kashani (pp. 15, 51, 59–79, folios 5, 41, 49–69; pp. 90, 93, 115, folios 80, not stated, 105; pp. 204, 213–217, folios 186, 195–199; pp. 269, 272–274, folios 251, 254–256); Shah Ismāʿīl Khaṭāʾī, founder of the Persian state, whose Turkish poems entered the Bektashi tradition (p. 156, folio 138; p. 278, folio 260).
- **Anatolia and the Turkish world** — Yunus Emre, printed also "Yunus Ernre" [text-layer?: Yunus Emre], "the Anatolian minstrel" who "sang around 1300", the most-cited vernacular poet in the notes (pp. 18, 157–160, 167–168, 187, folios 8, 139–142, 149–150, 169; p. 197, folio 179; pp. 222, 225, folios 204, 207; pp. 275, 278–280, folios 257, 260–262); Shayyad Hamza, first Turkish verses in Anatolia (p. 278, folio 260); Süleyman Chelebi of Bursa (d. 1422) and his still-popular *mevlud* (pp. 197, 199, folios 179, 181); Kaygusuz Abdal, Niyazi Misri, Pir Sultan Abdal, Ommi Kemal, Ommi Sinan, Ismail Emre, the Hurufis, ʿItrī, Zia Pasha (d. 1880), Nazim Hikmet on Qadi Badruddin of Simawna (p. 15, folio 5; p. 67, folio 57; pp. 182, 185, folios 164, 167; p. 212, folio 194; pp. 279–280, folios 261–262; p. 288, folio 270); Sultan Selim Yavuz (r. 1514-1520) and Babur as royal poets (p. 278, folio 260); Genghis Khan, whose destructions from 1220 the spiritual revival outweighed (p. 45, folio 35).
- **Elsewhere** — Swahili devotional poetry, Barzanji's *maulid*, "one al-Fayyazi" of Nigeria (pp. 193, 198–199, folios 175, 180–181; p. 229, folio 211); Shushtari "(d. 1269)" and the ʿIsawiyya of North Africa (p. 174, folio 156); Kabir, paired with Yunus Emre (p. 182, folio 164; p. 275, folio 257); Shaikh Sanʿan, ʿAṭṭār's hero (p. 175, folio 157); Krishna and Radha and the *gopis*, and Marui and ʿUmar (pp. 169–172, folios 151–154; p. 225, folio 207 — the notes flag two archive rows for Krishna); Sir Sayyid Ahmad Khan and Altaf Husain Hali (p. 226, folio 208; p. 233, folio not stated); Ghalib (pp. 81, 176, 252, 321, 334, 337, folios 71, 158, 234, 303, 316, 319); Mir Taqi Mir (p. 193, folio 175; pp. 242, 253, folios 224, 235); scriptural and romance motifs indexed without cult: Solomon and the Queen of Sheba, David, Abraham, the blind Jacob, Yusuf's shirt, Laila's dog, Israfil (pp. 366–369, 374, folios 348–351, 356; p. 150, folio 132). *Chunk 012's closing list adds Sundardas, Bishr al-Hafi, Jamaluddin as-Sarsari, ʿAbdur Rahim al-Burʿi, Fatima, Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya and Tamim ad-Dari with no page reference attached; they are recorded here without one because the notes give none.*
- **Places outside the Subcontinent, named without a shrine** — Baghdad (pp. 32, 37, 40, 46, folios 22, 27, 30, 36), Konya and its bazaars (p. 94, folio 84; pp. 278, 280), Tabriz (pp. 94–99, folios 84–89), Damascus as *dimashq-i ʿishq* (p. 121, folio 111), Cairo (p. 52, folio 42), Mecca and Medina and the Kaʿba (pp. 48–49, 52, folios 38–39, 42; p. 100, folio 90; p. 229, folio 211), Kerbela (p. 15, folio 5), Aleppo (p. 247, folio 229), Balkh, Tirmidh, Kaiseri and Mt. Erjiyas (p. 94, folio 84), Ghazna (p. 62, folio 52), Nishapur (p. 63, folio 53), Maihana (p. 61, folio 51), Herat and Ganja (p. 62, folio 52), Shiraz, Bukhara and Samarqand (p. 248, folio 230), Divrigi (p. 267), Hamadhan (p. 265, folio 247), Lebanon (p. 35, folio 25), Yemen (p. 249, folio 231), Kairouan (p. 224, folio 206), Bosnia (p. 278, folio 260), Mount Qaf and Hindustan as poetic topoi (pp. 85, 87, folios 75, 77; p. 113, folio 103).
- **Modern scholars, translators and critics named in the notes** — Massignon (p. 12, folio 2; p. 241, folio 223; p. 251, folio 233), Nicholson (pp. 242–243, folios 224–225), Nyberg and Affifi (p. 242, folio 224), Nwyia (p. 46, folio 36), Richard M. ## Citable passages

Fifteen selected from the roughly eighty candidates in the chunk notes. Page references are exactly
as the source notes give them; every `[text-layer?]` flag is kept.

- "the ecstatic mystic of the Qadiri order who is buried together with his Hindu beloved in a modest
  tomb near the Shalimar gardens in Lahore" (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153) — Madho Lal Husain.
- "flayed alive by the mollas in Multan where his shrine is still venerated" (p. 175, folio 157) —
  Shams-i Tabriz, attributed by the book to "the Indian tradition", not to itself.
- "called the first social reformer in Sindhi Islam, who was executed by the Moghul governor in
  early 1718" (p. 175, folio 157) — Shah ʿInayat of Jhok.
- "each community has hymns in praise of the mystical guide at whose shrine the qawwali gather"
  (p. 179, folio 161).
- "Mecca and Medina (and for the Sindhi and Panjabi, also Multan) are inside the heart" (p. 178,
  folio 160).
- "Multan is the resting place of the great Suhrawardi saint Baha'uddin Zakariya (d. 1267) ."
  (p. 286, folio 268).
- "we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan, who is often implored when a woman wants
  a son" (p. 287, folio 269).
- "even when the pilgrims travel to Mt. Hinglaj, which is situated west of Sind, they are said to "go
  East," i.e., to find illumination." (p. 288, folio 270).
- "In Panjabi, there are biirahmasa about Fariduddin Ganj Shakar of Pakpattan"
  [text-layer?: bārahmāsa] (p. 287, folio 269).
- "The `Kash/ al-mafJjub`[text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] of Hujwiri, who spent his last years in
  Lahore, is the first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- "The Naqshbandi mystic c Abdur Rahim Girhori [text-layer?: ʿAbdur Raḥīm Girhōrī], who was killed
  when destroying a Shiva temple (1778)" (p. 224, folio 206).
- "curs, "wedding," celebrations during a saint's death anniversary" [text-layer?: ʿurs] (p. 377,
  folio 359) — the book's own gloss.
- "Dargah, seat of the head of an order or its branches" (p. 366, folio 348) — the book's own gloss,
  institutional rather than architectural.
- "Qawwiili, religious folk songs, group of singers, mainly in lndo-Pakistan" [text-layer?:
  qawwālī] (p. 373, folio 355) — the gloss makes the word name both the songs and the performers.
- "Pictures of Buraq are found today on trucks and tank waggons in Pakistan" (p. 201, folio 183).

## Doubts and review points

### 1. Facts that conflict with the archive's own data

**Seven death years in the book disagree with the archive rows.** All seven are printed headwords in
the `Index of Proper Names`, i.e. a 1982 Columbia monograph disagreeing with the sheet, not OCR
noise. The archive's `figure_died` is given only for comparison.

- `Bullhe Shah (d. 1754)` (p. 351, folio 333) against the archive's 1757.
- `Sacha! Sarmast (d. 1826)`[text-layer?: `Sachal Sarmast`] (p. 361, folio 343) against 1827.
- `Fanduddrn Ganj-i Shakar (d . 1265)`[text-layer?: `Farīduddīn Ganj-i Shakar`] (p. 353, folio 335)
  against 1266.
- `Madho Lal };Iusain (d. 1593)`[text-layer?: `Madho Lāl Ḥusain`] (p. 357, folio 339) against 1599.
- `Hujwiri … (d. c. 1071)` (p. 355, folio 337) against 1072 — the book hedges with its own `c.`
- `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (13th cent.)` (p. 357, folio 339) against 1274 — a century where the archive
  has a year.
- `Warith Shah (late 18th cent .)` (p. 364, folio 346) against 1798 — likewise a century only.

The seven that **agree**, and can therefore be cited with confidence, are recorded for contrast:
`Ba ha' uddin Zakariya M ultani (d . 1267)` (p. 351, folio 333; also p. 286, folio 268);
`' lnayat of JhOk, Shah (d. 1718)` (p. 355, folio 337; also p. 175, folio 157);
`Girhon, c Abdur Ral;lim (d . 1778)` (p. 353, folio 335; also p. 224, folio 206);
`Fand, Khwaja Ghulam (d . 1901)` (p. 353, folio 335); `Sultan Bahii (d . 1691)` (p. 362, folio 344);
`Iqbal, Mul;lammad (d . 1938)` (p. 356, folio 338; also p. 226, folio 208);
`c Abdul Latif, Shah (d . 1752)` (p. 349, folio not stated).

**Two silsila statements conflict with the archive's field.**

- Madho Lal Husain is "the ecstatic mystic of the Qadiri order" (p. 170, folio 152); the
  `madho-lal-hussain` row records the silsila as "Malamati".
- Sultan Bahu: "as used in the Qadiriyya order, to which Sultan Bahu belonged" (p. 161, folio 143);
  the `garh-maharaja-shorkot` row reads "Sarwari Qadiri". Note also that the `Index of Proper Names`
  has **no `Qadiriyya` headword at all** (pp. 349–364); the order's eponym is indexed only as a
  person, `c Abdul Qadir al-Gilani (d. 1 166)` (p. 349, folio not stated).

**Name forms differ from the archive's and are kept as printed, never normalised.** "Warith Shah"
(p. 171, folio 153; p. 364, folio 346) against Waris Shah; "Bullhe Shah" (p. 158, folio 140; p. 279,
folio 261; p. 333, folio 315) against "Bulleh Shah (Abdullah Shah Qadri)"; "Sultan Bahoo" (p. 341,
folio 323) against Sultan Bahu; "Madho Lal Husain" against Madho Lal Hussain; "Shah c Abdul Karim of
Bulrri" [text-layer?: Bulri's] (p. 171, folio 153) against Shah Abdul Karim Bulri; "Sacha! Sarmast"
against "Sachal Sarmast (Abdul Wahab Faruqi)"; "Shaikh Farid Ganj-i Shakar" (p. 332, folio 314)
against Baba Fariduddin Mas'ud Ganjshakar.

**One near-identity refused on the archive's own data.** `Asuram, $flfi Bhai (d. 1941)`
[text-layer?: `Asuram, Ṣūfī Bhai`] (p. 350, folio 332) is **not** mapped to
`sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil` (Sant Baba Asudaram (Sakhi Baba), died 1960): different
honorific, a missing syllable, and a death year nineteen years earlier. If Rauf reads them as one
figure, that row is waiting.

### 2. Internal contradictions in the book

- **Sorley's first name is printed two ways, with two forms of the title.** "Henry T. Sorley, Shah
  Abdul Latif of Bhit, His Poetry, Life and Times" (p. 283, folio 265) against "Sorley, Herbert T.
  Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit. London: Oxford University Press, t940 [text-layer?: 1940]; repr. 1966."
  (p. 340, folio 322). The bibliography year is damaged as well.
- **Shah Abdul Latif's death year appears in the index and nowhere in the body.** The index prints
  `c Abdul Latif, Shah (d . 1752)` (p. 349, folio not stated), but every chunk of body text and
  notes that names him records the death year as **(not stated)** — he is only "eighteenth-century"
  (p. 158, folio 140), "of Bhit in the eighteenth century" (p. 171, folio 153), and unfixed in the
  Prophet chapter and the chapter-5 notes (p. 223, folio 205; p. 308, folio 290). The 1752 rests on
  the index alone.
- **The Mathnawi chronology does not add up.** The first book is placed "in this period (late 1256)"
  and "After the completion of the first book … there was a break of some four years" (p. 102,
  folio 92), yet "the dictation was taken up again in 1262" (p. 103, folio 93).
- **Ghauth and Qutb are each glossed as the single highest rank.** `Ghauth, "Help," title of the
  highest member of the mystical hierarchy` (p. 368, folio 350) against `Qufb, "Pole, axis," the
  highest member of the mystical hierarchy` [text-layer?: quṭb] (p. 373, folio 355); the index does
  not reconcile them.
- **Gibb's initials print three ways**: "E. H. W. Gibb's introduction to his useful History of
  Ottoman Poetry" (p. 65, folio 55), "E. J. W. Gibb, History of Ottoman Poetry, l" (p. 289,
  folio 271), "E. W. J. Gibb, History of Ottoman Poetry, III" (p. 295, folio 277).
- **One Egyptian scholar, four forms**: "e Ali Safi Husain [text-layer?: ʿAlī Ṣafī Ḥusain]" (p. 46,
  folio 36) and "Dr. A. S. Husain" (p. 47, folio 37) in the body; "A. S . Husain, Al-adab aNufi"
  (p. 242, folio 224) and "S . A. Husain, Al-adab a�-�ufi" (p. 244, folio 226) in the notes, with the
  initials transposed.
- **Notes against bibliography, same work, different wording.** Tor Andrae: "Die person Mohammeds"
  in the notes (p. 292, folio 274, as "Die person Mohammeds in glaube und leh re seiner gemeinde")
  against "Die person Muhammeds in lehre und glauben seiner gemeinde" in the bibliography (p. 312,
  folio 294) — **Mohammeds against Muhammeds is a substantive difference in a title.** Also:
  "Literary Heritage of Bengal: The Medieval Period" (p. 307, folio 289) against "Literary H eritage
  of Bangladesh, Medieval Period ." (p. 311, folio not stated), whose author is "Qazi Abdul Mannan"
  in the note and "Abdul Mannan, Qadi" in the bibliography, and "Qadi Abdul Mannan" elsewhere
  (p. 283, folio 265; p. 291, folio 273) against "Qazi Abdul Mannan" (p. 296, folio 278); Bannerth
  "Ernst" (p. 298, folio 280; p. 314, folio 296) against "Ernest" (p. 307, folio 289); "Nazeer
  al-Azma" (p. 299, folio 281) against "Azma, Nazeer el-" (p. 314, folio 296); Algar's "Naqshbandi
  tariqa" (p. 297, folio 279) against "t,ariqat" (p. 312, folio 294); Abdul Ghani's "Persian Language
  and Literature at the Mughal Court" (p. 305, folio 287) against "A History of Persian Language and
  Litera­ture at the Mughal Court. 3 vols." (p. 311, folio not stated); Cerulli's title differing
  word by word between p. 299 (folio 281) and p. 317 (folio 299); Bell's "Love Theories **of** Later
  Hanbalite Islam" (p. 244, folio 226) against "Lave [text-layer?: Love] Theories **in** Later
  Hanbalite Islam" (p. 251, folio 233).
- **The place of publication of the 1969 Urdu Burda is not settled by the book.** "HyderabadJAP"
  [text-layer?: Hyderabad/AP] in note 75 (p. 300, folio 282) against "Hyderabad/Deccan" in the
  bibliography (p. 317, folio 299); its translator is "Fayyazaddin Nizami" in the note and
  "Fayyaiaddin Ni+ami" in the bibliography.
- **One poet is entered twice under two spellings as consecutive headwords**: "Hafis. Gedich te aus
  dem Diwan …" and "Hafiz. Die Lieder des Hafiz …" (p. 323, folio 305). The Turkish poet is
  `Wali (d. i695)` (p. 297, folio 279) and `Wall (d. 1695)` (p. 298, folio 280).
- **Two apparatus inconsistencies in the indexes themselves.** `Annaghiin-i l;Iijiiz`[text-layer?:
  `Armaghān-i Ḥijāz`] has a see-reference to Iqbal (p. 350, folio 332) but **no matching sub-entry**
  under the Iqbal headword (p. 356, folio 338). Arberry's bibliography run is **out of alphabetical
  order in two places** (p. 313, folio 295).
- **The vernacular chapter dates its own "first" three ways within three pages.** Maneri's remark is
  "one of the first known remarks …", while "the use of local languages in Sufi circles goes back to
  the thirteenth century" and vernacular mystical poetry "began as early as the eleventh century in
  Iran" (p. 153, folio not stated), against "the late thirteenth and the early fourteenth centuries"
  for the first songs in Anatolia and Hindwi (p. 155, folio 137). Different claims about different
  things, easily conflated.
- **The book flags one chronological problem itself**: "Everywhere, Hasan is integrated into the
  story of Kerbela although he had died about twenty years before his younger brother was killed
  there in 680." (p. 285, folio 267).

### 3. Damage that destroys a fact

- **Two Koranic verse numerals are destroyed outright and no reading is offered.** "(Sura 94./i)"
  (p. 196, folio 178) and "(Sura 21/HYJ)" (p. 209, folio 191): the sura numbers 94 and 21 are
  legible, the verse numbers are not. Neither may be used. More broadly, the Sura slash prints as an
  apostrophe or vanishes — `Sura 7'157-158`, `Sura 51'21`, `Sura 21'10'7`, `Sura 50116` (p. 157,
  folio 139; p. 160, folio 142; p. 164, folio 146) — and `(Sura 8cj27)` (p. 86, folio 76) has an
  unreadable number and **must not be cited as a sura reference**.
- **The p. 220 arithmetic is impossible as printed.** Ghalib's letter-numerology reads "the two last
  letters have the numerical value of '1   = 8 and d   = 41 which makes 12" (p. 220, folio 202).
  8 + 41 is not 12, so at least one numeral on the line is damaged; **no corrected value is
  supplied**, and a human must check the page image before any part of the sentence is used. A
  related case: "an acrostic qatiida of 28 x 10 x 5 1400 lines by one al-Fayyazi" with the `=` pushed
  onto its own line (p. 199, folio 181), internally consistent but quoted as printed; and two
  glossary entries with a stray `=` on a line of its own, `Sibr baltll "licit magic" =    poetry: 14`
  (p. 375, folio 357) and `Nafth ash-shaifan, "what Satan has spit out" poetry` (p. 372, folio 354).
- **Damaged dates, quoted as printed and never repaired.** One is irrecoverable: ʿAinulqudat
  Hamadhani's execution year, "who was executed in 1 13 i" (p. 64, folio 54) — both the three-digit
  split and a final digit rendered as a letter, and it **must not enter an entry**. The rest are
  readings, not readings of the print: "When Ansari died in 1o89" (p. 62, folio 52); "Maghribi
  (d. 14o6)" (p. 73, folio 63); "he passed away in early `123 1`" (p. 94, folio 84); "Ibn Khaldun
  (d. 14o6)" (p. 193, folio 175); "in 1<)63" (p. 198, folio 180); "composed in Panjabi in 1317hh899"
  (p. 281, folio 263); "who died in t941" (p. 282, folio 264); "(Beirut 1310'1892)" (p. 292,
  folio 274); "Jamaluddin as-Sarsari (d.65611258)" (p. 295, folio 277); "(d. 45o/1058)" (p. 300,
  folio 282); "(d . 837'1434)", "(d. 662./1264)", "(d. 684'1285)" (p. 301, folio 283); "(d. 1
  11511703)" (p. 302, folio 284); "t940" for Sorley (p. 340, folio 322). Two entries print "1900" on
  one page and may both be damaged by the `0`→`o` / `8`→`B` class (p. 333, folio 315). Gesudaraz's
  age prints "even at the age of 9<J" (p. 77, folio 67) — probably 90, but the second character is
  not a digit.
- **One Contents line is wholly destroyed**, printed `��` with the locator `2�`, standing by position
  between the abbreviations and the bibliography (p. 8, folio not stated): **its label and its page
  are both unrecoverable** and recorded as `(not stated)`. On the same page `Bibliography  2 93`
  [text-layer?: 293] is a three-digit split in prose, not a folio.
- **Five bibliography entries where the repeat-author dash fails, so attribution is positional
  rather than printed.** The normal form is `--` at the line head; these carry only leading
  whitespace: "c nm ul-kitab. Delhi, 131oh/1891-92" under Dard (p. 318, folio 300); "Urdii Diwan,
  ed . l:famid c Ali Khan" under Ghalib (p. 321, folio 303); "A Persian Forerunner of Dante.
  Towyn-on-Sea, 1944" and "Studies in Islamic Mysticism . Cambridge: Cambridge University Press,
  1921; repr. 1967" under Nicholson (p. 332, folio 314); and "'Das Proomium des Matnawi-i maulawL'
  ZDMG 93 (1939)" under Ritter (p. 335, folio 317). A stray `II` also stands alone on its own line
  inside the Goldammer entry (p. 321, folio 303). **Two further entries carry no dash and no
  headword at all and are attributed to nobody**: "`Ushturniima`, ed . Mehdi Muhaqqiq . Tehran:
  University, `1339sh'1960`" inside the ʿAttar run, and "'The Tammuzi Movement and the Influence of
  T. S . Eliot on Badr Shakir al-Sayyab.' `/ADS` [text-layer?: JAOS] 88 (1968)" after the Azma entry
  (both p. 314, folio 296). In the notes, "S. Freytag, Arabische Verskunst" carries an initial that
  matches no initial of the fuller "Georg Wilhelm Freytag" on the same page (p. 244, folio 226) and
  is left unattributed.
- **One entry names only a translator.** "Nicholson, Reynold Alleyne, trans . The Secrets of the
  Self. London: MacMillan, 1920." (p. 332, folio 314) does not state whose work it translates; the
  notes attach Nicholson's translation to the *Asrar* elsewhere (p. 309, folio 291), but the entry
  itself must be confirmed before it is cited as Iqbal's.
- **Words left unread, with no reading supplied.** `mcyir` (p. 145, folio 127); `Tu-lis` (p. 43,
  folio 33); `ba`, glossed "fragrance", and `£iaumaca-i kiriim` (p. 108, folio 98; p. 122, folio 112);
  `ifa` (p. 101, folio 91); `al-N sha` (p. 293, folio 275); `bhiin` (p. 222, folio 204); `p. 2cn` and
  `cum ul-kitiib` (p. 240, folio 222; p. 239, folio 221); `caza` (p. 225, folio 207); `waliiya`
  (p. 287, folio 269); `Svar-ashtra` (p. 283, folio 265); `Zajqczkowski` and `Rasa/ viyaj` (p. 344,
  folio 326; p. 360, folio 342).

### 4. Structural traps in the source

- **Index and endnote locators are destroyed and are never usable as page references.** The
  note-reference `n` renders as `0`, `"`, `}` or `9`, and the three-digit space-split then breaks the
  page number: `2700 1 13` (p. 369, folio 351), `2900 169` / `29011 169` (p. 373, folio 355),
  `27� 14` (p. 372, folio 354), and, worst, the six mutually inconsistent renderings in one locator
  string under Shah ʿAbdul Latif, `22}" 107, 258n418, 265"67, 260077, 2700 1 13, 29on 169` (p. 349,
  folio not stated). **Plain page locators are damaged too**, not only the note references —
  `Mirror: 75, So` (p. 371, folio 353), `Fish: 1o8, 1ow` (p. 367, folio 349), `Elephant: u2`
  (p. 367, folio 349). Note numbers through the whole apparatus are split or letter-substituted
  (`5 i`, `6o.`, `Bo`, `g;.`, `CJJ .` wholly destroyed, `1oi .`, `i77.`) — pp. 233–240, 241–258,
  259–280, 281–296, 297–309. **The only safe fact from an index entry is that the book indexes the
  term, plus the wording of the gloss.**
- **PDF 379–382 is a digitiser's Sindhi appendix and not Schimmel's book at all.** The last printed
  entry of the book proper is `Zuhdiyyat, ascetic verse` (p. 377, folio 359); p. 378 is a genuine
  blank; pp. 379–382 (folio not stated) carry four pages of Sindhi matter headed and footed on each
  page with the bilingual line `The Reading Generation`. The Sindhi is not recoverable and nothing
  from these pages may be cited as the book's content.
- **p. 373's two columns are transcribed interleaved**, one line at a time with blank lines between,
  so several left-column entries (`Orders, fraternities`, `Pantheism`, `Paradoxes`, `Parwiina`,
  `Pearl`, `Pen`, `Perfect Man`, `Physician`) read as fragments; the glosses are legible but **the
  reading order of that page is unreliable** (p. 373, folio 355).
- **p. 375 prints two identically spelled headwords that must not be merged.** `Shahid, witness: 127;
  "witness of Di" vine Beauty," beloved, 67, 68, 151` and, four lines below, `Shahid, "martyr": 127`
  (p. 375, folio 357). In print these are shāhid and shahīd; with the vowel marks gone they are
  indistinguishable by spelling, and `Martyr, shahfd` (p. 371, folio 353) is a third form of the same
  word. Do not merge them, and do not split them differently.
- **The abbreviations key contains mis-keyable sigla, and `5` for `S` is the dangerous one.** The
  five flagged entries are `AR W`, `El`, `El2`, `]AOS`, `]RAS` (p. 231, folio not stated) and `5`
  (p. 232, folio 214). `5` = `(See GAL above)` is the one that would **silently corrupt a citation**,
  because `S` is used throughout the notes for Brockelmann's supplements — e.g. `GAL I 76, S I 1 1w`
  (p. 235, folio 217).
- **The plate insert is eight pages of images transcribed as `[blank page]`.** pp. 129–136 (folio not
  stated on all eight) each carry a full-page plate; **their subjects, captions, legends, credits and
  plate numbers are wholly absent** from the transcription. The Contents announces `Photo insert
  follows p. 118` (p. 8, folio not stated) and gives no count. No plate may be cited, described or
  counted beyond "eight pages of plates exist here"; a human with the page images must supply them.
- **The folio regime has two offsets and one collision.** folio = PDF − 10 for pp. 12–128, folio =
  PDF − 18 from p. 137, the plate insert being exactly the eight pages between. **PDF p. 92 is a
  genuine blank while folio 92 is PDF p. 102**, and the chapter opening at PDF p. 93 prints nothing
  while folio 93 is PDF p. 103 (p. 92, folio not stated; p. 93, folio not stated). p. 90's header
  prints the folio as `Bo`[text-layer?: 80]. Chapter- and section-opening pages and genuine blanks
  are written `folio not stated` throughout (pp. 11, 21, 59, 92, 93, 152, 153, 188, 189, 230, 231,
  233, 310, 311, 345, 348, 349, 365, 378).
- **The `Index of Proper Names` is a near-complete roster, so an absence from it is evidence.** Over
  pp. 349–364 there is **no Sikh Guru of any kind** (the column runs `Nabhani, Nabulusi, Nag Hammadi,
  Nahj ul-balagha, Nakhshabi, Naqshbanduyya`, p. 359, folio 341), **no Rahman Baba** (p. 360,
  folio 342), **no Peshawar figure at all** (p. 360, folio 342), and of the archive's eight Multan
  rows only Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā is named (p. 351, folio 333). No Uch Sharif figure, no Pir Mangho,
  Abdullah Shah Ghazi, Mian Mir, Bibi Pak Daman, Shah Jamal, Makhdoom Nooh or Shah Abdul Karim Bulri
  appears. The `Index of Technical Terms` (pp. 365–377) has **no person as a headword at all**, and
  `saint` is not a headword (p. 377, folio 359; p. 372, folio 354).
- **Titles and names print in many forms and are deliberately not unified, so a token search of the
  corpus will miss most occurrences.** *Kashf al-maḥjūb* five ways across five pages (pp. 245, 248,
  250, 251, 254; folios 227, 230, 232, 233, 236); *qaṣīda* seven ways in one chunk (pp. 217–240,
  folios 199–222) and five more in another (pp. 57, 66, 67; folios 47, 56, 57); *naʿt* in nine
  printed shapes in twenty-five pages, the worst being `na0 t` with a zero for the ʿayn (p. 207,
  folio 189); *Risālō* six ways (pp. 276–280, folios 258–262); *Dīwān* five ways (pp. 231–239, folios
  not stated–221); `Madiil1an` five ways (pp. 301–314, folios 283–296); `Ilahinama` six ways
  (pp. 299–316, folios 281–298).

### 5. Mapping doubts

- **The toponym heads are not the same size in every chunk, and one row is in dispute.** Lahore is
  31 rows in `toponym_map.txt`; chunks working from `shrine_index.tsv` used **35**, adding
  `darbar-abul-muali-qadri`, `ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed`, `tahir-bandagi-qadri` and `wasif-ali-wasif`
  (p. 254, folio 236; p. 357, folio 339). One chunk used **36** by also adding
  `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz`, whose `location_short` is truncated at "Shah Alam Market; the survey
  places the mazar near Darbar Al…" and never names a city (p. 233, folio not stated); four other
  chunks deliberately excluded it. **A human must settle that one row once.** Karachi is 9 on the map
  and **11** as used, adding `darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir` and
  `shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi` (p. 311, folio not stated; p. 318, folio 300). Multan is
  "Multan (7)" on the map against BRIEF §4's 8; **8** is used throughout, the map omitting
  `shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani` (p. 104, folio 94). Islamabad is the union of two map
  lines, **4** (p. 316, folio 298).
- **The chunks apply two opposite rules to imprint-only toponyms, and one must be chosen.** Four
  chunks attach Lahore, Karachi, Islamabad and Hyderabad/Sind heads on places of publication alone —
  "(Lahore, 1932)" (p. 233, folio not stated), "Lahore 191 1"[text-layer?: 1911] and "Lahore, 1939"
  (p. 254, folio 236), and the whole bibliography run (pp. 311–341, folios not stated–323). One chunk
  **refuses** imprints as place-level material and heads Lahore only on its own index headword
  (p. 342, folio 324; p. 357, folio 339), leaving Karachi and Hyderabad/Sind unheaded. Until the
  policy is fixed, every imprint-derived bullet is provisional; each was written so it can be dropped
  whole without losing any other fact.
- **Region-level attachment is refused throughout and the refusal is reversible.** "Sind", "the
  Panjab", "Indo-Pakistan", "the Subcontinent" and "Pakistan" take no id (p. 43, folio 33; p. 154,
  folio 136; p. 199, folio 181; p. 201, folio 183; p. 281, folio 263; p. 369, folio 351), because
  `toponym_map.txt` has no province or region line. If Rauf extends ruling (a) to provinces, most of
  the archive would map at once.
- **Thin attachments — author-only, imprint-only, title-only.** `data-darbar`: Hujwiri is only an
  author, "Hujwiri/Nicholson" five times (pp. 245–254, folios 227–236), plus the single clause "who
  spent his last years in Lahore" (p. 61, folio 51); the epithet "Data Ganj Bakhsh" appears only in
  the index headword (p. 355, folio 337) and nowhere in the body.
  `rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine` is the weakest of all: the name appears **only inside a
  book title**, "Jens Enevoldsen, Selections from Rahman Baba" (p. 278, folio 260; p. 319, folio 301),
  and the index has no Rahman Baba headword. `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori` rests in one
  chunk on a damaged word inside a title, "Kalam-i Girhoi;i" (p. 318, folio 300), and in another on
  "the Sindhi mystic Girhori" with no first name (p. 294, folio 276) — the surname match is the
  note-writer's, not the book's, though the index does print `Girhon, c Abdur Ral;lim (d . 1778)`
  (p. 353, folio 335). `shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi` rests on "Abu Hanifa and Hanuman are
  one and the same" (p. 176, folio 158) plus a bare headword (p. 354, folio 336).
  `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` rests on the Mahmud–Ayaz pairing alone, "his Turkish officer Ayaz"
  (p. 84, folio 74) — no Lahore, no tomb, no office; the identification is inferential.
  `mazar-e-iqbal`, `shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri`, `garh-maharaja-shorkot`,
  `mazar-of-bulleh-shah`, `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` and `mithankot-kot-mithan` each rest, in
  the bibliography chunk, on one printed entry with no tomb, date, place or silsila (pp. 325–341,
  folios 307–323).
- **Names that could match two rows.** "Abdul Latif": `bhit-bhit-shah` against `bari-imam` (Sayyid
  Abdul Latif Kazmi) — resolved once on "Shah" plus "in Sind" (p. 43, folio 33) and once on the date
  plus the `Risal6` sub-entry (p. 349, folio not stated), but the bare name reads either way. "Shah
  Inayat": `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` against `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri`, settled by
  the book's own "of Jhok" and 1718 (p. 175, folio 157; p. 285, folio 267; p. 338, folio 320).
  "Shams" is refused for `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`, `shamsabad` and `sial-sharif`
  wherever it is Rumi's companion (p. 118, folio 108; p. 143, folio 125; p. 365, folio not stated;
  p. 376, folio 358) — and, separately, the book **does not identify** "Shams Tabriz of Multan"
  (p. 285, folio 267; p. 326, folio 308) or the venerated Multan shrine of "Shams-i Tabriz" (p. 175,
  folio 157) with any archive figure; `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari` sits in the Multan head
  **because of the place only**, and the distinction must not be collapsed. "Omarkot" →
  `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir` on spelling alone (p. 224, folio 206; p. 359, folio 341), and was
  refused elsewhere (p. 172, folio 154). "Rohri": no archive row is *in* Rohri, both candidates name
  it as adjacent, and the chunks split between parking it (p. 224, folio 206) and heading it (p. 361,
  folio 343). "Sehwan": taken as the saint's epithet in one chunk, claiming only `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`
  (p. 287, folio 269), and as a bare toponym in another, adding `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`
  (p. 361, folio 343). "Hyderabad": the book prints "Hyderabad/Sind", "Hyderabad/Deccan", "Hyderabad
  AP" and one bare, undisambiguated "Hyderabad" (p. 301, folio 283; p. 321, folio 303; p. 355,
  folio 337) — only the first carries `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat`. "Hinglaj" is headed with
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` only; `chandragup-baba-chandragup` (Hingol National Park) is
  the other candidate and is not claimed (p. 354, folio 336). A bare "Zakariya" occurs twice and must
  not be merged with the Multan saint: the prophet in al-Kisāʾī (p. 285, folio 267) and a bare index
  headword (p. 363, folio 345), against "Baha'uddin Zakariya Multani" (p. 286, folio 268; p. 351,
  folio 333).
- **The many-row person sub-case is handled two ways across the chunks.** Goraknath (→
  `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple`, `tilla-jogian`), Krishna (→ `krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar`,
  `krishna-mandir-ravi-road`) and Shiva (→ eight rows) were **parked** as an open case in one chunk
  (p. 183, folio 165; pp. 169–172, folios 151–154) and **mapped in full** in another (p. 353,
  folio 335; p. 357, folio 339; p. 362, folio 344). The three deity heads are the weakest evidence in
  the book — bare headwords in the index of a work on Islamic mystical poetry — and a consolidator
  may reasonably judge that a deity is not "a person" in the sense of the person ruling.
- **Refusals recorded so they are not later re-read as evidence.** Ibn al-Farid is not Baba Farid or
  Khwaja Ghulam Farid (pp. 51–56, folios 41–46); "Sarmad in Delhi" is not Sachal Sarmast (p. 43,
  folio 33; p. 241, folio 223); Hafiz is not Hafiz Muhammad Jamal Multani (p. 51, folio 41); Tirmidh
  and the two Tirmidhis are not `shrine-of-pir-baba-syed-ali-tirmizi` (p. 94, folio 84; p. 363,
  folio 345); Bakhtiar Kaki is not `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak` (p. 351, folio 333); Maulana Da'ud of
  `L6r Chanda` and Auhaduddin Kirmani are not `shergarh` (p. 352, folio 334; p. 251, folio 233);
  Burhanuddin Muhaqqiq is not `langer-makhdoom` (p. 351, folio 333); `cJdgiih` the common noun is not
  `eidgah-sharif` (p. 368, folio 350); the bare headword `Guru` is not the eighteen Nanak rows
  (p. 368, folio 350); Mahmud of Ghazna is the principal figure of no row (p. 65, folio 55);
  `mausoleum-of-waris-shah` is refused for "Matlrnawiyiit-i Hir Ranjhii" (p. 324, folio 306) and for
  Usborne's "Hir Ranjha" (p. 342, folio 324), neither of which names him; "Bedil Rohriwaro" is a
  nisba and not a toponym (p. 157, folio 139; p. 351, folio 333); "the Pir Pagaro" is a title, so
  `dargah-of-pir-muhammad-rashid-roze-dhani-pir-jo-goth` is not claimed (p. 287, folio 269; p. 360,
  folio 342). **Silsila-only matches are refused throughout** — Chishti (p. 77, folio 67; p. 214,
  folio 196), Shadhili (pp. 46–47, folios 36–37), ʿAbdul Qadir al-Gilani (p. 349, folio not stated),
  Naqshbandi and Sirhindi (pp. 211–212, folios 193–194) — the one silsila statement kept being "The
  Naqshbandi mystic c Abdur Rahim Girhori" (p. 224, folio 206), and only because the person is
  already mapped on his own name.
- **Two quotations and one attribution cannot be resolved from the notes.** "L. Ramakrishna, Panjabi
  Sufi Poets, pp. 24-25, a poem which shows, according to the author, his "love for intoxicating
  things."" and "For a translation of his poems see A. Schimmel, Aus dem goldenen Becher" (both
  p. 292, folio 274) refer by "his" to a poet named in body text outside the chunk; the likely
  candidate for the first is Bullhe Shah, cited from the same volume twice earlier (p. 283, folio 265;
  p. 286, folio 268), but the book does not say so and no attribution is made. Likewise the verse
  "Wondrous is the Reality of the Friend- / One cannot call him Lord, nor creature-" is introduced by
  "The great mystical poets of Sind" in the plural and **is not assigned to Shah ʿAbdul Latif**,
  though he is named in the next sentence (p. 223, folio 205). And "these verses are inscribed on his
  sarcophagus" (p. 257, folio 239) does not say whose — Shamsuddin's or Rumi's — so the inscription
  must not be attached to either tomb.
